) is the associated adjoint bundle of the principal
L
-bundle (resp.
K
-bundle) induced from the principal
P
-bundle
G{\times}\mathfrak{m}\rightarrow\mathsf{X}
. Assume that every simple constituent of
L
is of type
\mathrm{A}
,
\mathrm{C}
,
\mathrm{D}
,
\mathrm{E}_{6}
, or
\mathrm{G}_{2}
, then the same holds for
K
. Therefore,
T_{G/P}
is a partial polarization of
\mathsf{X}
under this assumption.

Example 3.4
.

A special case of the previous example is when
G=\mathrm{SL}_{n}
and
P
is the subgroup fixing a flag
F_{\bullet}=(0=F_{0}\subset\mathcal{F}_{1}\subset\cdots\subset F_{k-1}\subset F_{k}=\mathbb{C}^{n})
, we choose the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{m}_{s}
according to a marking number
s\in\{\pm 1\}^{k}
such that

\mathfrak{m}_{s}(F_{i})\subset\begin{cases}F_{i-1},&s_{i}=+1,\\
F_{i},&s_{i}=-1,\end{cases}

then
\mathsf{X}=G{\times}^{P}\mathfrak{m}_{s}
has a partial polarization for any choice of marking
s
. These varieties show up in the work of Rimányi and Rozansky
[
117
, Section 2.3.3]
as the moduli space of certain quiver-like diagram construction.

3.1
Chambers and attracting sets

Let
\mathrm{Cochar}(\mathsf{A})
be the cocharacter lattice of
\mathsf{A}
, and we denote

\mathfrak{a}_{\mathbb{R}}:=\mathrm{Cochar}(\mathsf{A})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}\subset\mathfrak{a}=\mathrm{Lie}(\mathsf{A})
.
We define the
roots
of the pair
(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{A})
to be the set of weights
\{\alpha\}
appearing in the normal bundle to
\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}
. A
chamber
is defined to be a connected component of the complement of hyperplanes cut out by roots, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}_{\mathbb{R}}\big\backslash\bigcup_{\alpha\in\mathrm{roots}}\alpha^{\perp}=\bigsqcup_{i}\mathfrak{C}_{i}.

(3.3)

For every
\sigma\in\mathfrak{C}_{i}\cap\mathrm{Cochar}(\mathsf{A})
, we have
\mathsf{X}^{\sigma}=\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}
.

Let
\mathfrak{C}
be a chamber, then we say that a root
\alpha
is attracting (resp. repelling) if
\alpha
is positive (resp. negative) on
\mathfrak{C}
. For a connected component
\mathcal{F}\subset\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}
, we define the attracting part
N^{+}_{\mathsf{X}/\mathcal{F}}
of normal bundle to be the span of attracting root space in
N_{\mathsf{X}/\mathcal{F}}
; similarly, the repelling part
N^{-}_{\mathsf{X}/\mathcal{F}}
is defined to be the span of repelling root space in
N_{\mathsf{X}/\mathcal{F}}
. Then we define attracting submanifold
\mathsf{Attr}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\mathcal{F})
to be the subset

\displaystyle\{x\in\mathsf{X}\mid\lim_{t\rightarrow 0}\sigma(t)\cdot x\in\mathcal{F}\},

(3.4)

for some
\sigma\in\mathfrak{C}\cap\mathrm{Cochar}(\mathsf{A})
.
\mathsf{Attr}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\mathcal{F})
does not depend on the choice of
\sigma
, in fact, it is the exponential of attracting part of the normal bundle
N^{+}_{\mathsf{X}/\mathcal{F}}
. Define the union
{\mathsf{Attr}_{\mathfrak{C}}:=\coprod_{\mathcal{F}}\mathsf{Attr}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\mathcal{F})}
, then
\mathsf{Attr}_{\mathfrak{C}}
admits an immersion

\mathsf{Attr}_{\mathfrak{C}}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}
,
x\mapstochar\rightarrow(x,\lim_{t\rightarrow 0}\sigma(t)\cdot x)
.
In general,
\mathsf{Attr}_{\mathfrak{C}}
is not closed in
\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}
, since
\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\sigma(t)\cdot x
, if exists, is not in
\mathsf{Attr}_{\mathfrak{C}}
. We define
\mathsf{Attr}^{f}_{\mathfrak{C}}
to be the set of pairs
(x,y)
that belongs to a chain of closures of attracting
\mathsf{A}
-orbits.
\mathsf{Attr}^{f}_{\mathfrak{C}}
is closed in
{\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}}
.

A partial order
\preceq
on the set of connected components of
\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}
is generated by letting

\mathcal{F}_{j}\subset\overline{\mathsf{Attr}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\mathcal{F}_{i})}\ \Rightarrow\ \mathcal{F}_{j}\preceq\mathcal{F}_{i}.

We define closed subvarieties
\mathsf{Attr}^{<}_{\mathfrak{C}}\subset\mathsf{Attr}^{\leq}_{\mathfrak{C}}\subset\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}
by

\mathsf{Attr}^{<}_{\mathfrak{C}}:=\bigcup_{\mathcal{F}_{j}\prec\mathcal{F}_{i}}\mathsf{Attr}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\mathcal{F}_{j})\times\mathcal{F}_{i},\qquad\mathsf{Attr}^{\leq}_{\mathfrak{C}}:=\bigcup_{\mathcal{F}_{j}\preceq\mathcal{F}_{i}}\mathsf{Attr}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\mathcal{F}_{j})\times\mathcal{F}_{i}.

Note that
\mathsf{Attr}^{f}_{\mathfrak{C}}
is a closed subvariety of
\mathsf{Attr}^{\leq}_{\mathfrak{C}}
and

\mathsf{Attr}^{f}_{\mathfrak{C}}\cap\bigl(\mathsf{Attr}^{\leq}_{\mathfrak{C}}\setminus\mathsf{Attr}^{<}_{\mathfrak{C}}\bigr)=\mathsf{Attr}_{\mathfrak{C}}
.

Example 3.5
.

In the case when
\mathsf{X}
is constructed from quiver representation, there is a nice description of attracting sets, due to Andrei Neguţ
[
96
]
. The setting in loc. cit. is for a Nakajima quiver variety (i.e.,
G_{\mathrm{odd}}=1
), nevertheless the arguments are applicable to the situation when
G_{\mathrm{odd}}\neq 1
. We record the result here. Consider
\mathsf{X}=\mathcal{M}^{\zeta_{+}}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})
for a quiver
Q
with dimension vectors
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})
and the stability condition
\zeta_{+}
, let
\mathsf{A}=\mathbb{C}^{\times}_{a}
that acts on framing vector space as
W=aW^{(1)}\oplus W^{(2)}
, so that the framing dimension vector decomposes accordingly
{mathbfw=\mathbf{w}^{(1)}+\mathbf{w}^{(2)}}
, then

\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}=\bigsqcup_{\mathbf{v}^{(1)}+\mathbf{v}^{(2)}=\mathbf{v}}\mathcal{M}^{\zeta}\bigl(\mathbf{v}^{(1)},\mathbf{w}^{(1)}\bigr)\times\mathcal{M}^{\zeta}\bigl(\mathbf{v}^{(2)},\mathbf{w}^{(2)}\bigr).

We choose the chamber
\mathfrak{C}
such that
a>0
, then the same argument in
[
96
, Lemma 3.10]
can be applied, and
\mathsf{Attr}_{\mathfrak{C}}
consists of quiver representations
(V,W)
,
\bigl(V^{(1)},W^{(1)}\bigr)
,
\bigl(V^{(2)},W^{(2)}\bigr)
in
\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}
that fits into exact sequences

0\rightarrow V^{(1)}\hookrightarrow V\twoheadrightarrow V^{(2)}\rightarrow 0
,
that commutes with
\bigl(x_{a},\widetilde{x}_{a}\bigr)
and also commutes with
(\alpha_{i},\widetilde{\alpha}_{i})
through the splitting exact sequence

0\rightarrow W^{(1)}\hookrightarrow W\twoheadrightarrow W^{(2)}\rightarrow 0
.
Moreover, the same argument in
[
96
, Lemma 3.11]
can be applied (since the moment map equation is not essential in the proof), and
\mathsf{Attr}^{f}_{\mathfrak{C}}
consists of quiver representations
(V,W)
,
\bigl(V^{(1)},W^{(1)}\bigr)
,
\bigl(V^{(2)},W^{(2)}\bigr)
in
\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}
that fits into the complex

V^{(1)}\overset{f}{\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow}V\overset{g}{\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow}V^{(2)}
,
such that

•

the composition
g\circ f=0
, and

•

f
and
g
commutes with
(x_{a},\widetilde{x}_{a})
and also commutes with
(\alpha_{i},\widetilde{\alpha}_{i})
through the splitting exact sequence

0\rightarrow W^{(1)}\hookrightarrow W\twoheadrightarrow W^{(2)}\rightarrow 0
.

•

there exist filtrations of quiver representations

\displaystyle V^{(1)}=E^{0}\twoheadrightarrow E^{1}\twoheadrightarrow\cdots\twoheadrightarrow E^{k-1}\twoheadrightarrow E^{k}=\mathrm{Im}(f),

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ker}(g)=F^{k}\twoheadrightarrow F^{k-1}\twoheadrightarrow\cdots\twoheadrightarrow F^{1}\twoheadrightarrow F^{0}=V^{(2)},

such that the kernels of
E^{l}\twoheadrightarrow E^{l+1}
and
F^{l+1}\twoheadrightarrow F^{l}
are isomorphic quiver representations.

In particular,

\mathcal{M}^{\zeta}\bigl(\mathbf{v}^{(1)},\mathbf{w}^{(1)}\bigr)\times\mathcal{M}^{\zeta}\bigl(\mathbf{v}^{(2)},\mathbf{w}^{(2)}\bigr)\preceq\mathcal{M}^{\zeta}\bigl(\mathbf{v}^{(1)}+\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w}^{(1)}\bigr)\times\mathcal{M}^{\zeta}\bigl(\mathbf{v}^{(2)}-\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w}^{(2)}\bigr),

for all
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{N}^{Q_{0}}
.

The restriction of partial polarization
\mathrm{Pol}_{\mathsf{X}}
to
\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}
decomposes according to the chamber
\mathfrak{C}
as

\smash{\mathrm{Pol}_{\mathsf{X}}|_{\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}}=\mathrm{Pol}_{\mathsf{X}}|_{\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}},>0}+\mathrm{Pol}_{\mathsf{X}}|_{\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}},\mathrm{fixed}}+\mathrm{Pol}_{\mathsf{X}}|_{\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}},<0}.}

Definition 3.2
.

We define the index bundle

\mathsf{ind}=\mathrm{Pol}^{+}_{\mathsf{X}}|_{\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}},>0}-\mathrm{Pol}^{-}_{\mathsf{X}}|_{\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}},>0}\in K_{\mathsf{T}}\bigl(\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}\bigr)
,
the alternating sum of attracting part of partial polarization.

Lemma 3.2
.

\mathrm{Pol}_{\mathsf{X}}|_{\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}},\mathrm{fixed}}
is a partial polarization on
\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}
.

Proof.

Let
T_{\mathsf{X}}=\mathrm{Pol}^{+}_{\mathsf{X}}+\hbar^{-1}\bigl(\mathrm{Pol}^{+}_{\mathsf{X}}\bigr)^{\vee}+\mathrm{Pol}^{-}_{\mathsf{X}}+\hbar(\mathrm{Pol}^{-}_{\mathsf{X}})^{\vee}+\mathscr{E}
, then there exists a decomposition of
\mathscr{E}
into eigenspaces of
\mathsf{A}
-actions

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}|_{\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}}=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathrm{Cochar}(\mathsf{A})}\mathscr{E}^{\lambda}.

By the duality
\mathscr{E}\cong\mathscr{E}^{\vee}
, we have
\mathscr{E}^{-\lambda}\cong\bigl(\mathscr{E}^{\lambda}\bigr)^{\vee}
, therefore we have

\displaystyle\Theta\bigl(\mathscr{E}^{0}\bigr)=\Theta(\mathscr{E}|_{\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}})\otimes\bigotimes_{\lambda(\mathfrak{C})>0}\Theta\bigl(\mathscr{E}^{\lambda}\bigr)^{\otimes 2}.

In particular, we see that
\Theta\bigl(\mathscr{E}^{0}\bigr)
admits a square root. Moreover, we have the decomposition of the tangent bundle of
\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}
in
K_{\mathsf{T}}\bigl(\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}\bigr)

\displaystyle T_{\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}}=\mathrm{Pol}^{+}_{\mathsf{X}}|_{\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}},\mathrm{fixed}}+\hbar^{-1}\left(\mathrm{Pol}^{+}_{\mathsf{X}}|_{\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}},\mathrm{fixed}}\right)^{\vee}+\mathrm{Pol}^{-}_{\mathsf{X}}|_{\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}},\mathrm{fixed}}+\hbar\left(\mathrm{Pol}^{-}_{\mathsf{X}}|_{\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}},\mathrm{fixed}}\right)^{\vee}+\mathscr{E}^{0},

which implies that
\mathrm{Pol}_{\mathsf{X}}|_{\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}},\mathrm{fixed}}
is a partial polarization on
\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}
.
∎

We denote by
\mathrm{Pol}_{\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}}
the restriction of partial polarization
\mathrm{Pol}_{\mathsf{X}}|_{\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}},\mathrm{fixed}}
on
\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}
.

3.2
Attractive line bundle

This is the most crucial part of our construction of elliptic stable envelopes for partially-polarized varieties. An important ingredient that we need from
[
109
]
is the following.

Definition 3.3
.

A line bundle
\mathscr{L}
on
\mathrm{Ell}_{\mathsf{T}}(\mathsf{X})
is called attractive for a given chamber
\mathfrak{C}
if

\deg_{\mathsf{A}}\mathscr{L}=\deg_{\mathsf{A}}\Theta(N^{-}_{\mathsf{X}/\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}})
,
where
\deg_{\mathsf{A}}\mathscr{L}
is the degree of the restriction of
\mathscr{L}
to the fiber along the projection
\mathrm{Ell}_{\mathsf{T}}\bigl(\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}\bigr)\rightarrow\mathrm{Ell}_{\mathsf{T}/\mathsf{A}}\bigl(\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}\bigr)
.
6
6

6

The degree of a line bundle
\mathscr{L}
on an abelian variety
\mathcal{A}
is defined as the image of
\mathscr{L}
in the Néron–Severi group
{\mathrm{NS}(\mathcal{A})=\mathrm{Pic}(\mathcal{A})/\mathrm{Pic}^{0}(\mathcal{A})}
, see Appendix
A
for details.
The
\deg_{\mathsf{A}}
takes value in
H^{0}\bigl(\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}},S^{2}\mathrm{Char}(\mathsf{A})\bigr)
.

Definition 3.4
.

From now on, we fix a square root for
\Theta(\mathscr{E})
and define
\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X}}:=\Theta(\mathrm{Pol}_{\mathsf{X}})\otimes\smash{\Theta(\mathscr{E})^{\otimes\frac{1}{2}}}
.

Proposition 3.2
.

\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X}}
is an attractive line bundle for every chamber
\mathfrak{C}
of
\mathrm{Cochar}(\mathsf{A})
.

Proof.

Since
S^{2}\mathrm{Char}(\mathsf{A})
is a torsion-free abelian group, it suffices to show that
\deg_{\mathsf{A}}\bigl(\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X}}^{\otimes 2}\bigr)=\smash{\deg_{\mathsf{A}}\bigl(\Theta\bigl(N^{-}_{\mathsf{X}/\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}}+N^{-\vee}_{\mathsf{X}/\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}}\bigr)\bigr)}
. Since
N^{-\vee}_{\mathsf{X}/\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}}
is
\mathsf{A}
-equivariantly isomorphic to
N^{+}_{\mathsf{X}/\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}}
, we have

\deg_{\mathsf{A}}\bigl(\Theta\bigl(N^{-}_{\mathsf{X}/\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}}+N^{-\vee}_{\mathsf{X}/\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}}\bigr)\bigr)=\deg_{\mathsf{A}}\bigl(\Theta(N_{\mathsf{X}/\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}})\bigr)=\deg_{\mathsf{A}}(\Theta(T_{\mathsf{X}})).

On the other hand,
\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X}}^{\otimes 2}\cong\Theta\bigl(\mathrm{Pol}_{\mathsf{X}}+\mathrm{Pol}^{\vee}_{\mathsf{X}}+\mathscr{E}\bigr)
, and its
\mathsf{A}
-degree equals to that of

\Theta\bigl(\mathrm{Pol}^{+}_{\mathsf{X}}+\hbar^{-1}\bigl(\mathrm{Pol}^{+}_{\mathsf{X}}\bigr)^{\vee}+\mathrm{Pol}^{-}_{\mathsf{X}}+\hbar(\mathrm{Pol}^{-}_{\mathsf{X}})^{\vee}+\mathscr{E}\bigr)=\Theta(T_{\mathsf{X}}),

this proves the lemma.
∎

Remark 3.3
.

For a line bundle
\mathscr{L}
on
\mathrm{Ell}_{\mathsf{T}}(\mathsf{X})
, define
\mathscr{L}^{\triangledown}:=\mathscr{L}^{\vee}\otimes\Theta(T_{\mathsf{X}})
. Then there is an isomorphism
\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X}}^{\triangledown}\cong\Theta\bigl(\mathrm{Pol}^{\mathrm{op}}_{\mathsf{X}}\bigr)\otimes\Theta(\mathscr{E})^{\otimes\frac{1}{2}}
, therefore
\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X}}^{\triangledown}
is the attractive line bundle associated to the opposite partial polarization.

The attractive line bundle associated to a polarization naturally restricts to
\mathsf{A}
-fixed points. In fact we can define
\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X},\mathsf{A}}:=i^{*}\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X}}\otimes\Theta(-N^{-}_{\mathsf{X}/\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}})
where
i\colon\mathrm{Ell}_{\mathsf{T}}\bigl(\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}\bigr)\rightarrow\mathrm{Ell}_{\mathsf{T}}(\mathsf{X})
is the map induced by the inclusion
\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{X}
. On the other hand, we also have the line bundle
\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}}
defined using the restriction of the partial polarization
\mathrm{Pol}_{\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}}\in\mathrm{Pic}\bigl(\mathrm{Ell}_{\mathsf{T}}\bigl(\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}\bigr)\bigr)
, i.e.,

\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}}=\Theta(\mathrm{Pol}_{\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}})\otimes\Theta\bigl(\mathscr{E}^{0}\bigr)^{\otimes\frac{1}{2}}
.

\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X},\mathsf{A}}
is not isomorphic to
\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}}
in general, and they are related as follows

\displaystyle\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X},\mathsf{A}}\otimes\mathscr{U}\cong\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}}\otimes\Theta(\hbar)^{-\operatorname{rk}\mathsf{ind}}\otimes\tau(-\hbar\det\mathsf{ind})^{*}\mathscr{U}.

(3.5)

\mathscr{U}
is the universal line bundle and
\tau(-\hbar\det\mathsf{ind})
is the translation
[
2
]
, whose definitions are recalled below.

3.3
Universal line bundle, Kähler torus and resonant locus

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an abelian variety and
\mathcal{A}^{\vee}
its dual abelian variety, then there is a universal line bundle
\mathscr{U}_{\text{Poincar\'{e}}}
on
\mathcal{A}^{\vee}\times\mathcal{A}
. The sections of
\mathscr{U}_{\text{Poincar\'{e}}}
on the universal cover of
\mathcal{A}^{\vee}_{s}\times\mathcal{A}_{z}
has the same factor of automorphy as the function

\frac{\vartheta(sz)}{\vartheta(s)\vartheta(z)}
.

Assumption.
We assume that
\mathrm{Pic}(\mathsf{X})
is finitely generated as an abelian group, and we fix a set of generators
\{\mathscr{L}^{\circ}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{r}
, which induces a group homomorphism

\mathsf{K}=(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{r}\twoheadrightarrow\mathrm{Pic}(\mathsf{X})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{C}^{\times}
.

\mathsf{K}
is called the Kähler torus.

We choose an equivariant lift
\mathscr{L}_{i}\in\mathrm{Pic}_{\mathsf{T}}(\mathsf{X})
for each
\mathscr{L}^{\circ}_{i}
, then the elliptic Chern class gives a map
c_{i}\colon\mathrm{Ell}_{\mathsf{T}}(\mathsf{X})\rightarrow\mathbb{E}
. We consider
c_{i}\times 1\colon\mathrm{Ell}_{\mathsf{T}}(\mathsf{X})\times\mathscr{E}_{z_{i}}\rightarrow\mathbb{E}\times\mathscr{E}_{z_{i}}
and define

\mathscr{U}(\mathscr{L}_{i},z_{i}):=(c_{i}\times 1)^{*}\mathscr{U}_{\text{Poincar\'{e}}},

where we identify
\mathbb{E}\cong\mathscr{E}_{z_{i}}^{\vee}
. Moreover, we define the extended equivariant elliptic cohomology
{\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{T}}(\mathsf{X}):=\mathrm{Ell}_{\mathsf{T}}(\mathsf{X})\times\mathscr{E}_{\mathsf{K}}}

which is a scheme over

\mathscr{B}_{\mathsf{T},\mathsf{X}}:=\mathscr{E}_{\mathsf{T}}\times\mathscr{E}_{\mathsf{K}}
.
Using the identification
{\mathscr{E}_{\mathsf{K}}\cong\prod_{i=1}^{r}\mathscr{E}_{z_{i}}}
, the line bundle
\mathscr{U}(\mathscr{L}_{i},z_{i})
is naturally defined on the extended equivariant elliptic cohomology
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{T}}(\mathsf{X})
, and we set

\mathscr{U}:=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{r}\mathscr{U}(\mathscr{L}_{i},z_{i})
.

Let
\mathcal{F}_{j}\preceq\mathcal{F}_{i}
be a pair of connected components of
\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}
and consider the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{T}}(\mathcal{F}_{j}\times\mathcal{F}_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{T}/\mathsf{A}}(\mathcal{F}_{j}\times\mathcal{F}_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{B}_{\mathsf{T},\mathsf{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{B}_{\mathsf{T}/\mathsf{A},\mathsf{X}},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

where
\mathscr{B}_{\mathsf{T}/\mathsf{A},\mathsf{X}}:=\mathscr{E}_{\mathsf{T}/\mathsf{A}}\times\mathscr{E}_{\mathsf{K}}
.

Definition 3.5
.

Let
\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X},\mathcal{F}}
be the restriction of
\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X},\mathsf{A}}
to a connected component
\mathcal{F}
of
\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}
. The resonant locus
\mathbf{\Delta}
is defined as the union of

p\bigl(\mathrm{supp}R\phi_{*}\bigl(\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X},\mathcal{F}_{j}}\otimes\mathscr{U}\boxtimes(\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X},\mathcal{F}_{i}}\otimes\mathscr{U})^{\vee}\bigr)\bigr)\subset\mathscr{B}_{\mathsf{T}/\mathsf{A},\mathsf{X}},

over all pairs
\mathcal{F}_{j}\preceq\mathcal{F}_{i}
of connected components of
\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}
. We also use the same notation
\mathbf{\Delta}
for its preimages.

Verbatim to that of
[
109
, Proposition 2.6]
, we have the following.

Lemma 3.3
.

The complement of
\mathbf{\Delta}
in
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{T}}(\mathsf{X})
is open and dense.

For a homomorphism between abelian varieties
g\colon\mathscr{E}_{\mathsf{T}}\rightarrow\mathscr{E}_{\mathsf{K}}
, we associate an automorphism
{\tau(g)\colon\mathscr{B}_{\mathsf{T},\mathsf{X}}\cong\mathscr{B}_{\mathsf{T},\mathsf{X}}}
by

(t,z)\mapstochar\rightarrow(t,z+g(t))
,
where
t
is the coordinate on
\mathscr{E}_{\mathsf{T}}
. We also use the same notation
\tau(g)
for its pullback to
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{T}}(\mathsf{X})
. Such transformation does not affect
\mathrm{Ell}_{\mathsf{T}}(\mathsf{X})
, only the Kähler parameters are shifted. An example is as follows: for a pair

\displaystyle\mu\in\mathrm{Char}(\mathsf{T})=\mathrm{Hom}(\mathscr{E}_{\mathsf{T}},\mathbb{E}),\qquad\lambda\in\mathrm{Cochar}(\mathsf{K})=\mathrm{Hom}(\mathbb{E},\mathscr{E}_{\mathsf{K}}),

\lambda\mu
is an abelian variety homomorphism, so we have a shift map
\tau(\lambda\mu)
.

The line bundle
\tau(g)^{*}\mathscr{U}\otimes\mathscr{U}^{-1}
depends linearly on
g
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\frac{\tau(g_{1})^{*}\mathscr{U}}{\mathscr{U}}\otimes\frac{\tau(g_{2})^{*}\mathscr{U}}{\mathscr{U}}\cong\frac{\tau(g_{1}\cdot g_{2})^{*}\mathscr{U}}{\mathscr{U}}.

In the case when
\mu\in\mathrm{Char}(\mathsf{T})
and
\lambda\in\mathrm{Cochar}(\mathsf{K})
, there is a meromorphic section

\frac{\vartheta(\lambda\cdot\mu)}{\vartheta(\lambda)\vartheta(\mu)}
,
of the line bundle
\tau(\lambda\mu)^{*}\mathscr{U}\otimes\mathscr{U}^{-1}
, here
\lambda\cdot\mu
is the coordinate multiplication
[
2
, Lemma 2.4]
. We will write
\mathscr{U}(\lambda,\mu)
for the line bundle
\tau(\lambda\mu)^{*}\mathscr{U}\otimes\mathscr{U}^{-1}
.

3.4
Elliptic stable envelope

Now we have all the ingredients, and the main result of this section: the existence and uniqueness of the elliptic stable envelope for partially-polarized varieties, directly follows from the general construction in
[
109
]
.

Theorem 3.1
.

If
\mathsf{X}
is partially polarized, then there exists a unique section

\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C},\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X}}}\in\Gamma\bigl(\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{T}}\bigl(\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}\bigr)\backslash\mathbf{\Delta},\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X}}\otimes\mathscr{U}\boxtimes(\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X},\mathsf{A}}\otimes\mathscr{U})^{\triangledown}\bigr),

such that it is supported on
\mathsf{Attr}^{f}_{\mathfrak{C}}
and that its restriction to the complement of
\mathsf{Attr}^{<}_{\mathfrak{C}}
is given by
(-1)^{\operatorname{rk}\mathsf{ind}}[\mathsf{Attr}_{\mathfrak{C}}]
.
7
7

7

For the definition of supports, see Appendix
A
.

Proof.

This is a special case of
[
109
, Theorem 1]
applied to the attractive line bundle
\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X}}
.
∎

Equivalently,
\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C},\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X}}}
gives rise to a map between sheaves
\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X},\mathsf{A}}\otimes\mathscr{U}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X}}\otimes\mathscr{U}
over the non-resonant locus
\mathscr{B}_{\mathsf{T},\mathsf{X}}\backslash\mathbf{\Delta}
, which is uniquely characterized by

(1)

The support of
\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C},\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X}}}
is triangular with respect to
\mathfrak{C}
, i.e.,
\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C},\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X}}}([\mathcal{F}_{i}])|_{\mathcal{F}_{j}}=0
for a pair of connected components of
\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}
such that
\mathcal{F}_{j}\npreceq\mathcal{F}_{i}
.

(2)

The diagonal
\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C},\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X}}}([\mathcal{F}_{i}])|_{\mathcal{F}_{i}}=(-1)^{\operatorname{rk}\mathsf{ind}}\vartheta(N^{-}_{\mathsf{X}/\mathcal{F}_{i}})
, where
\vartheta(N^{-}_{\mathsf{X}/\mathcal{F}_{i}})
is the canonical section of the elliptic Thom line bundle
\Theta(N^{-}_{\mathsf{X}/\mathcal{F}_{i}})
.

We will use the above point of view on the stable envelope in the following discussions.

According to (
3.5
), the elliptic stable envelope is a map between sheaves

\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C},\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X}}}\colon\ \mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}}\otimes\Theta(\hbar)^{-\operatorname{rk}\mathsf{ind}}\otimes\tau(-\hbar\det\mathsf{ind})^{*}\mathscr{U}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X}}\otimes\mathscr{U}.

This turns out to be more practical since it is usually easier to compute
\mathrm{Pol}_{\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}}
than
\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X},\mathsf{A}}
.

Change of partial polarization.
If we change the partial polarization by

\mathrm{Pol}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{X}}=\mathrm{Pol}_{\mathsf{X}}-(\mathscr{F}_{+}+\mathscr{F}_{-})+\hbar^{-1}\mathscr{F}^{\vee}_{+}+\hbar\mathscr{F}^{\vee}_{-},

for some
\mathscr{F}_{+},\mathscr{F}_{-}\in K_{\mathsf{T}}(\mathsf{X})
, then the associated attractive line bundle changes by

\mathscr{S}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{X}}\otimes\mathscr{U}\cong\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X}}\otimes\tau(\hbar\det(\mathscr{F}_{+}-\mathscr{F}_{-}))^{*}\mathscr{U}\otimes\Theta(\hbar)^{\operatorname{rk}\mathscr{F}},

and the elliptic stable envelope changes accordingly

\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C},\mathscr{S}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{X}}}(z)=\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C},\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X}}}(z+\hbar\det(\mathscr{F}_{+}-\mathscr{F}_{-}))\cdot(-1)^{\operatorname{rk}\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{moving}}},

where
z
is the Kähler parameter (coordinate on
\mathscr{E}_{\mathsf{K}}
), and
\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{moving}}
is the
\mathsf{A}
-moving part of
\mathscr{F}|_{\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}}
. In particular, if we choose the opposite partial polarization
\mathrm{Pol}^{\mathrm{op}}_{\mathsf{X}}=\hbar^{-1}\bigl(\mathrm{Pol}^{+}_{\mathsf{X}}\bigr)^{\vee}+\hbar(\mathrm{Pol}^{-}_{\mathsf{X}})^{\vee}
, then

\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C},\mathscr{S}^{\triangledown}_{\mathsf{X}}}(z)=\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C},\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X}}}\bigl(z+\hbar\det\bigl(\mathrm{Pol}^{+}_{\mathsf{X}}-\mathrm{Pol}^{-}_{\mathsf{X}}\bigr)\bigr)\cdot(-1)^{\operatorname{rk}\mathrm{Pol}_{\mathsf{X},\mathrm{moving}}}.

Example 3.6

(abelian Gauge groups)
.

Let us consider the Higgs branch of an Abelian
\mathcal{N}=2
gauge theory, i.e., Example
2.1
. In this case
\mathsf{X}:=\mu^{-1}_{\mathrm{ev}}(0)^{\zeta-ss}/\!\!/G
is a
\mathsf{T}
-equivariant smooth deformation of
\mathsf{X}_{0}:=\mu^{-1}(0)^{\zeta-ss}/\!\!/G
over the base
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathrm{ev}}^{\perp}
, where
\mathsf{T}=\mathsf{A}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}_{\hbar}=\bigl(\bigl(\mathbb{C}^{\times}\bigr)^{\operatorname{rk}\mathcal{R}}/G\bigr)\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}_{\hbar}
.
\mathbb{C}^{\times}_{\hbar}
acts on
\mathcal{R}\oplus\mathcal{R}^{\vee}
as
\mathcal{R}\oplus\hbar^{-1}\mathcal{R}^{\vee}
and acts on
\mathfrak{g}
as
\hbar^{-1}\mathfrak{g}
. Then every
\mathsf{A}
-fixed point of the hypertoric variety
\mathsf{X}_{0}
extends to an étale cover of the base
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathrm{ev}}^{\perp}
, which must be trivial cover since every point in this cover is repelled by the
\mathbb{C}^{\times}_{\hbar}
action from the fixed point. Let
\mathcal{F}_{p}
be the component of
\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}
that contains fixed point
p\in\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}_{0}
, then for any chamber
\mathfrak{C}
of
\mathrm{Lie}(\mathsf{A})_{\mathbb{R}}
,
\mathcal{F}_{p}\preceq\mathcal{F}_{q}
in
\mathsf{X}
if and only if
p\preceq q
in
\mathsf{X}_{0}
. In other words, the chamber
\mathfrak{C}
induces exactly the same partial order on
\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}
and
\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}_{0}
. Moreover, the inclusion
\mathsf{X}_{0}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{X}
induces isomorphism on equivariant elliptic cohomology
{\mathrm{Ell}_{\mathsf{T}}(\mathsf{X}_{0})\cong\mathrm{Ell}_{\mathsf{T}}(\mathsf{X})}
. It is straightforward to see that
\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X}}\cong\Theta(\mathscr{R})\cong\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X}_{0}}
, i.e.,
\mathsf{X}
and
\mathsf{X}_{0}
have the same attractive line bundle. The normal bundles are isomorphic
N^{\pm}_{\mathsf{X}/\mathcal{F}_{p}}|_{p}=N^{\pm}_{\mathsf{X}_{0}/p}|_{p}
, so the index bundles are also isomorphic. To summarize,
\mathsf{X}
and
\mathsf{X}_{0}
have the same elliptic stable envelope

\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C},\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X}}}([\mathcal{F}_{p}])=\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C},\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X}_{0}}}([p])
.
The explicit formula of the elliptic stable envelope for hypertoric varieties can be found in
[
2
]
, see also
[
133
]
.

Example 3.7

(resolved determinantal varieties)
.

Consider the quiver
Q
with one odd node, i.e.,
Q_{0}=Q^{\mathrm{odd}}_{0}=\{1\}
and
\mathbf{v}=N,\mathbf{w}=L
, and
\zeta=\zeta_{-}
, then
\mathcal{M}^{\zeta}(N,L)
is nonempty if and only if
N\leq L
, and if nonempty then it is isomorphic to the total space of the vector bundle

\mathrm{Tot}\bigl(\mathcal{V}^{\oplus L}\rightarrow\mathrm{Gr}(N,L)\bigr)
,
where
\mathcal{V}
is the tautological bundle of rank
N
on the Grassmannian. It is a resolution of the determinantal variety which parameterizes
L\times L
matrices of rank
\leq N
.

In this case
\mathsf{A}=T_{W}=(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{L}
and
\mathsf{T}=\mathsf{A}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}_{\hbar}
.
\mathsf{A}
acts on both the base and the fiber of the bundle
\mathrm{Tot}\bigl(\mathcal{V}^{\oplus L}\rightarrow\mathrm{Gr}(N,L)\bigr)
, and
\mathbb{C}^{\times}_{\hbar}
scales the fiber with weight
\hbar^{-1}
. Let the equivariant parameters of the gauge group
\mathrm{GL}_{N}
be
\bm{s}=\{s_{a}\}_{a=1}^{N}
and the equivariant parameters of
\mathsf{A}
be
\bm{x}=\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{L}
. Connected components of
\mathsf{A}
-fixed points are labelled by order-preserving embedding
p\colon\{1,\dots,N\}\hookrightarrow\{1,\dots,L\}
. Denote by
\{m_{1},\dots,m_{L}\}
the coordinates on
\mathrm{Lie}(\mathsf{A})
, then we choose the chamber
\mathfrak{C}
such that
m_{1}<\cdots<m_{L}
. We choose the partial polarization as in (
3.2
). Then we claim that the stable envelope is the following:
8
8

8

For the gauge-theoretic derivation of (
3.6
), see Section
6.1
. Note that the gauge-theoretic computation uses the normalization of stable envelope such that
\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C},\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X}}}([\mathcal{F}])|_{\mathcal{F}}=\vartheta(N^{-}_{\mathsf{X}/{\mathcal{F}}})
.

\displaystyle\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C},\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X}}}([\mathcal{F}_{p}])=\text{Sym}_{S_{N}}\left[\left(\prod_{a=1}^{N}\bm{f}_{p(a)}(s_{a},\bm{x},\hbar,z)\right)\cdot\left(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}a>b\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\vartheta\bigl(s_{a}s_{b}^{-1}\bigr)}\right)\right],

(3.6)

where
\mathrm{Sym}_{S_{N}}
means summation over permutations
\{s_{a}\}\mapstochar\rightarrow\{s_{\sigma(a)}\}_{\sigma\in S_{N}}
, and
\bm{f}_{m}(s,\bm{x},\hbar,z)
is the following function:

\bm{f}_{m}(s,\bm{x},\hbar,z):=\frac{\vartheta\bigl(sx_{m}\hbar^{m-L}z\bigr)}{\vartheta\bigl(\hbar^{m-L}z\bigr)}\prod_{i<m}\vartheta(sx_{i})\prod_{j>m}\vartheta(sx_{j}\hbar),

where
z
is the Kähler parameter corresponding to the ample line bundle
(\det\mathcal{V})^{-1}
.

Let us show that (
3.6
) is indeed the stable envelope. The first thing to check is that (
3.6
) is a map between line bundles
\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}}\otimes\Theta(\hbar)^{-\operatorname{rk}\mathsf{ind}}\otimes\tau(-\hbar\det\mathsf{ind})^{*}\mathscr{U}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X}}\otimes\mathscr{U}
. Note that
\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}}
is a trivial line bundle, and
\mathsf{ind}
has equivariant weights
x_{i}/x_{p(a)}
such that
i>p(a)
. Every summand in the symmetrization (
3.6
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\Biggl(\prod_{i,a}\vartheta(s_{a}x_{i})\Biggr)\Biggl(\prod_{a}\frac{\vartheta\bigl(s_{a}x_{p(a)}\hbar^{p(a)-L}z\bigr)}{\vartheta(s_{a}x_{p(a)})\vartheta\bigl(\hbar^{p(a)-L}z\bigr)}\Biggr)\Biggl(\prod_{i>p(a)}\frac{\vartheta(s_{a}x_{i}\hbar)}{\vartheta(s_{a}x_{i})\vartheta(\hbar)}\Biggr)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\Biggl(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}a>b\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\vartheta\bigl(s_{a}s_{b}^{-1}\bigr)}\Biggr)\vartheta(\hbar)^{\#(i>p(a))},

which is a meromorphic section of the line bundle

\displaystyle\Theta(\mathrm{Pol}_{\mathsf{X}})\otimes\mathscr{U}\otimes(\tau(-\hbar\det\mathsf{ind})^{*}\mathscr{U}|_{\mathcal{F}_{p}})^{-1}\otimes\Theta\bigl(\mathcal{V}\mathcal{V}^{\vee}\bigr)^{\otimes\frac{-1}{2}}\otimes\Theta(\hbar)^{\operatorname{rk}\mathsf{ind}},

which is isomorphic to
\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X}}\otimes\mathscr{U}\otimes(\tau(-\hbar\det\mathsf{ind})^{*}\mathscr{U})^{-1}\otimes\Theta(\hbar)^{\operatorname{rk}\mathsf{ind}}
, this shows that (
3.6
) lives in the correct line bundle.

Next, we verify that (
3.6
) is triangular with respect to
\mathfrak{C}
. In fact, if
\mathcal{F}_{p^{\prime}}\npreceq\mathcal{F}_{p}
is another connected component of fixed points, in other words,
p^{\prime}\colon\{1,\dots,N\}\hookrightarrow\{1,\dots,L\}
is an order-preserving embedding and there exists
1\leq a\leq N
such that
p^{\prime}(a)<p(a)
, then for every
\sigma\in S_{N}
there exists
b
such that
p^{\prime}(\sigma(b))<p(b)
, so every summand in the symmetrization (
3.6
) vanishes.

Finally, the restriction of (
3.6
) to diagonal is

\displaystyle(-1)^{\#(i>p(a))}\prod_{a=1}^{N}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i<p(a)\\
i\notin\mathrm{Im}(p)\end{subarray}}\vartheta\left(\frac{x_{i}}{x_{p(a)}}\right)\prod_{i>p(a)}\vartheta\left(\frac{x_{p(a)}}{x_{i}\hbar}\right),

which equals to
(-1)^{\operatorname{rk}\mathsf{ind}}\vartheta(N^{-}_{\mathsf{X}/\mathcal{F}_{p}})
. This verifies that (
3.6
) is indeed the stable envelope for
\mathcal{M}^{\zeta}(N,L)
with respect to the chamber
\mathfrak{C}
.

3.5
Triangle lemma and duality

Let
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}\subset\mathfrak{C}
be a face, and let
\mathsf{A}^{\prime}\subset\mathsf{A}
be the subtorus associated to the span of
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}
in
\mathrm{Lie}(\mathsf{A})
.

Proposition 3.3

(triangle lemma)
.

We have

\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C},\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X}}}=\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C}^{\prime},\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X}}}\circ\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C}/\mathfrak{C}^{\prime},\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X},\mathsf{A}^{\prime}}}
.
Moreover,

\displaystyle\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C}/\mathfrak{C}^{\prime},\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X},\mathsf{A}^{\prime}}}(z)=\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C}/\mathfrak{C}^{\prime},\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}^{\prime}}}}(z-\hbar\det\mathsf{ind}_{\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}^{\prime}}}),

(3.7)

where
\mathsf{ind}_{\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}^{\prime}}}=\mathrm{Pol}^{+}_{\mathsf{X}}|_{\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}^{\prime}},>0}-\mathrm{Pol}^{-}_{\mathsf{X}}|_{\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}^{\prime}},>0}
is the alternating sum of the attracting part of partial polarization with respect to the chamber
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

This is verbatim to that of
[
2
, Proposition 3.3]
, replacing polarization with partial polarization.
∎

Assume that
\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{T}}
is proper, then we have the equivariant pushforward
f_{\circledast}\colon\Theta(T_{\mathsf{X}})\!\rightarrow\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{E}_{\mathsf{T}},\mathrm{localized}}
for the map
f\colon\mathsf{X}\rightarrow\mathrm{pt}
, and accordingly we define

\mathbf{Stab}^{*}_{\mathfrak{C},\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X}}}(z):=\mathbf{Stab}_{-\mathfrak{C},\mathscr{S}^{\triangledown}_{\mathsf{X}}}(-z)^{\vee}
,
where
z\in\mathscr{E}_{\mathrm{Pic}_{\mathsf{T}(\mathsf{X})}}
is the Kähler parameter and the dual means transpose with respect to the duality

\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X}}\otimes\mathscr{S}^{\triangledown}_{\mathsf{X}}\overset{\mathrm{product}}{\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow}\Theta(T_{\mathsf{X}})\overset{f_{\circledast}}{\rightarrow}\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{E}_{\mathsf{T}},\mathrm{localized}}.

Proposition 3.4

(duality)
.

We have

\displaystyle\mathbf{Stab}^{*}_{\mathfrak{C},\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X}}}\circ\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C},\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X}}}=1.

(3.8)

Proof.

This is verbatim to that of
[
2
, Proposition 3.4]
, replacing polarization by partial polarization.
∎

Combine the duality (
3.8
) with the formula of the stable envelope for opposite partial polarization, and we obtain the following corollary.

Corollary 3.1

(inverse stable envelope for the opposite chamber)
.

We have

\mathbf{Stab}^{-1}_{-\mathfrak{C},\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X}}}(z)=(-1)^{\operatorname{rk}\mathrm{Pol}_{\mathsf{X},\mathrm{moving}}}\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C},\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X}}}\bigl(-z+\hbar\det\bigl(\mathrm{Pol}^{+}_{\mathsf{X}}-\mathrm{Pol}^{-}_{\mathsf{X}}\bigr)\bigr)^{\vee}.

where
z
is the Kähler parameter
(
coordinate on
\mathscr{E}_{\mathsf{K}})
.

From now on, unless specified, we will drop the attractive line bundle in the notation and write
\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C}}
for
\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C},\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X}}}
and
\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C}/\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}}
for
\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C}/\mathfrak{C}^{\prime},\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}^{\prime}}}}
, etc.

3.6
Abelianization

A general strategy to compute elliptic stable envelope for GIT quotients by nonabelian gauge group is abelianization. This is described in detail for the symplectic reduction in
[
2
, Section 4.3]
, and in this subsection, we adapt their technique to the more general setting in Section
2.1
. In the following, we point out the necessary modification to
[
2
, Section 4.3]
.

Let
S_{b}
(resp.
S_{f}
) be a maximal torus of
G_{\mathrm{ev}}
(resp.
G_{\mathrm{odd}}
) and let
B_{b}\supset S_{b}
(resp.
B_{f}\supset S_{f}
) be a Borel subgroup with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{b}_{b}
(resp.
\mathfrak{b}_{f}
). Denote by
S:=S_{b}\times S_{f}
and
B:=B_{b}\times B_{f}
, then we have a diagram similar to that of
[
2
, equation (59)]

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Fl}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu^{-1}\bigl(\mathfrak{b}_{b}^{\perp}\bigr)^{\zeta-ss}/\!\!/S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{X}_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{X}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{j}_{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{j}_{-}}

(3.9)

\mathsf{Fl}=\mu^{-1}\bigl(\mathfrak{g}_{\mathrm{ev}}^{\perp}\bigr)^{\zeta-ss}/\!\!/B\cong(\mu\times\mu_{\mathbb{R}})^{-1}\bigl(\mathfrak{g}_{\mathrm{ev}}^{\perp}\times\{\zeta\}\bigr)/U\cap S
, where
\mu_{\mathbb{R}}\colon\mathcal{R}\oplus\mathcal{R}^{\vee}\rightarrow\mathrm{Lie}(U)^{\vee}
is the real moment map with respect to the action of a maximal compact subgroup
U\subset G
and a
U
-invariant Hermitian metric on
\mathcal{R}\oplus\mathcal{R}^{\vee}
, and
\mathsf{X}_{S}=\mu^{-1}\bigl(\mathfrak{s}_{b}^{\perp}\bigr)/\!\!/_{\zeta}S
is the GIT quotient of
\mu^{-1}\bigl(\mathfrak{s}_{b}^{\perp}\bigr)
by the torus
S
. Note that
\mathsf{j}_{+}
is a non-holomorphic embedding and
\mathsf{j}_{-}
is a holomorphic immersion.
\pi
is a
G/B
-bundle, so every statement in
[
2
, Section 4.3.2]
still holds for
\pi
.

We have decompositions

\pi^{*}\mathscr{G}_{\mathrm{ev}}=\mathscr{N}_{b}+\mathscr{N}_{b}^{\vee}+\mathrm{Lie}(S_{b})\otimes\mathscr{O}_{\mathsf{Fl}},\qquad\pi^{*}\mathscr{G}_{\mathrm{odd}}=\mathscr{N}_{f}+\mathscr{N}_{f}^{\vee}+\mathrm{Lie}(S_{f})\otimes\mathscr{O}_{\mathsf{Fl}},

where
\mathscr{N}_{b}
(resp.
\mathscr{N}_{b}
) is the bundle on
\mathsf{X}_{S}
associated to the adjoint action of
S
on
\mathfrak{n}_{b}=[\mathfrak{b}_{b},\mathfrak{b}_{b}]
(resp.
\mathfrak{n}_{f}=[\mathfrak{b}_{f},\mathfrak{b}_{f}]
). The relative tangent bundle of
\pi
is
T_{\pi}=\mathscr{N}^{\vee}=\mathscr{N}_{b}^{\vee}+\mathscr{N}_{f}^{\vee}
, and the normal bundle of
\mathsf{j}_{+}
is
N_{\mathsf{j}_{+}}=\mathscr{N}+\hbar^{-1}\mathscr{N}_{b}
, and the normal bundle of
\mathsf{j}_{-}
is
N_{\mathsf{j}_{-}}=\hbar^{-1}\mathscr{N}_{b}^{\vee}
.

For simplicity, let us choose the partial polarizations

\mathrm{Pol}_{\mathsf{X}}=\mathrm{Pol}^{+}_{\mathsf{X}}=\mathscr{R}-\mathscr{G}_{\mathrm{ev}},\qquad\mathrm{Pol}_{\mathsf{X}_{S}}=\mathrm{Pol}^{+}_{\mathsf{X}_{S}}=\mathscr{R}+\hbar^{-1}\mathscr{N}_{b}^{\vee}-\mathscr{N}_{b}-\mathrm{Lie}(S_{b})\otimes\mathscr{O}_{\mathsf{X}_{S}}.

Note that the partial polarization on
\mathsf{X}_{S}
differs from the choice in Example
3.1
by
\hbar^{-1}\mathscr{N}_{b}^{\vee}-\mathscr{N}_{b}
, which affects the stable envelope by

\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C}}(z)\mapstochar\rightarrow\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C}}(z+\hbar\det\mathscr{N}_{b})\cdot(-1)^{\operatorname{rk}\mathscr{N}_{b,\mathrm{moving}}},

thus (
3.24
) still holds for such modified choice of partial polarization.

Now we have the modified version of
[
2
, equation (63)]

\mathsf{j}^{*}\bigl(\mathrm{Pol}_{\mathsf{X}_{S}}-\hbar^{-1}\mathscr{N}_{b}^{\vee}+\mathscr{N}_{f}^{\vee}\bigr)=\pi^{*}\mathrm{Pol}_{\mathsf{X}}+T_{\pi},

where
\mathsf{j}=\mathsf{j}_{-}\circ\mathsf{j}_{+}
. Also notice that
\Theta\bigl(\mathscr{N}_{f}^{\vee}\bigr)\cong\Theta(\mathscr{N}_{f})\cong\pi^{*}\Theta(\mathscr{G}_{\mathrm{odd}})^{\otimes\frac{1}{2}}
. Therefore, we have the modified version of
[
2
, equation (64)]

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{*}\mathscr{S}_{X}\otimes\Theta(T_{\pi})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{j}^{*}_{-}\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X}_{S}}\otimes\Theta\bigl(-\hbar^{-1}\mathscr{N}_{b}^{\vee}\bigr)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X}_{S}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\circledast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{j}^{*}_{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{j}_{-\circledast}}

(3.10)

The map
\mathsf{j}^{*}_{+}
in (
3.10
) is surjective since any tautological class extends to
\mathsf{X}_{S}
.

Next, we choose a connected component
\mathcal{F}\subset\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}
, and let
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
be the unique connected component of
\pi^{-1}(\mathcal{F})^{\mathsf{A}}
such that normal weights of
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
in
\pi^{-1}(\mathcal{F})
are repelling with respect to the chamber
\mathfrak{C}
, i.e.,
\left(T_{\pi}|_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}\right)_{>0}=0
. This implies that
\bigl(\mathscr{N}^{\vee}|_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}\bigr)_{>0}=0
by the same argument with
[
2
, Section 4.3.8]
. Consider the following analog of (
3.9
)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}_{S}\cap\mu^{-1}\bigl(\mathfrak{b}_{b}^{\perp}\bigr)^{\zeta-ss}/\!\!/S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{\mathcal{}}F_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{j}^{\prime}_{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{j}^{\prime}_{-}}

where
\mathcal{F}_{S}
is the connected component of
\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}_{S}
that contains
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
. Then by the same argument with
[
2
, Section 4.3.9]
,
\mathsf{j}^{\prime*}_{+}
is surjective, and

\pi^{*}(\mathsf{ind}\mathcal{F})=\mathsf{j}^{\prime*}(\mathsf{ind}\mathcal{F}_{S})
,
where
\mathsf{j}^{\prime}=\mathsf{j}^{\prime}_{-}\circ\mathsf{j}^{\prime}_{+}
.

Now we have all ingredients to state the abelianization result. We claim that the following analog of
[
2
, equation (72)]
is well defined and the diagram is commutative

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{S}_{\mathcal{F}}\otimes\mathscr{U}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{S}_{\mathcal{F}_{S}}\otimes\mathscr{U}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X}}\otimes\mathscr{U}\otimes\Theta(\hbar)^{\operatorname{rk}\mathsf{ind}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X}_{S}}\otimes\mathscr{U}\otimes\Theta(\hbar)^{\operatorname{rk}\mathsf{ind}},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{j}^{\prime}_{-\circledast}\left(\mathsf{j}^{\prime*}_{+}\right)^{-1}(\pi^{\prime}_{\circledast})^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Stab}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Stab}_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\circledast}\mathsf{j}_{+}^{*}\left(\mathsf{j}_{-\circledast}\right)^{-1}}

(3.11)

where
\mathscr{U}^{\prime}=\tau(-\hbar\det\mathsf{ind})^{*}\mathscr{U}
. In fact, the argument in
[
2
, Section 4.3.11]
works verbatim, so the inverse of the top horizontal arrow is well defined; the normal weights to
\mathfrak{b}_{b}^{\perp}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}
are non-attracting then it follows that

\mathsf{Attr}^{f}\bigl(\mu^{-1}\bigl(\mathfrak{b}_{b}^{\perp}\bigr)^{\mathsf{A}}\bigr)\subset\mu^{-1}\bigl(\mathfrak{b}_{b}^{\perp}\bigr)
,
thus
\mathbf{Stab}_{S}\circ\mathsf{j}^{\prime}_{-\circledast}
factors through
\mathsf{j}_{-\circledast}
, so the inverse of the bottom horizontal arrow is also well defined. To show that the diagram commutes, we only need to show that
\mathbf{Stab}
agrees with the composition of the rest of the arrows in the diagram when restricted to the diagonal. The point is to compare the difference between
\pi^{*}N^{-}_{\mathsf{X}/\mathcal{F}}
and
N^{-}_{\mathsf{X}_{S}/\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}
, and from our previous discussion we learn that the new repelling directions are
(T_{\pi}|_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}})_{\mathrm{moving}}
and
(N_{\mathsf{j}_{-}}|_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}})_{\mathrm{moving}}
.
(T_{\pi}|_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}})_{\mathrm{moving}}
is cancelled out by
\pi_{\circledast}
, and
(N_{\mathsf{j}_{-}}|_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}})_{\mathrm{moving}}
is cancelled with
(\mathsf{j}_{-\circledast})^{-1}\circ\mathbf{Stab}_{S}\circ\mathsf{j}^{\prime}_{-\circledast}
. This concludes the proof of abelianization (
3.11
).

3.7
\bm{R}
-matrices and dynamical Yang–Baxter equations

Definition 3.6
.

Let
\mathfrak{C}_{1}
,
\mathfrak{C}_{2}
be two chambers in
\mathrm{Lie}(\mathsf{A})
, then we define the
R
-matrix

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}_{\mathfrak{C}_{2}\shortleftarrow\mathfrak{C}_{1}}:=\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C}_{2}}^{-1}\circ\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C}_{1}}.

(3.12)

This is a map from
\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}}\otimes\tau(-\hbar\det\mathsf{ind}_{1})^{*}\mathscr{U}
to
\mathscr{S}_{\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}}\otimes\tau(-\hbar\det\mathsf{ind}_{2})^{*}\mathscr{U}
, where
\mathsf{ind}_{1}
and
\mathsf{ind}_{2}
are index bundles for the chambers
\mathfrak{C}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{C}_{2}
respectively.

Obviously it follows from definition that

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}_{\mathfrak{C}_{3}\shortleftarrow\mathfrak{C}_{2}}\mathbf{R}_{\mathfrak{C}_{2}\shortleftarrow\mathfrak{C}_{1}}=\mathbf{R}_{\mathfrak{C}_{3}\shortleftarrow\mathfrak{C}_{1}}.

(3.13)

Moreover, if
\mathfrak{C}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{C}_{2}
share a face
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}
, then (
3.7
) implies that

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}_{\mathfrak{C}_{2}\shortleftarrow\mathfrak{C}_{1}}(z)=\mathbf{R}_{\mathfrak{C}_{2}/\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}\shortleftarrow\mathfrak{C}_{1}/\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}}(z-\hbar\det\mathsf{ind}_{\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}^{\prime}},\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}}),

(3.14)

where
\mathsf{A}^{\prime}
is the subtorus corresponding to the chamber
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}
and
\mathsf{ind}_{\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}^{\prime}},\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}}
is the index bundle on
\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}^{\prime}}
for the chamber
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}
. If
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}
is of codimension one in both
\mathfrak{C}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{C}_{2}
, then we say
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}
is a wall and define

\mathbf{R}_{\mathrm{wall}}:=\mathbf{R}_{\mathfrak{C}_{2}/\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}\shortleftarrow\mathfrak{C}_{1}/\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}}
.
The dynamical Yang–Baxter equation is a direct consequence of (
3.13
) and (
3.14
) for
\mathsf{A}=(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{3}
. To see this, let
m_{1}
,
m_{2}
,
m_{3}
be coordinates on
\mathrm{Lie}(\mathsf{A})
, then the two paths connecting the chamber
\mathfrak{C}_{123}=\{m_{1}<m_{2}<m_{3}\}
to its opposite
{\mathfrak{C}_{321}=\{m_{3}<m_{2}<m_{1}\}}
, namely
\mathfrak{C}_{123}\rightarrow\mathfrak{C}_{132}\rightarrow\mathfrak{C}_{312}\rightarrow\mathfrak{C}_{321}
and
\mathfrak{C}_{123}\rightarrow\mathfrak{C}_{213}\rightarrow\mathfrak{C}_{231}\rightarrow\mathfrak{C}_{321}
, gives rise to equation between two sequence of composing
R
-matrices

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}_{12}(z-\hbar\det\mathsf{ind}_{3(12)})\mathbf{R}_{13}(z-\hbar\det\mathsf{ind}_{(13)2})\mathbf{R}_{23}(z-\hbar\det\mathsf{ind}_{1(23)})

\displaystyle\qquad=\mathbf{R}_{23}(z-\hbar\det\mathsf{ind}_{(23)1})\mathbf{R}_{13}(z-\hbar\det\mathsf{ind}_{2(13)})\mathbf{R}_{12}(z-\hbar\det\mathsf{ind}_{(12)3}),

(3.15)

where
\mathbf{R}_{ij}
is the
R
-matrix for the
ij
-wall, and the subscript of
\mathsf{ind}
means the wall and chamber for which the index bundle is taken, for example,
3(12)
means the chamber
\{m_{3}<m_{1}=m_{2}\}
.

The particular case
\mathfrak{C}_{3}=\mathfrak{C}_{1}
in (
3.13
) implies the unitarity

\mathbf{R}_{21}(-u,\hbar,z)\mathbf{R}(u,\hbar,z)=1
,
where
\mathbf{R}(u,\hbar,z)
is the wall
R
-matrix and subscript
21
means swapping the two tensor components.

3.8
\bm{R}
-matrices for quiver varieties

Let us write down the dynamical Yang–Baxter equation for the quiver variety
\mathcal{M}^{\zeta_{-}}(\mathbf{w})
for a quiver
Q
with decomposition of nodes
Q_{0}=Q^{\mathrm{ev}}_{0}\sqcup Q^{\mathrm{odd}}_{0}
into even and odd parts and decomposition of arrows
Q_{1}=Q^{+}_{1}\sqcup Q^{-}_{1}
such that the condition
(\star)
in Example
3.2
is satisfied. We take
\mathsf{A}=(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{3}\subset T_{W}
such that the framing vector space decomposes into eigenspaces as

W=a_{1}W^{(1)}+a_{2}W^{(2)}+a_{3}W^{(3)}
.
Choosing the partial polarization (
3.2
), we can write down the index bundle for the chamber corresponding to the weight decomposition
W=aW^{\prime}+bW^{\prime\prime}
such that
b/a
is attracting

\mathsf{ind}=\sum_{i,j\in Q_{0}}\mathsf{D}_{ij}\mathrm{Hom}\bigl(\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{i},\mathcal{W}^{\prime\prime}_{j}\bigr)+(\mathsf{Q}_{ij}-\mathsf{P}_{ij})\mathrm{Hom}\bigl(\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{i},\mathcal{V}^{\prime\prime}_{j}\bigr),

where
\mathsf{Q}_{ij}
is the
weighted
adjacency matrix of
Q
defined by

\mathsf{Q}_{ij}=\#\bigl(a\in Q_{1}^{+}:h(a)=j,\,t(a)=i\bigr)-\#(a\in Q_{1}^{-}:h(a)=j,t(a)=i),

and
\mathsf{D}_{ij}
is the diagonal matrix such that

\mathsf{D}_{ii}=\begin{cases}\hphantom{-}1,&i\notin Q^{\mathrm{ev},-}_{0},\\
-1,&i\in Q^{\mathrm{ev},-}_{0},\end{cases}

and
\mathsf{P}_{ij}
is the diagonal matrix such that

\mathsf{P}_{ii}=\begin{cases}\hphantom{-}1,&i\in Q^{\mathrm{ev},+}_{0},\\
-1,&i\in Q^{\mathrm{ev},-}_{0},\\
\hphantom{-}0,&i\in Q^{\mathrm{odd}}_{0}.\end{cases}

Define the quiver
R
-matrix

\mathbf{R}^{Q}(\bm{z}):=\mathbf{R}_{\mathrm{wall}}\bigl(\bm{z}+\hbar\bigl(\mathsf{P}-\mathsf{Q}^{\mathrm{t}}\bigr)\mathbf{v}\bigr)
,
where
\bm{z}:=\{z_{i}\}_{i\in Q_{0}}
are the equivariant parameters corresponding to the ample line bundles
(\det\mathcal{V}_{i})^{-1}
. Let
\bm{\mu}:=\mathsf{D}\mathbf{w}-\mathsf{C}\mathbf{v}
, where

\displaystyle\mathsf{C}=2\mathsf{P}-\mathsf{Q}-\mathsf{Q}^{\mathrm{t}},

(3.16)

then (
3.15
) is specialized to

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}^{Q}_{12}(\bm{z})\mathbf{R}^{Q}_{13}\bigl(\bm{z}-\hbar\bm{\mu}^{(2)}\bigr)\mathbf{R}^{Q}_{23}(\bm{z})=\mathbf{R}^{Q}_{23}\bigl(\bm{z}-\hbar\bm{\mu}^{(1)}\bigr)\mathbf{R}^{Q}_{13}(\bm{z})\mathbf{R}^{Q}_{12}\bigl(\bm{z}-\hbar\bm{\mu}^{(3)}\bigr).

(3.17)

This kind of equation is called the dynamical Yang–Baxter equations and was first studied by Felder
[
51
,
52
]
.

Example 3.8

(
\mathfrak{sl}(1|1)
)
.

Consider the resolved determinantal variety case (see Example
3.7
), namely a quiver
Q
with one odd node, i.e.,
Q_{0}=Q^{\mathrm{odd}}_{0}=\{1\}
. We choose stability condition
{\zeta=\zeta_{-}}
and partial polarization (
3.2
). We choose the framing dimensions
\mathbf{w}^{(1)}=\mathbf{w}^{(2)}=\mathbf{w}^{(3)}=1
, then
\mathcal{M}^{\zeta}\bigl(\mathbf{w}^{(1)}\bigr)
is disjoint union of two points, and

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{\zeta}\bigl(\mathbf{w}^{(1)}+\mathbf{w}^{(2)}\bigr)=\mathrm{pt}\sqcup\mathrm{Tot}\bigl(\mathscr{O}(-1)^{\oplus 2}\rightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}\bigr)\sqcup\mathbb{C}^{4}.

Let us label the basis for the elliptic cohomology
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{T}}\bigl(\mathcal{M}^{\zeta}\bigl(\mathbf{w}^{(1)}\bigr)\bigr)
, which is a free module of rank two over
\mathscr{E}_{x}\times\mathscr{E}_{\hbar}\times\mathscr{E}_{z}
, by
v_{0}=\bigl[\mathcal{M}^{\zeta}(0,1)\bigr]
and
v_{1}=\bigl[\mathcal{M}^{\zeta}(1,1)\bigr]
,
then we can write down the quiver
R
-matrix in the basis
\{v_{0}\otimes v_{0},v_{1}\otimes v_{0},v_{0}\otimes v_{1},v_{1}\otimes v_{1}\}

\mathbf{R}^{Q}(u,z)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c|cc|c}1&0&0&0\\
\hline\cr 0&\frac{\vartheta(z)\vartheta(z\hbar^{-2})\vartheta(u)}{\vartheta\bigl(z\hbar^{-1}\bigr)^{2}\vartheta\bigl(u\hbar^{-1}\bigr)}&\frac{\vartheta(\hbar)\vartheta(uz\hbar^{-1})}{\vartheta\bigl(z\hbar^{-1}\bigr)\vartheta\bigl(u^{-1}\hbar\bigr)}&0\\[6.0pt]
0&\frac{\vartheta(\hbar)\vartheta(zu^{-1}\hbar^{-1})}{\vartheta\bigl(z\hbar^{-1}\bigr)\vartheta\bigl(u^{-1}\hbar\bigr)}&\frac{\vartheta(u)}{\vartheta\bigl(u\hbar^{-1}\bigr)}&0\\[4.0pt]
\hline\cr 0&0&0&\frac{\vartheta(u\hbar)}{\vartheta\bigl(u^{-1}\hbar\bigr)}\end{array}\right),

(3.18)

where
u=x_{2}/x_{1}
is the ratio between equivariant weights in the decomposition
W=x_{1}W^{\prime}+x_{2}W^{\prime\prime}
. In this case
\mathsf{C}=0
, which is the Cartan matrix for
\mathfrak{sl}(1|1)
, and
\bm{\mu}(v_{0})=\bm{\mu}(v_{1})=1
, so the dynamical Yang–Baxter equation (
3.17
) reads

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}^{Q}_{12}(u,z)\mathbf{R}^{Q}_{13}(uw,z/\hbar)\mathbf{R}^{Q}_{23}(w,z)=\mathbf{R}^{Q}_{23}(w,z/\hbar)\mathbf{R}^{Q}_{13}(uw,z)\mathbf{R}^{Q}_{12}(u,z/\hbar),

(3.19)

which is the dynamical Yang–Baxter equation for
\mathfrak{sl}(1|1)
in the fundamental representation.

Example 3.9

(
\mathfrak{sl}(n|1)
)
.

Next, we consider a generalization to the above example, namely the quiver in Example
2.3
. Let
Q
be an
A_{n}
quiver with
Q^{\mathrm{ev}}_{0}=\{1,\dots,n-1\}
and
Q^{\mathrm{odd}}_{0}=\{n\}
, and the orientation is such that
h(a)<t(a)
for all arrows
a\in Q_{1}
. We take
Q_{1}=Q_{1}^{+}
. We choose stability condition
\zeta=\zeta_{-}
and the partial polarization (
3.2
). We choose framing dimensions
{\mathbf{w}^{(1)}=\mathbf{w}^{(2)}=\mathbf{w}^{(3)}=(1,0,\dots,0)}
, then
\mathcal{M}^{\zeta}\bigl(\mathbf{w}^{(1)}\bigr)
is the disjoint union of
n+1
points which is labeled by integers
\alpha\in\{0,1,\dots,n\}
so that the gauge dimension vector of the corresponding quiver variety is

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{\alpha}=(\overbrace{\underbrace{1,\dots,1}_{\alpha},0,\dots,0}^{n}).

Using the above notation, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{\zeta}\bigl(\mathbf{v}_{\alpha}+\mathbf{v}_{\beta},\mathbf{w}^{(1)}+\mathbf{w}^{(2)}\bigr)=\begin{cases}\mathrm{pt},&\alpha=\beta<n,\\
T^{*}\mathbb{P}^{1},&\alpha<\beta<n\text{ or }\beta<\alpha<n,\\
\mathrm{Tot}\bigl(\mathscr{O}(-1)^{\oplus 2}\rightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}\bigr),&\alpha<\beta=n\text{ or }\beta<\alpha=n,\\
\mathbb{C}^{4},&\alpha=\beta=n.\end{cases}

(3.20)

Let us label the basis for the elliptic cohomology
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{T}}\bigl(\mathcal{M}^{\zeta}\bigl(\mathbf{w}^{(1)}\bigr)\bigr)
, which is a free module of rank
{n+1}
over
\mathscr{E}_{x}\times\mathscr{E}_{\hbar}\times\mathscr{E}_{\bm{z}}
, by
v_{\alpha}=\bigl[\mathcal{M}^{\zeta}\bigl(\mathbf{v}_{\alpha},\mathbf{w}^{(1)}\bigr)\bigr]
. Here the Kähler parameter
{\bm{z}=(z_{i})_{i=1}^{n}}
corresponds to the ample line bundles
\bigl(\det\mathcal{V}_{i}^{\vee}\bigr)_{i=1}^{n}
, where
\mathcal{V}_{i}
is the tautological bundle corresponding to the
i
-th node.

For the quiver
Q
, we have the Cartan matrix

\mathsf{C}_{i,j}=2\delta_{i,j}(1-\delta_{i,n})-\delta_{i+1,j}-\delta_{i-1,j},\qquad 1\leq i,j\leq n,

which is the symmetric Cartan matrix of
\mathfrak{sl}(n|1)

[
55
]
. The weights of
v_{\alpha}
are

\displaystyle\bm{\mu}(v_{\alpha})=\mathbf{w}^{(1)}-\mathsf{C}\mathbf{v}_{\alpha}=\begin{cases}(1,0,\dots,0),&\alpha=0,\\
(\underbrace{0,\dots,0}_{\alpha-1},-1,1,0,\dots,0),&0<\alpha<n,\\
(0,\dots,0,1),&\alpha=n,\end{cases}

(3.21)

which is aligned with the weights for the fundamental representation of
\mathfrak{sl}(n|1)
. Moreover, the Kähler parameter shifts between the wall
R
-matrix and the quiver
R
-matrix is

\bigl(\mathsf{P}-\mathsf{Q}^{\mathrm{t}}\bigr)\mathbf{v}_{\alpha}=\begin{cases}(\underbrace{0,\dots,0}_{\alpha-1},1,0,\dots,0),&0<\alpha<n,\\
(0,\dots,0),&\alpha=0\text{ or }\alpha=n.\end{cases}

Using the known results on the
R
-matrices for all four varieties in (
3.20
), see (
3.18
) and
[
2
, equation (85)]
, we can write down the quiver
R
-matrix in this case. We introduce shorthand notations for some special functions

\displaystyle\begin{split}&A(u,z)=\frac{\vartheta(z\hbar)\vartheta\bigl(z\hbar^{-1}\bigr)\vartheta(u)}{\vartheta(z)^{2}\vartheta\bigl(u\hbar^{-1}\bigr)},\qquad B(u,z)=\frac{\vartheta(\hbar)\vartheta(uz)}{\vartheta(z)\vartheta\bigl(u^{-1}\hbar\bigr)},\qquad C(u)=\frac{\vartheta(u)}{\vartheta\bigl(u\hbar^{-1}\bigr)},\\
&D(u)=\frac{\vartheta(u\hbar)}{\vartheta\bigl(u^{-1}\hbar\bigr)},\end{split}

where we have suppressed the
\hbar
-dependence. Then the quiver
R
-matrix in this case is the following:

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}^{Q}(u,\bm{z})(v_{\alpha}\otimes v_{\beta})

\displaystyle\qquad=\begin{cases}v_{\alpha}\otimes v_{\beta},&\alpha=\beta<n,\\
D(u)v_{\alpha}\otimes v_{\beta},&\alpha=\beta=n,\\
C(u)v_{\alpha}\otimes v_{\beta}+B\bigl(u,\hbar^{-\delta_{\beta,n}}\prod_{i=\alpha+1}^{\beta}z_{i}\bigr)v_{\beta}\otimes v_{\alpha},&\alpha<\beta,\\
A\bigl(u,\hbar^{-\delta_{\alpha,n}}\prod_{i=\beta+1}^{\alpha}z_{i}\bigr)v_{\alpha}\otimes v_{\beta}+B\bigl(u,\hbar^{\delta_{\alpha,n}}\prod_{i=\beta+1}^{\alpha}z_{i}^{-1}\bigr)v_{\beta}\otimes v_{\alpha},&\beta<\alpha.\end{cases}

\mathbf{R}^{Q}
satisfies the dynamical Yang–Baxter equation (
3.17
) for
\bm{\mu}
given by weights for the fundamental representation of
\mathfrak{sl}(n|1)
(
3.21
).

For quivers of finite or affine type A, we make the following observation. In addition to the condition
(\star)
in Example
3.2
, we also require that

(\star\star)

if the valency at an odd node is two then the arrows connected to it are from different groups, i.e., exactly one of the arrows is from
Q^{+}_{1}
and the other is from
Q^{-}_{1}
.

For simplicity, we consider an
\widehat{\mathrm{A}}_{m+n-1}
quiver
Q
and label the nodes by
Q_{0}=\mathbb{Z}/(m+n)\mathbb{Z}
. We choose the orientation
Q_{1}=\{i-1\rightarrow i\colon i\in\mathbb{Z}/(m+n)\mathbb{Z}\}
, and choose a splitting
Q_{1}=Q_{1}^{+}\sqcup Q_{1}^{-}
such that
|Q_{1}^{+}|=m
and
|Q_{1}^{-}|=n
. Define

c_{i}=\begin{cases}+1,&\text{if }(i-1\rightarrow i)\in Q_{1}^{+},\\
-1,&\text{if }(i-1\rightarrow i)\in Q_{1}^{-}.\end{cases}

Then the condition
(\star)
is equivalent to
c_{i}=c_{i+1}
for all
i\in Q^{\mathrm{ev}}_{0}
, and the condition
(\star\star)
is equivalent to
c_{i}=-c_{i+1}
for all
i\in Q^{\mathrm{odd}}_{0}
. The Cartan matrix (
3.16
) reads

\mathsf{C}_{i,j}=(c_{i}+c_{i+1})\delta_{i,j}-c_{i}\delta_{i,j+1}-c_{j}\delta_{i+1,j}.

\mathsf{C}_{i,j}
is exactly the symmetric Cartan matrix of the Kac–Dynkin diagram for
\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}(m|n)
associated to the quiver
Q
. Moreover, the quiver
R
-matrix
\mathbf{R}^{Q}
satisfies the dynamical Yang–Baxter equation (
3.17
) for
\bm{\mu}
given by weights in a certain highest-weight module of
\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}(m|n)
.

3.9
K-theory and cohomology limit

In the
q\rightarrow 0
limit, the elliptic curve
\mathbb{E}=\mathbb{C}^{\times}/q^{\mathbb{Z}}
degenerates to a nodal compactification of
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
, and the elliptic cohomology
\mathrm{Ell}_{\mathsf{T}}(\mathsf{X})
degenerates to the K-theory
K_{\mathsf{T}}(\mathsf{X})\otimes\mathbb{C}
, such that sections of line bundles on
\mathrm{Ell}_{\mathsf{T}}(\mathsf{X})
becomes sections of line bundles on
K_{\mathsf{T}}(\mathsf{X})\otimes\mathbb{C}
. Strictly speaking, because of half-periodicity property of
\vartheta
-function:
\vartheta\bigl({\rm e}^{2\pi{\rm i}}x\bigr)=-\vartheta(x)
, their
q\rightarrow 0
limit is defined on the double cover of
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
.

We expect that the elliptic stable envelope degenerates to the K-theoretic stable envelope in the
q\rightarrow 0
limit, such that the Kähler parameter
z
degenerates to the slope parameter in the K-theory side. Suppose that

\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ln z\rightarrow\infty\\
\ln q\rightarrow\infty\end{subarray}}\text{Re}\left(-\frac{\ln z}{\ln q}\right)=\mathsf{s}\in\mathrm{Pic}_{\mathsf{T}}(\mathsf{X})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R},

(3.22)

such that the slope
\mathsf{s}
is
generic
, and then define

\mathsf{Stab}^{\mathsf{s}}_{\mathfrak{C}}:=\lim_{q\rightarrow 0}\bigl[(\det\mathrm{Pol}_{\mathsf{X}})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\circ\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C}}\circ(\det\mathrm{Pol}_{\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}})^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigr]\in K_{\mathsf{T}}\bigl(\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}\bigr).

(3.23)

For every connected component
\mathcal{F}\subset\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{Stab}^{\mathsf{s}}_{\mathfrak{C}}([\mathcal{F}])|_{\mathcal{F}}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\operatorname{rk}\mathsf{ind}}\left(\frac{\det N^{-}_{\mathsf{X}/{\mathcal{F}}}}{\det\mathrm{Pol}_{\mathsf{X}}|_{\mathcal{F},\mathrm{moving}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigwedge(N^{-}_{\mathsf{X}/{\mathcal{F}}})^{\vee}

\displaystyle=\bigl(-\sqrt{\hbar}\bigr)^{\operatorname{rk}\mathsf{ind}}(\det\mathrm{Pol}_{\mathsf{X}}|_{\mathcal{F},>0})^{-1}(\det\mathscr{G}_{\mathrm{odd}}|_{\mathcal{F},<0})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\bigwedge(N^{-}_{\mathsf{X}/{\mathcal{F}}})^{\vee}.

Moreover,
\mathsf{Stab}^{\mathsf{s}}_{\mathfrak{C}}
is supported on
\mathsf{Attr}^{f}_{\mathfrak{C}}
, i.e., if
\mathcal{F}_{2}\npreceq\mathcal{F}_{1}
then
\mathsf{Stab}^{\mathsf{s}}_{\mathfrak{C}}([\mathcal{F}_{1}])|_{\mathcal{F}_{2}}=0
. We expect that
\mathsf{Stab}^{\mathsf{s}}_{\mathfrak{C}}
is the K-theoretic stable envelope with slope
\mathsf{s}
for the chamber
\mathfrak{C}
. In other words,
\mathsf{Stab}^{\mathsf{s}}_{\mathfrak{C}}
also satisfies the degree condition, which is checked in the following.

For a connected component
\mathcal{F}
of
\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}
, define

\Delta_{\mathcal{F}}:=\text{Convex hull}\left(\mathrm{Supp}_{\mathsf{A}}\mathsf{Stab}^{\mathsf{s}}_{\mathfrak{C}}([\mathcal{F}])|_{\mathcal{F}}\right),

where
\mathrm{Supp}_{\mathsf{A}}
of a
K_{\mathsf{T}}(\mathcal{F})
class is the set of
\mathsf{A}
-weights that appears in that class.

Proposition 3.5
.

Let
\mathsf{X}
be a quotient of Hamiltonian reduction
(
2.1
)
, assume moreover that either

•

the gauge group is abelian, i.e., the situation in Example
2.1
, or

•

\mathsf{X}
is constructed from the quiver representation, i.e., the situation in Section
2.2
,

then for every pair of connected components
\mathcal{F}_{2}\preceq\mathcal{F}_{1}
in
\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}
with respect to the chamber
\mathfrak{C}
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Supp}_{\mathsf{A}}\mathsf{Stab}^{\mathsf{s}}_{\mathfrak{C}}([\mathcal{F}_{1}])|_{\mathcal{F}_{2}}\subset\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{2}}+\mathsf{A}\text{-weight of }\mathsf{s}|_{\mathcal{F}_{2}}-\mathsf{A}\text{-weight of }\mathsf{s}|_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}.

(3.24)

Proof.

Suppose that
\mathsf{X}=\mu_{\mathrm{ev}}^{-1}(0)^{\zeta-ss}/\!\!/G
, where
G=G_{\mathrm{ev}}\times G_{\mathrm{odd}}
acts on the representation
\mathcal{R}
with moment map
\mu\colon\mathcal{R}\oplus\mathcal{R}^{\vee}\rightarrow\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}
and
\mu_{\mathrm{ev}}
is the composition of
\mu
with projection
{\mathrm{pr}_{\mathrm{ev}}\colon\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}\rightarrow\mathfrak{g}_{\mathrm{ev}}^{\vee}}
, and
\zeta
is a generic stability such that
\mu_{\mathrm{ev}}^{-1}(0)^{\zeta-ss}=\mu_{\mathrm{ev}}^{-1}(0)^{\zeta-s}
. As we have assumed in the setup of Section
2.1
,
\mu_{\mathrm{ev}}^{-1}(0)^{\zeta-ss}
is smooth with free
G
action.

Step 1.
Assume that
G
is abelian, then according to our previous discussions in Example
3.6
, the equivariant elliptic cohomology of
\mathsf{X}
is isomorphic to that of the central fiber
{\mathsf{X}_{0}=\mu^{-1}(0)^{\zeta-ss}/\!\!/G}
:
\mathrm{Ell}_{\mathsf{T}}(\mathsf{X})\cong\mathrm{Ell}_{\mathsf{T}}(\mathsf{X}_{0})
, and elliptic stable envelopes of
\mathsf{X}
and
\mathsf{X}_{0}
are identified via this isomorphism.
\mathsf{X}_{0}
is a hypertoric variety, thus the result follows from
[
2
, Proposition 4.2]
.

Step 2.
If
G
is nonabelian, then we shall utilize the abelianization (
3.11
). It suffices to prove in the special case when
\mathsf{A}=\mathbb{C}^{\times}
, by the same argument of
[
2
, Proposition 4.2]
. By step 1, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Supp}_{\mathsf{A}}\mathsf{Stab}^{\mathsf{s}}_{S}\bigl(\bigl[\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{1}\bigr]\bigr)|_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{2}}\subset\Delta_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{2}}+\mathsf{A}\text{-weight of }\mathsf{s}|_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{2}}-\mathsf{A}\text{-weight of }\mathsf{s}|_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{1}}.

The abelianization gives

\displaystyle\mathsf{Stab}^{\mathsf{s}}([\mathcal{F}_{1}])|_{\mathcal{F}_{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\bigl(\det\mathscr{N}_{f}^{\vee}|_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{2}}\bigr)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathsf{Stab}^{\mathsf{s}}_{S}\bigl(\bigl[\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{1}\bigr]\bigr)|_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{2}}}{\bigwedge((N_{\mathsf{j}_{-}}|_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{2}})_{\mathrm{moving}})^{\vee}\cdot\bigwedge((T_{\pi}|_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{2}})_{\mathrm{moving}})^{\vee}}\bigl(\det\mathscr{N}_{f}^{\vee}|_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{1},\mathrm{fixed}}\bigr)^{\frac{1}{2}},

\displaystyle\mathsf{Stab}^{\mathsf{s}}([\mathcal{F}_{2}])|_{\mathcal{F}_{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(\det\mathscr{N}_{f}^{\vee}|_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{2},\mathrm{moving}})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathsf{Stab}^{\mathsf{s}}_{S}\bigl(\bigl[\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{2}\bigr]\bigr)|_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{2}}}{\bigwedge((N_{\mathsf{j}_{-}}|_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{2}})_{\mathrm{moving}})^{\vee}\cdot\bigwedge((T_{\pi}|_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{2}})_{\mathrm{moving}})^{\vee}}.

The first line in the above equation implies that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Supp}_{\mathsf{A}}\mathsf{Stab}^{\mathsf{s}}([\mathcal{F}_{1}])|_{\mathcal{F}_{2}}\cdot\bigwedge((N_{\mathsf{j}_{-}}|_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{2}})_{\mathrm{moving}})^{\vee}\cdot\bigwedge((T_{\pi}|_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{2}})_{\mathrm{moving}})^{\vee}

\displaystyle\qquad\subset\Delta_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{2}}-\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{A}\text{-weight of }\det\mathscr{N}_{f}^{\vee}|_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{2}}+\mathsf{A}\text{-weight of }\mathsf{s}|_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{2}}-\mathsf{A}\text{-weight of }\mathsf{s}|_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{1}},

and the second line implies that

\Delta_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{2}}=\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{2}}\cdot\bigwedge((N_{\mathsf{j}_{-}}|_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{2}})_{\mathrm{moving}})^{\vee}\cdot\bigwedge((T_{\pi}|_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{2}})_{\mathrm{moving}})^{\vee}+\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{A}\text{-weight of }\det\mathscr{N}_{f}^{\vee}|_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{2}},

thus we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Supp}_{\mathsf{A}}\mathsf{Stab}^{\mathsf{s}}([\mathcal{F}_{1}])|_{\mathcal{F}_{2}}\subset\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{2}}+\mathsf{A}\text{-weight of }\mathsf{s}|_{\mathcal{F}_{2}}-\mathsf{A}\text{-weight of }\mathsf{s}|_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}.

This finishes the proof.
∎

A further reduction from the K-theory to cohomology can be defined by

\displaystyle\mathrm{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C}}:=\text{lowest cohomological degree term in }\mathrm{ch}\bigl(\mathsf{Stab}^{\mathsf{s}}_{\mathfrak{C}}\bigr),

(3.25)

where
\mathrm{ch}\colon K_{\mathsf{T}}\bigl(\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}\bigr)\rightarrow H_{\mathsf{T}}\bigl(\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}\bigr)
is the
\mathsf{T}
-equivariant Chern character map. The composition of reductions
\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C}}\rightarrow\mathsf{Stab}^{\mathsf{s}}_{\mathfrak{C}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C}}
amounts to replacing
\vartheta(x)
by
x
.

For every connected component
\mathcal{F}\subset\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}
,

\mathrm{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C}}([\mathcal{F}])|_{\mathcal{F}}=(-1)^{\operatorname{rk}\mathsf{ind}}e(N^{-}_{\mathsf{X}/{\mathcal{F}}})
,
where
e(\cdot)
is the
\mathsf{T}
-equivariant Euler class. Moreover,
\mathrm{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C}}
is supported on
\mathsf{Attr}^{f}_{\mathfrak{C}}
, i.e., if
{\mathcal{F}_{2}\npreceq\mathcal{F}_{1}}
then
\mathrm{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C}}([\mathcal{F}_{1}])|_{\mathcal{F}_{2}}=0
. We expect that
\mathrm{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C}}
is the cohomological stable envelope for the chamber
\mathfrak{C}
. In other words,
\mathrm{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C}}
also satisfies the degree condition, which is checked in the following.

Proposition 3.6
.

Under the assumption of Proposition
3.5
, then for every pair of connected components
\mathcal{F}_{2}\preceq\mathcal{F}_{1}
in
\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}
with respect to the chamber
\mathfrak{C}
,

\deg_{\mathsf{A}}\mathrm{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C}}([\mathcal{F}_{1}])|_{\mathcal{F}_{2}}<\dim N^{-}_{\mathsf{X}/{\mathcal{F}}_{2}}
,
where
\deg_{\mathsf{A}}
of an element in
H_{\mathsf{T}}\bigl(\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}\bigr)
is the degree in
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{a}]
under the non-canonical splitting
H_{\mathsf{T}}\bigl(\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}\bigr)\cong H_{\mathsf{T}/\mathsf{A}}\bigl(\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}\bigr)\otimes\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{a}]
.
9
9

9

Different choice of splitting does not change the degree in
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{a}]
.

Proof.

The idea is essentially the same as Proposition
3.5
. Namely, the abelian case is the same as hypertoric varieties, which is shown by using the explicit formula
[
2
, equation (56)]
. For the nonabelian case, we use the abelianization and get

\displaystyle\mathrm{Stab}([\mathcal{F}_{1}])|_{\mathcal{F}_{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{Stab}_{S}\bigl(\bigl[\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{1}\bigr]\bigr)|_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{2}}}{e((N_{\mathsf{j}_{-}}|_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{2}})_{\mathrm{moving}})\cdot e((T_{\pi}|_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{2}})_{\mathrm{moving}})}.

Since the abelian stable envelope satisfies

\deg_{\mathsf{A}}\bigl(\mathrm{Stab}_{S}\bigl(\bigl[\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{1}\bigr]\bigr)|_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{2}}\bigr)<\dim N^{-}_{\mathsf{X}_{S}/{\mathcal{F}}_{2,S}}
,
we conclude that

\displaystyle\deg_{\mathsf{A}}\mathrm{Stab}([\mathcal{F}_{1}])|_{\mathcal{F}_{2}}

\displaystyle=\deg_{\mathsf{A}}(\mathrm{Stab}_{S}\bigl(\bigl[\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{1}\bigr]\bigr)|_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{2}})-\dim(N_{\mathsf{j}_{-}}|_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{2}})_{\mathrm{moving}}-\dim(T_{\pi}|_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{2}})_{\mathrm{moving}}

\displaystyle<\dim N^{-}_{\mathsf{X}_{S}/{\mathcal{F}}_{2,S}}-\dim(N_{\mathsf{j}_{-}}|_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{2}})_{\mathrm{moving}}-\dim(T_{\pi}|_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{2}})_{\mathrm{moving}}

\displaystyle=\dim N^{-}_{\mathsf{X}/{\mathcal{F}}_{2}}.

This finishes the proof.
∎

Propositions
3.5
and
3.6
are summarized in the following

Corollary 3.2
.

Under the assumption of Proposition
3.5
,
\mathsf{Stab}^{\mathsf{s}}_{\mathfrak{C}}
is the K-theoretic stable envelope with slope
\mathsf{s}
for the chamber
\mathfrak{C}
, and
\mathrm{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C}}
is the cohomological stable envelope for the chamber
\mathfrak{C}
.

Example 3.10

(Resolved Determinantal Varieties)
.

We apply (
3.23
) to (
3.6
) and obtain the K-theoretic stable envelope for the resolved determinantal variety
10
10

10

Here we use the identities
\lim_{q\rightarrow 0}\vartheta(w)=w^{\frac{1}{2}}-w^{-\frac{1}{2}}
and
\lim_{q\rightarrow 0}\frac{\vartheta(wz)}{\vartheta(z)}=w^{\lfloor\mathsf{s}\rfloor+\frac{1}{2}}
.

\displaystyle\mathsf{Stab}^{\mathsf{s}}_{\mathfrak{C}}([\mathcal{F}_{p}])=\hbar^{\frac{\#(i>p(a))}{2}}\text{Sym}_{S_{N}}\left[\left(\prod_{a=1}^{N}\mathsf{f}_{p(a)}(s_{a},\bm{x},\hbar,\bm{s})\right)\cdot\Biggl(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}a>b\end{subarray}}\hat{\mathbf{a}}\bigl(s_{a}s_{b}^{-1}\bigr)\Biggr)\right],

(3.26)

where
\mathsf{f}_{m}(s,\bm{x},\hbar,\mathsf{s})
is the following function

\mathsf{f}_{m}(s,\bm{x},\hbar,\mathsf{s}):=(sx_{m})^{\lfloor{\mathsf{s}}\rfloor}\prod_{i<m}\bigl(1-s^{-1}x_{i}^{-1}\bigr)\prod_{j>m}\bigl(1-\hbar^{-1}s^{-1}x_{j}^{-1}\bigr),

and
\hat{\mathbf{a}}
is a version of
\hat{A}
-genus

\hat{\mathbf{a}}(w)=\frac{1}{w^{\frac{1}{2}}-w^{-\frac{1}{2}}}
.
A further reduction to cohomology using (
3.25
) gives the cohomological stable envelope

\displaystyle\mathrm{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C}}([\mathcal{F}_{p}])=\text{Sym}_{S_{N}}\left[\left(\prod_{a=1}^{N}\mathrm{f}_{p(a)}(s_{a},\bm{x},\hbar)\right)\cdot\Biggl(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}a>b\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{s_{a}-s_{b}}\Biggr)\right],

(3.27)

where
\mathrm{f}_{m}(s,\bm{x},\hbar)
is the following function

\mathrm{f}_{m}(s,\bm{x},\hbar):=\prod_{i<m}(s+x_{i})\prod_{j>m}(s+x_{j}+\hbar)
.
Note that (
3.27
) equals to
(-1)^{\#(i>p(a))}W^{(10)}_{w_{0},I}(-\bm{s},\bm{x},-\hbar)
, where
W^{(10)}_{w_{0},I}
is the super weight function in
[
117
]
for the longest element
w_{0}\in S_{N}
and
I=w_{0}(\mathrm{image}(p))
. The sign
(-1)^{\#(i>p(a))}
, which equals to
(-1)^{\operatorname{rk}\mathsf{ind}}
, reflects the choice of normalization of the stable envelope in the
loc. cit
. being
{\mathrm{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C}}([\mathcal{F}])|_{\mathcal{F}}=e(N^{-}_{\mathsf{X}/{\mathcal{F}}})}
as opposed to
(-1)^{\operatorname{rk}\mathsf{ind}}e(N^{-}_{\mathsf{X}/{\mathcal{F}}})
, see Axiom A1 in Definition 4.1 of loc. cit.

Part II
The solution to dYBE for
\bm{\mathfrak{sl}(1|1)}

from 3d
\bm{\mathcal{N}=2}
SQCD

4
Stable envelopes and
\bm{R}
-matrix from gauge theory: the setup

In the previous sections, we have defined the elliptic stable envelope for certain non symplectic varieties. Examples of such varieties include Higgs branches of 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
theories. We have characterized the stable envelopes by their formal properties and using these stable envelopes, we defined the
R
-matrix satisfying the dynamical Yang–Baxter equations for superspin chains. In practice, it is rather difficult to construct stable envelopes from their definitions alone. To write down explicit formulas for these elliptic functions, we turn to the 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
theories. In this section, we explain the motivation behind computing certain partition functions of these theories that we argue should give us stable envelopes. Finally, in Section
6
when we actually compute these partition functions, we shall give an a posteriori justification for the computation by showing that the partition functions satisfy the criteria for elliptic stable envelopes set up earlier and that the
R
-matrix satisfies the dynamical Yang–Baxter equations. In fact, results from these computations were already used to define the functional forms of the elliptic
\mathfrak{sl}(1|1)
stable envelopes (
3.6
) and the
R
-matrix (
3.18
) and we checked that they indeed fulfill all the necessary criteria. Let us now go through the details of how we actually computed them.

4.1
The Bethe/Gauge correspondence

The correspondence relates integrable spin chains to supersymmetric quiver gauge theories, for both purely bosonic
[
102
,
103
]
and superspin
[
99
]
chains. One way to introduce this is from a correspondence between Kac–Dynkin diagrams and quivers.

An integrable spin chain is based on a Lie algebra, say
\mathfrak{sl}(m|n)
, which can be characterized by its Kac–Dynkin diagram. For example, the following is a Kac–Dynkin diagram for
\mathfrak{sl}(1|3)
