\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

A hollow circle represents an even simple root and a crossed circle represents an odd simple root. In order to fully characterize a spin chain, in addition to the symmetry, we also need to specify the representations or the spins that appear in the chain and the anisotropy. For simplicity, let us only consider spin chains with highest-weight representations, with the highest-weight being the fundamental weight, then we just need to specify the number of sites in the spin chain. For the anisotropy, we have three choices (see Table
1
), fully isotropic (XXX/rational), partially anisotropic (XXZ/trigonometric/K-theoretic), and fully anisotropic (XYZ/elliptic).

The Bethe/Gauge correspondence relates this data to a quiver such as the following:
11
11

11

In the hierarchy of quivers, the first one is just a Kac–Dynkin diagram (e.g., (
1.8
)), then there’s the framed and doubled quiver (e.g., (
1.7
)), and finally the one in (
4.1
) is a tripled quiver that is traditionally used in physics to define gauge theories with 4 supercharges.

N_{1}
N_{2}
N_{3}
L

(4.1)

Each circular/gauge node corresponds to a node in the Kac–Dynkin diagram – even nodes are distinguished from the odd ones by the presence of a self-loop. The presence of a single framing node attached to the left-most gauge node reflects our choice regarding the spin chain only having the fundamental highest weights. The value
L
of the framing node is the size of the spin chain. The gauge dimensions
N_{1}
,
N_{2}
and
N_{3}
are not part of the data defining the spin chain. Rather, they correspond to a sector of the spin chain with definite magnon numbers. To find the gauge theory dual of the full spin chain we need to consider multiple quivers, varying the gauge dimensions over all possible magnon numbers. The choice of anisotropy is not part of the quiver itself, but it will be reflected in the choice of gauge theory that we shall assign to the quiver. The quiver (
4.1
) is a special case of Example
2.4
with
n=3
.

To quivers like (
4.1
), we can assign supersymmetric gauge theories with 4 supercharges in various dimensions. In the version of the Bethe/Gauge correspondence in which we are interested in this paper, the relation between anisotropy and gauge theory is as follows:

Rational

1d
{\mathcal{N}}=4
(quantum mechanics),

Trigonometric

2d
{\mathcal{N}}=(2,2)
,

Elliptic

3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
.

(4.2)

In the absence of any odd node, the amount of supersymmetry is doubled in all cases. All these theories possess the same classical Higgs branch
\mathcal{M}_{H}
, which is the quiver variety
{\mathcal{M}}^{\zeta}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})
(
2.3
) with
\mathbf{v}=(N_{1},N_{2},N_{3})
and
\mathbf{w}=(L,0,0)
. The stability condition
\zeta
corresponds to the real FI parameter in gauge theories. Supersymmetric ground states of these theories correspond to different cohomologies of the Higgs branch. For quantum mechanics, Witten’s Morse theory arguments
[
144
]
describe the space of ground states as the ordinary equivariant cohomology
H_{\mathsf{A}}(\mathcal{M}_{H})
where
\mathsf{A}
is the maximal torus of the
\SU(L)
flavor symmetry of the quiver. In the 2d and 3d cases the cohomology is replaced by K-theory
K_{\mathsf{A}}(\mathcal{M}_{H})
and elliptic cohomology
\text{Ell}_{\mathsf{A}}(\mathcal{M}_{H})
respectively
[
145
,
146
]
. On the spin chain side, for the rational, trigonometric, and elliptic cases, we have the Yangian
\mathsf{Y}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})
, the quantum affine algebra
\mathscr{U}_{\hbar}(\widehat{\mathfrak{g}})
, and the elliptic dynamical quantum group
E_{\tau,\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})
as the spectrum generating algebras. The Bethe/Gauge correspondence identifies the Bethe eigenstates of the spin chain with the space of supersymmetric vacua of the corresponding gauge theories. Consequently, it conjectures an action of the spectrum generating algebra on the corresponding cohomology
[
53
,
88
,
106
]
.
12
12

12

Since a single gauge theory corresponds to a specific magnon sector, we need to take direct sum of the cohomologies over gauge dimensions to get the full spin chain Hilbert space and the algebra action (cf. the sums over
\mathbf{v}
in Conjecture
1.1
).

There are more choices of gauge theories that we can assign to quivers such as (
4.1
) and find dual gauge theoretic interpretations of spin chain quantities. For example, in the original formulation of the Bethe/Gauge correspondence
[
99
,
102
,
103
]
, for both bosonic and supersymmetric spin chains, the gauge dual of the rational spin chain was presented as a 2d
{\mathcal{N}}=(4,4)
(bosonic spin chain) or an
{\mathcal{N}}=(2,2)
(superspin chain) theory. The quantum mechanics from (
4.2
) is simply the dimensional reduction of these 2d theories. The supersymmetric vacua of these 2d theories correspond to the quantum deformation of the equivariant cohomology. In principle, there should be 3d and 4d theories with 4 supercharges that quantize K-theory
[
72
,
73
,
75
,
106
]
and elliptic cohomology, and whose dimensional reductions correspond to the 2d and 3d theories in (
4.2
). In this paper we shall not be concerned with quantum cohomology, regardless, it is of some interest to look at the 2d dual of the rational spin chains. We consider the example of a
\mathfrak{sl}(1|1)
spin chain in the following.

Example 4.1

(
\mathfrak{sl}(1|1)
spin chains and 2d
{\mathcal{N}}=(2,2)
Gauge theories)
.

(a)

Kac–Dynkin diagram.

N
L
{\mathcal{Q}}
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}

(b)

Gauge theory quiver.

Figure 3
:
\mathfrak{sl}(1|1)
Kac–Dynkin diagram and the corresponding quiver.

Consider a length
L
spin chain whose sites are labeled by
i=1,\dots,L
. At the
i
th site we assign the
\mathfrak{sl}(1|1)
representation with the highest-weight
\bigl(\lambda^{(1)}_{i},\lambda^{(2)}_{i}\bigr)
. We are using the
\mathfrak{gl}(1|1)
notation to write down the weights, i.e.,
\lambda^{(1)}_{i}+\lambda^{(1)}_{i}
is the
\mathfrak{sl}(1|1)
weight of the
i
th site. We also consider complex inhomogeneities
v_{i}
. Excitations containing
N
magnons with rapidities
\sigma^{\mathbb{C}}_{a}
for
a=1,\dots,N
correspond to Bethe eigenstates if the rapidities satisfy the Bethe ansatz equations (BAE)
[
114
]
(also see
[
143
]
)

\prod_{i=1}^{L}\frac{+\sigma^{\mathbb{C}}_{a}+\frac{\mathfrak{i}}{2}\epsilon-v_{i}+\mathfrak{i}\epsilon\lambda_{i}^{(1)}}{-\sigma_{a}^{\mathbb{C}}-\frac{\mathfrak{i}}{2}\epsilon+v_{i}+\mathfrak{i}\epsilon\lambda_{i}^{(2)}}={\rm e}^{\mathfrak{i}\varphi},\qquad a=1,\dots,N,

(4.3)

The inhomogeneities and the phase on the right-hand side are arbitrary auxiliary parameters of the spin chain.
\epsilon
is the quantization parameter of the spin chain.
13
13

13

Usually the quantization parameter in a quantum mechanical system is written as
\hbar
, however in this paper, we are using
\hbar
for the elliptic deformation parameter which is roughly related to
\epsilon
by
\epsilon\sim\ln\hbar
.
Note that the value of
\lambda_{i}^{(1)}-\lambda_{i}^{(2)}
is somewhat ambiguous since it can be changed by shifting the (arbitrary) inhomogeneity
v_{i}
. The
\mathfrak{sl}(1|1)
charge
\lambda^{(1)}_{i}+\lambda^{(2)}_{i}
is invariant under such shifts.

The BAE is the condition of extremizing a potential function called the Yang–Yang function

\displaystyle Y\bigl(\bm{\sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}\bigr)={}

\displaystyle\sum_{a=1}^{N}\sum_{i=1}^{L}\bigl(\sigma^{\mathbb{C}}_{a}-m^{\mathbb{C}}_{i}\bigr)\bigl(\ln\bigl(\sigma^{\mathbb{C}}_{a}-m^{\mathbb{C}}_{i}\bigr)-1\bigr)

\displaystyle+\sum_{a=1}^{N}\sum_{i=1}^{L}\bigl(-\sigma^{\mathbb{C}}_{a}-\widetilde{m}^{\mathbb{C}}_{i}\bigr)\bigl(\ln\bigl(-\sigma^{\mathbb{C}}_{a}-\widetilde{m}^{\mathbb{C}}_{i}\bigr)-1\bigr)-\mathfrak{i}\varphi\sum_{a=1}^{N}\sigma^{\mathbb{C}}_{a},

(4.4)

where we have defined the new parameters

m_{i}^{\mathbb{C}}:=v_{i}-\mathfrak{i}\epsilon\lambda_{i}^{(1)}-\frac{\mathfrak{i}}{2}\epsilon,\qquad\widetilde{m}_{i}^{\mathbb{C}}:=-v_{i}-\mathfrak{i}\epsilon\lambda_{i}^{(2)}+\frac{\mathfrak{i}}{2}\epsilon.

(4.5)

In terms of this function, the BAE becomes:

\exp\bigl(2\pi\mathfrak{i}\frac{\partial Y(\bm{\sigma}^{\mathbb{C}})}{\partial\sigma^{\mathbb{C}}_{a}}\bigr)\!=\!1
,
a\!=\!1,\dots,N
.
The Bethe/Gauge correspondence identifies the Yang–Yang function (
4.3
) as the effective twisted superpotential of the 2d
{\mathcal{N}}=(2,2)
theory defined by the quiver Figure
3(b)
in the presence of twisted masses
[
99
]
. We identify the rapidities
\sigma^{\mathbb{C}}_{a}
with the adjoint scalars in the Cartan part of the
{\mathcal{N}}=(2,2)

\U(N)
vector multiplet. Then we recognize the 1-loop contributions of the fundamental and the anti-fundamental chirals
{\mathcal{Q}}^{i}_{a}
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{a}_{i}
in the first and the second line of (
4.4
)
[
65
,
147
]
. Masses of these chirals are precisely the parameters defined in (
4.5
). We also notice the microscopic linear twisted superpotential as the last term in the second line.

For positive real FI parameter, the 2d theory flows in the infrared to a phase where it is described by a sigma model into its Higgs branch
[
147
]
. The Higgs branch is acted on by
\SU(L)
and the fixed points of the
\U(1)^{L-1}
action correspond to the vacua of the theory. When put on an interval, such as
I\times S^{1}
for some interval
I
, the theory has brane-type boundary conditions supported on the attracting and repelling sets of these fixed points. All this Higgs branch structure can just as well be studied by looking at the dimensionally reduced
{\mathcal{N}}=4
quantum mechanics (from (
4.2
)) on
I
using Morse theory
[
144
]
.

4.2
3d
\bm{{\mathcal{N}}=2}
SQCD and its parameters

The 3d theory assigned to the quiver Figure
3(b)
is the
{\mathcal{N}}=2
SQCD. The theory has a gauge group

G:=\U(N)
with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}:=\mathfrak{u}(N)
.
The flavor symmetry visible in the quiver is
F_{\mathsf{A}}:=\SU(L)
with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{f}_{\mathsf{A}}:=\mathfrak{su}(L)
.
There is an additional
\U(1)_{\hbar}
symmetry with Lie algebra
\mathbb{R}_{\hbar}
. The total flavor symmetry we consider is

F:=\SU(L)\times\U(1)_{\hbar}
,
whose Lie algebra we denote by
\mathfrak{f}
.

Let
\operatorname{tr}A
be the component of the gauge field valued in the center of
\mathfrak{u}(N)
. From this abelian gauge field, we can form a current
\star\operatorname{tr}F
in 3d which is conserved by Bianchi identity. This gives rise to an abelian topological symmetry
\U(1)_{\text{top}}
. It is topological in the sense that only the monopoles and none of the fields in the Lagrangian are coupled to this current. The Lie algebra of
\U(1)_{\text{top}}
is written as
\mathbb{R}_{\text{top}}
.
This global symmetry can be weakly gauged by introducing a background vector multiplet for it. Let
A_{\text{top}}
,
\sigma_{\text{top}}
and
\mathsf{D}_{\text{top}}
be the gauge field, the scalar, and the auxiliary scalar in this vector multiplet. The purely bosonic part of the Lagrangian coupling this multiplet to the topological current is

A_{\text{top}}\wedge\operatorname{tr}F+\sigma_{\text{top}}\operatorname{tr}\mathsf{D}+\mathsf{D}_{\text{top}}\operatorname{tr}\sigma.

(4.6)

The theory has a
\mathfrak{g}
-valued vector multiplet
\sigma
and chiral multiplets
14
14

14

We refer to a multiplet by its scalar component. Their full field content and our conventions about supersymmetry are presented in Section
B.2
.

\displaystyle\bigl({\mathcal{Q}},\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}\bigr)\in{\mathcal{R}}\oplus{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee},\qquad\text{where}\qquad{\mathcal{R}}=\text{Hom}\bigl(\mathbb{C}^{L},\mathbb{C}^{N}\bigr),\qquad{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee}=\text{Hom}\bigl(\mathbb{C}^{N},\mathbb{C}^{L}\bigr).

(4.7)

We also define
\mathbf{M}:={\mathcal{R}}\oplus{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee}
to refer to the space of all the chirals. The chiral multiplets are accompanied by their conjugate anti-chiral multiplets
\bigl(\overline{{\mathcal{Q}}},\overline{\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}}\bigr)\in{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee}\oplus{\mathcal{R}}
. Charges of these multiplets under the groups defined above are as follows

\begin{array}[]{|c|cccc|}\hline\cr&\U(N)&\SU(L)&\U(1)_{\hbar}&\U(1)_{\text{top}}\\
\hline\cr\sigma&\mathbf{ad}&\mathbf{1}&0&0\\
{\mathcal{Q}}&\mathbf{N}&\mathbf{L}^{\vee}&\frac{1}{2}&0\\
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}&\mathbf{N}^{\vee}&\mathbf{L}&\frac{1}{2}&0\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Table 4
:
Charges of 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
multiplets in the quiver gauge theory of Figure
3(b)
.
\mathbf{N}
and
\mathbf{L}
refer to the fundamental representations of
\U(N)
and
\SU(L)
respectively.

There is also a
\U(1)_{R}

R
-symmetry but charges of various multiplets under this symmetry are a bit ambiguous since the symmetry can be mixed with any other
\U(1)
global symmetry. Also, we do not turn on any background for the
R
-symmetry.

We put the 3d theory on a Euclidean space-time manifold
[y_{-},y_{+}]\times\mathbb{E}_{\tau}
.
Here
\mathbb{E}_{\tau}
is the elliptic curve with complex structure
\tau
, defined as the quotient

\mathbb{E}_{\tau}=\mathbb{C}^{\times}/q^{\mathbb{Z}}
,
q:={\rm e}^{2\pi\text{i}\tau}
.
For the global symmetries
\SU(L)\times\U(1)_{\hbar}\times\U(1)_{\text{top}}
we turn on background vector multiplets. The multiplets contain, among other fields, flat connections.
15
15

15

The connections need to be flat to preserve supersymmetry (cf. (
5.5a
)). The full constraints on the background fields follow from the BPS equations of Section
5.1.2
.
These flat connections are parameterized by their holonomies around the non-contractible cycles,
S^{1}_{\text{A}}
and
S^{1}_{\text{B}}
, of
\mathbb{E}_{\tau}
. For example, if
A_{\mathfrak{a}}
is the background connection for the
F_{\mathsf{A}}=\SU(L)
flavor symmetry, then we can compute the
{\mathfrak{a}_{\mathbb{C}}=\mathfrak{su}(L)\otimes\mathbb{C}}
valued periods

a_{\mathfrak{a}}:=\oint_{S^{1}_{\text{B}}}A_{\mathfrak{a}}-\tau\oint_{S^{1}_{\text{A}}}A_{\mathfrak{a}}
.
We can always take the background fields to be Cartan valued

a_{\mathfrak{a}}=\text{diag}((a_{\mathfrak{a}})_{1},\dots,(a_{\mathfrak{a}})_{L}),\qquad(a_{\mathfrak{a}})_{1}+\cdots+(a_{\mathfrak{a}})_{L}=0.

Once we quotient by equivalences due to gauge transformations, these periods are really valued in the torus
a_{\mathfrak{a}}\in\frac{\mathfrak{a}_{\mathbb{C}}}{\Lambda^{\vee}_{\mathfrak{a}}\oplus\tau\Lambda^{\vee}_{\mathfrak{a}}}
,
where
\Lambda^{\vee}_{\mathfrak{a}}
is the coroot lattice of
\mathfrak{a}
. Consequently, the holonomies of this connection around the cycles of the elliptic curve, also called the elliptic equivariant parameters, are valued in the elliptic curve as well

(x_{i},\dots,x_{L}):=\bigl({\rm e}^{2\pi\text{i}\tau(a_{\mathfrak{a}})_{1}},\dots,{\rm e}^{2\pi\text{i}\tau(a_{\mathfrak{a}})_{L}}\bigr)\in\mathbb{E}^{L}_{\tau},\qquad i=1,\dots,L.

The tracelessness condition translates to
x_{1}\cdots x_{L}=1
for the equivariant parameters. Another common term for these parameters is fugacity, which we shall also use.

In a completely analogous fashion, we turn on elliptic fugacities for the flavor symmetries
\U(1)_{\hbar}
,
\U(1)_{\text{top}}
, as well as for the gauge symmetry
\U(N)

\begin{array}[]{|c|c|}\hline\cr\text{Symmetry}&\text{Fugacities}\\
\hline\cr\U(1)_{\hbar}&\hbar\in\mathbb{E}_{\tau}\\
\U(1)_{\text{top}}&z\in\mathbb{E}_{\tau}\\
\U(N)&(s_{1},\dots,s_{N})\in\mathbb{E}^{N}_{\tau}\\
\hline\cr\end{array}.

The values of the flavor fugacities are constant along the interval
[y_{-},y_{+}]
due to BPS equations (
5.6a
). The values of the gauge fugacities along the interval are unconstrained and only their values at the boundaries with fixed holonomy are parameters of the theory. These fugacities are the same equivariant parameters as in Example
3.7
.

In addition to the holonomies, we also turn on
\SU(L)
twisted masses, by giving nonzero VEV to the adjoint scalar in the background
\SU(L)
vector multiplet

\left\langle\sigma_{\mathfrak{a}}\right\rangle=m=\text{diag}(m_{1},\dots,m_{L})
,
as well as
\U(1)_{\text{top}}
twisted mass

\left\langle\sigma_{\text{top}}\right\rangle=\zeta
.
Note that, due to the coupling (
4.6
) between the topological and the dynamical gauge multiplets, the
\zeta
is nothing but the real FI parameter. The absolute values of these mass parameters do not play any role in this paper. However, the relative ordering of their values matter. We fix the sign of the FI parameter once and for all localization computations

\zeta>0
.
For the
\SU(L)
-masses, we shall refer to different chambers at different times. A chamber simply refers to a particular ordering of the masses. For any permutation
\varsigma\colon\{1,\dots,L\}\hookrightarrow\{1,\dots,L\}
we have a chamber

\mathfrak{C}_{\varsigma}=\{(m_{1},\dots,m_{L})\mid m_{\varsigma(1)}<m_{\varsigma(2)}<\cdots<m_{\varsigma(L)}\}\subseteq\mathfrak{a}.

(4.8)

These are the same chambers as in (
3.3
). Two arbitrary but specific chambers defined by
i\mapstochar\rightarrow i
and
i\mapstochar\rightarrow L+1-i
, reintroduced in (
5.41
), will be used for explicit computations of stable envelopes and the
R
-matrix in Section
6
.

Reduction to 2d.
In Section
7
, we shall consider dimensional reduction of the 3d partition functions down to 2d and 1d. The 3d
\rightarrow
2d reduction is done by compactifying the
S^{1}_{\text{B}}
cycle of the elliptic curve. Then the period of the connections along the
S^{1}_{\text{B}}
cycle becomes a real adjoint scalar field in the 2d theory. Combined with the real adjoint scalar already in the 3d vector multiplet, this becomes the complex adjoint scalar of the 2d
{\mathcal{N}}=(2,2)
vector multiplet. For example, for the
F_{\mathsf{A}}
vector multiplet we define the following complex scalar

\sigma_{\mathfrak{a}}^{\mathbb{C}}:=\sigma_{\mathfrak{a}}+\text{i}\mathop{\text{\Large$\oint_{\text{\normalsize$\scriptstyle S^{1}_{\text{B}}$}}$}}\nolimits A_{\mathfrak{a}}.

(4.9)

We use
(\sigma_{\mathfrak{g}})^{\mathbb{C}}
,
(\sigma_{\hbar})^{\mathbb{C}}
, and
\zeta^{\mathbb{C}}
for the similarly defined complex adjoint scalars in the dynamical vector multiplet, in the
\U(1)_{\hbar}
vector multiplet, and the complex FI parameter in 2d respectively. A further reduction on
S^{1}_{\text{A}}
gives an
\mathcal{N}=4
quantum mechanics.

4.3
Branes and the Bethe/Gauge correspondence

In this section, we are going to look at the brane construction of the 3d quiver theory and relations between certain string dualities and the Bethe/Gauge correspondence. The goal is to find hints regarding which gauge theoretic quantities may correspond to stable envelopes and the
R
-matrix. The brane constructions will serve merely as a motivation to set up the purely gauge theoretic computations of the future sections, we will be rather schematic in the present section.

Gauge theories with 4 supercharges have standard brane constructions in terms of D-branes suspended between rotated NS5s
[
37
,
48
,
49
]
. We consider a 10d space-time
\mathbb{R}_{y}\times\mathbb{R}_{1}\times\mathbb{E}_{\tau}\times\mathbb{R}_{2}\times\mathbb{R}_{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+\epsilon}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}_{-\epsilon}
. Here
\mathbb{R}_{y}
is a real direction parameterized by the coordinate
y
and
\mathbb{R}_{1}
,
\mathbb{R}_{2}
,
\mathbb{R}_{3}
are three real directions parameterized by some unspecified coordinates.
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\pm\epsilon}
are two
\Omega
-deformed planes,
\epsilon
being the deformation parameter. The 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
SQCD can be seen as the world-volume theory of a stack of
N
D3 branes in this background. The complete brane configuration is in Table
5
, and we draw a cartoon of the branes in Figure
4
.

\begin{array}[]{|cc|ccccccc|}\hline\cr\text{No.}&&\mathbb{R}_{y}&\mathbb{R}_{1}&\mathbb{E}_{\tau}&\mathbb{R}_{2}&\mathbb{R}_{3}&\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+\epsilon}&\mathbb{R}^{2}_{-\epsilon}\\

\U(1)_{\hbar}

), the two parameters being roughly related by

.

T-dualizing the elliptic curve and the
\mathbb{R}_{1}
direction takes us to a type IIA configuration with
N
D2 branes suspended between rotated NS5 branes, with
L
D4 branes about. This is precisely the configuration studied in
[
66
]
. In this duality frame, the world-volume theory of the D2 branes is the 2d
{\mathcal{N}}=(2,2)
theory from Example
4.1
. If we do not T-dualize
\mathbb{R}_{1}
, then we find D1 branes suspended between NS5s and additional D3 branes. The D1 branes are described the quantum mechanics from (
4.2
) for which the D3 branes provide flavor symmetry. The main result of
[
66
]
was to relate this setup to 4d Chern--Simons theory.
16
16

16

Also see
[
33
]
for relations between
\Omega
-deformed D5s and bosonic 4d Chern–Simons theory.
By a further S-duality we can convert the D1s, NS5s, and D3s to F1s, D5s, and D3s respectively. The
\Omega
-deformed D5s give rise to a
\mathfrak{gl}(1|1)
4d holomorphic-topological Chern–Simons theory on
\mathbb{R}_{y}\times\mathbb{R}_{1}\times\mathbb{E}_{\tau}
where
{\mathbb{R}_{y}\times\mathbb{R}_{1}}
is the topological plane and the elliptic curve provides the holomorphic direction. The D3 branes create line operators in this theory
[
66
,
67
]
. From
[
31
]
we know that crossing of line operators in the topological plane of 4d Chern–Simons creates
R
-matrix for certain spin chains. In the starting configuration of Table
5
this crossing corresponds to changing the
\mathbb{R}_{1}
-positions of two D5 branes as we move along in the
\mathbb{R}_{y}
direction, ultimately swapping the two (see Figure
5
).

\left|i\right\rangle
\left|j\right\rangle
\left\langle k\right|
\left\langle l\right|
Line operators in 4d CS
or D5 branes from Table
5
\mathbb{R}_{y}
\mathbb{R}_{1}

Figure 5
:
Line operators crossing in the topological plane of 4d Chern–Simons, computing the matrix element
\left\langle k\right|\otimes\left\langle l\right|R\left|i\right\rangle\otimes\left|j\right\rangle
of an
R
-matrix. This translates to bending D5 branes (from Table
5
) in the
\mathbb{R}_{y}\times\mathbb{R}_{1}
plane.

The incoming and outgoing asymptotic states correspond to states in the spin chain, or according to the Bethe/Gauge philosophy, vacua of the D3 brane theory. Without further analysis of the string background, it is unclear how exactly to bend branes while preserving supersymmetry or how to specify incoming and outgoing states. However, there is a natural supersymmetric process in the field theory language that accomplishes something analogous. The locations of the D5 branes in the
\mathbb{R}_{1}
direction are real twisted masses for the chiral multiplets of the SQCD that lives on the D3 branes. Swapping the
\mathbb{R}_{1}
-locations,
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
, of two D5 branes then correspond to giving
y
-dependent masses
m_{1}(y)
and
m_{2}(y)
such that

\lim_{y\rightarrow-\infty}(m_{1}(y),m_{2}(y))=(m_{1},m_{2}),\qquad\lim_{y\rightarrow+\infty}(m_{1}(y),m_{2}(y))=(m_{2},m_{1}).

(4.10)

Such position-dependent masses can be made supersymmetric, creating a mass Janus interface
[
4
,
19
,
42
,
61
]
. The interface interpolates between two distinct chambers, as defined in (
4.8
). In Section
5
, we shall be more specific and identify suitable boundary conditions in the 3d theory, in any chamber, that mimics supersymmetric vacua, and define mass Janus interpolating between them.

4.4
Gauge-theoretic definition of elliptic stable envelope

The gauge-theoretic definition of elliptic stable envelopes as Janus partition functions first appeared in
[
24
,
40
]
. They define the Janus interfaces for 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
supersymmetry but provide boundary conditions for 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=4
vacua, which give elliptic stable envelopes of 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=4
Higgs branches. We take our previous discussion on the brane construction and in particular the position dependent masses (
4.10
) as motivations to study Janus interfaces. In this section, we briefly review the definition of the elliptic stable envelopes as Janus partition functions, and in Section
5
, we generalize the boundary conditions to correspond to the vacua of 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
theories computing the elliptic stable envelopes for the 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
Higgs branches.

Our starting point here is the 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
SQCD on
[y_{-},y_{+}]\times\mathbb{E}_{\tau}
. For the moment, replace the interval
[y_{-},y_{+}]
with the infinite line
(-\infty,+\infty)
. Suppose, for any chamber
\mathfrak{C}
, we have a set of asymptotic boundary conditions
\{p\}
for the 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
SQCD corresponding to its Higgs branch vacua.
17
17

17

In the presence of twisted masses, the classical Higgs branch of 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
SQCD retracts down to the fixed point set of the flavor torus action. This set is a disjoint union of connected components. Here
p
labels a connected component, and by the boundary condition labeled by
p
we roughly refer to a brane-type boundary supported on the attracting/repelling set of
p
. We shall describe in detail the fixed points, their attracting sets and the corresponding boundary conditions in Sections
5.2.2
,
5.2.3
and
5.3
, respectively.
We can construct a Janus interface interpolating between a vacuum
p_{-}
in a chamber
\mathfrak{C}_{-}
at infinite past and a vacuum
p_{+}
in a chamber
\mathfrak{C}_{+}
at infinite future. Then, our previous discussion on branes suggests that the expectation value of this interface with
p_{-}
and
p_{+}
boundary conditions at infinite past and future should give us a matrix element of an
R
-matrix

\mathbf{R}_{\mathfrak{C}_{+}\leftarrow\mathfrak{C}_{-}}(p_{+},p_{-})=\left\langle p_{+}\right|{\mathcal{J}}(m_{\mathfrak{C}_{-}},m_{\mathfrak{C}_{+}})\left|p_{-}\right\rangle,

where
m_{\mathfrak{C}_{-}}
and
m_{\mathfrak{C}_{+}}
are two generic masses from the chambers
\mathfrak{C}_{-}
and
\mathfrak{C}_{+}
respectively, and
{\mathcal{J}}
is the Janus interface interpolating between these two masses. A similar expression for the
R
-matrix in terms of Janus interfaces was also used in
[
23
]
in the context of bosonic rational spin chains.

As already mentioned in the introduction (equations (
1.3
), (
1.4
) and (
1.5
)), a more fundamental quantity than the
R
-matrix is the stable envelope. While the
R
-matrix corresponds to changing masses across chambers, the stable envelope corresponds to changing masses from some nonzero value to zero. The full
R
-matrix is then a composition of a stable envelope and an inverse stable envelope (
3.12
), the first one taking the nonzero masses from one chamber to zero, the second one taking the zero masses to a nonzero value in a different chamber. In the gauge theory setup, a stable envelope then corresponds to a Janus interface interpolating between nonzero and zero masses. We expect that due to the presence of nonzero equivariant elliptic parameters, even in the massless side of the interface we can choose a basis of boundary conditions labeled by the same
p
that labels the massive boundaries.
18
18

18

Note that, if we go down in dimension then the periods of the background connections become part of the twisted masses (
4.9
).
Then the stable envelope corresponds to the Janus partition function

\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C}}(p_{+},p_{-})=\left\langle p_{+}\right|{\mathcal{J}}(m_{\mathfrak{C}},0)\left|p_{-}\right\rangle.

We want to compute these partition functions using supersymmetric localization. In practice, localization on non-compact manifolds are technically subtle and it is more convenient to replace the infinite line with the finite interval
[y_{-},y_{+}]
. Then we need to find boundary conditions for the boundaries at finite distances that are cohomologous to the asymptotic vacuum boundaries with respect to our choice of localizing supercharge. We describe our choice of supercharge in Appendix
B.3
and in Section
5
, we shall identify the vacua and various boundary conditions equivalent to these vacua. For the massive boundary, we shall construct two types of boundary conditions, the so-called thimbles
\mathscr{D}_{\mathfrak{C}}(p)
(see Section
5.3.1
) and the enriched Neumann
\mathscr{N}_{\mathfrak{C}}(p)
(see Section
5.3.2
). For the massless boundary, we shall construct one type of boundary conditions depending on a choice of polarization
\mathbb{L}(p)
of the space of chirals
\mathscr{B}_{\mathbb{L}(p)}(p)
(see Section
5.3.3
). All these boundary conditions are labeled by the connected components of the (flavor) torus fixed-point set of the Higgs branch. We shall compute the matrix elements of the stable envelopes as the partition function

\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C}}(p_{+},p_{-})=\left\langle\mathscr{B}_{\mathbb{L}(p_{+})}(p_{+})\right|{\mathcal{J}}(m_{\mathfrak{C}},0)\left|\mathscr{N}_{\mathfrak{C}}(p_{-})\right\rangle.

(4.11)

Here we have the boundary conditions
\mathscr{N}_{\mathfrak{C}}(p_{-})
and
\mathscr{B}_{\mathbb{L}(p_{+})}(p_{+})
at the two endpoints of the finite interval
[y_{-},y_{+}]
and a Janus interface interpolating between them. By keeping the masses constant throughout most of the interval and varying them rapidly near
y=0
we can think of the Janus interface as being located at
y=0
. The setup of this computation is depicted in Figure
6
.

y_{-}
|\mathscr{N}_{\mathfrak{C}}(p_{-})\rangle
y=0
\mathcal{J}(m_{\mathfrak{C}},0)
y_{+}
\langle\mathscr{B}_{\mathbb{L}(p_{+}),p_{+}}|
m_{\mathfrak{C}}\neq 0
m=0

Figure 6
:
The physical setup for the computation of elliptic stable envelopes. We start with an enriched Neumann boundary condition
\mathscr{N}_{\mathfrak{C}}(p_{-})
at the past boundary
y_{-}
. This boundary imitates the choice of the vacuum
p_{-}
at
-\infty
. We insert a mass-Janus interface
\mathcal{J}(m_{\mathfrak{C}},0)
at
{y=0}
whose role is to change the real masses from nonzero to zero as we cross over to
y>0
. We then choose an exceptional Dirichlet boundary condition
\mathscr{B}_{\mathbb{L}(p_{+}),p_{+}}
at
y_{+}
. This future state is associated with the choice of a vacuum
p_{+}
at
+\infty
and a polarization
\mathbb{L}(p_{+})
of the space of the chirals. The interval partition function of this configuration gives the matrix elements (
4.11
) of elliptic stable envelopes.

Our choice of localizing supercharge makes the 3d theory topological in the
y
-direction (
B.10
), and therefore the length of the interval
[y_{-},y_{+}]
will not be a parameter of the theory. In fact, we shall find an effective description of the theory as a 2d
{\mathcal{N}}=(0,2)
theory on
\mathbb{E}_{\tau}
– the amount of supersymmetry reduced to half by the half BPS boundaries and interfaces. The 3d interval partition function will be given by the elliptic genus of this 2d theory.

Due to the presence of a mass-Janus interface along which real masses change, we have two different effective descriptions of the theory on the two sides of the interface. The theory living on
[0,y_{+}]
, where
m=0
, is the full gauge theory with the gauge Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{u}(N)
. On the other hand, the theory living on
[y_{-},0]
, where
m\neq 0
, is the gauge theory describing the effective theory of low energy (relative to
m
) excitations in the vacuum
p_{-}
. This theory depends on the choice of a chamber
\mathfrak{C}
for the mass
m
, which in turn determines the phase of the theory. For generic masses, the adjoint scalar
\sigma
from the vector multiplet takes a generic VEV at this boundary (according to (
5.25
)), and as a result gauge symmetry is broken down to its commutant, the maximal abelian subgroup
\mathfrak{h}=\mathbb{R}^{N}
.

5
Boundaries and interfaces in 3d
\bm{{\mathcal{N}}=2}
SQCD

In this section, we look at the classical Higgs branch vacua of 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
SQCDs and
{\mathcal{N}}=(0,2)
boundary conditions that are cohomologous to these vacua. Our goal is to compute Janus partition functions (
4.11
) of the 3d theory on space-time manifolds of the form
[y_{-},y_{+}]\times\mathbb{E}_{\tau}
. We do so using supersymmetric localization using the supercharge (
B.8
). The supercharge belongs to an
{\mathcal{N}}=(0,2)
subalgebra of the 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
supersymmetry algebra, which is why we shall look at boundary conditions preserving this subalgebra. We follow
[
40
]
to find such boundary conditions that mimic the Higgs branch vacua of the theory. The main difference in our case is that, unlike in the 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=4
cases, we do not have a discrete set of completely massive vacua even in the presence of generic twisted masses. This requires us to slightly modify the notion of the so-called thimble boundaries so that they can be attached to extended subspaces of the Higgs branch.

5.1
Vacua and BPS equations in 3d
\bm{{\mathcal{N}}=2}
Gauge theories

5.1.1
Vacuum equations

Consider a 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
Lagrangian gauge theory with gauge group
G
and flavor symmetry group
F
. We minimize its potential (consisting of bosonic fields only) to find the classical moduli of vacua.

All terms of the action containing dynamical and background vector multiplet fields can be packaged together in terms of a single vector multiplet for the group
G^{\prime}:=G\times F
. We denote the fields of the
G^{\prime}
-multiplet as
\bigl(A,\sigma,\lambda,\overline{\lambda},\mathsf{D}\bigr)
. Part of the Lagrangian containing
A
,
\sigma
and
\mathsf{D}
only is

\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{tr}\left(\frac{1}{2}F^{\mu\nu}F_{\mu\nu}+D^{\mu}\sigma D_{\mu}\sigma+\mathsf{D}^{2}\right)-\text{i}\zeta\operatorname{tr}\mathsf{D}.

(5.1)

The first trace is from the Yang–Mills action and the second is the FI term. We are assuming that
G
has a
\U(1)
center and so there is a single real FI parameter
\zeta
. Note that
\zeta
corresponds to the background value of the adjoint scalar in the vector multiplet for the
\U(1)_{\text{top}}
global symmetry (
4.6
).

We consider chiral multiplets in the representation
\mathbf{M}
of the gauge group
G
and anti-chiral multiplets in the contragradient representation
\mathbf{M}^{\vee}
. Let us denote the gauge invariant pairing between
\mathbf{M}
and
\mathbf{M}^{\vee}
as
\left\langle\,,\,\right\rangle
. Then, the part of the chiral multiplet Lagrangian containing only bosonic fields from the chiral and vector multiplets is

-\left\langle\overline{\phi},D^{\mu}D_{\mu}\phi\right\rangle+\left\langle\sigma\cdot\overline{\phi},\sigma\cdot\phi\right\rangle+\text{i}\left\langle\overline{\phi},\mathsf{D}\phi\right\rangle+\left\langle\overline{\mathsf{F}},\mathsf{F}\right\rangle,

(5.2)

The equations of motion for the auxiliary fields
\mathsf{D}
and
\mathsf{F}
are

\mathsf{D}=\text{i}\zeta-\text{i}\mu_{\mathfrak{g}},\qquad\mathsf{F}=\overline{\mathsf{F}}=0,

(5.3)

where
\mu_{\mathfrak{g}}=\phi\overline{\phi}
is the real moment map of the
G
-action on
\mathbf{M}
. Eliminating the auxiliary fields from the Lagrangians (
5.1
) and (
5.2
) using the equations of motion we find the on-shell Lagrangian containing bosonic fields only

\operatorname{tr}\left(\frac{1}{4}F^{\mu\nu}F_{\mu\nu}+\frac{1}{2}D^{\mu}\sigma D_{\mu}\sigma\right)-\left\langle\overline{\phi},D^{\mu}D_{\mu}\phi\right\rangle+\operatorname{tr}(-\zeta+\mu_{\mathfrak{g}})^{2}+\left\langle\sigma\cdot\overline{\phi},\sigma\cdot\phi\right\rangle.

The derivative terms are the kinetic terms and the non-derivative terms constitute the potential that we want to minimize. The potential, being a sum of squares, is minimized when the individual terms are zero

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathfrak{g}}-\zeta=0,

(5.4a)

\displaystyle(\sigma_{\mathfrak{g}}+\sigma_{\mathfrak{f}})\cdot\phi=0.

(5.4b)

Here
\sigma_{\mathfrak{g}}+\sigma_{\mathfrak{f}}=\sigma
is the decomposition of
\sigma
into its dynamical and background components.

The classical Higgs branch of the theory is defined as the real symplectic quotient
\mu^{-1}_{\mathfrak{g}}(\zeta)/G
. The first vacuum equation (
5.4a
) is precisely this real moment map equation, and after implementing the
G
-quotient, the second equation (
5.4b
) becomes the criteria for fixed points under the infinitesimal action generated by
\sigma_{\mathfrak{f}}
.
\sigma_{\mathfrak{f}}
will be some element from the Cartan subalgebra
\mathfrak{t}
of the flavor symmetry group. Nonzero
\sigma=\sigma_{\mathfrak{g}}+\sigma_{\mathfrak{f}}
generates mass terms for various chiral multiplets and W-bosons. Thus we come to the following characterization of the (classical) massive vacua of a 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
gauge theory: they correspond to the fixed point set in the Higgs branch under the action of the maximal torus of the flavor symmetry group.

5.1.2
BPS equations

We look for field configurations left invariant by the localizing supercharge
\mathscr{Q}
(
B.8
). We consider the 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
theory on the space-time
I\times\mathbb{E}_{\tau}
, where
I
is an interval with real coordinate
y
and
\mathbb{E}_{\tau}
is an elliptic curve with holomorphic coordinate
z
. A supercharge of the theory is parameterized by 2 Dirac spinors
\epsilon
and
\overline{\epsilon}
, as in (
B.4
), and our choice of
\mathscr{Q}
is determined by

\mathscr{Q}=\mathscr{Q}_{\epsilon,\overline{\epsilon}},\qquad\text{where}\quad\epsilon=\overline{\epsilon}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
1\end{pmatrix}.

We thus find the BPS equations by setting the supersymmetry variations for the fermions from the vector (
B.5
) and the chiral (
B.6
) multiplets to zero for this specific choice of parameters. Doing so, we find the BPS equations – the ones containing derivatives along the elliptic curve
\mathbb{E}_{\tau}

\displaystyle F_{z\overline{z}}=0,

(5.5a)

\displaystyle D_{\overline{z}}\sigma_{\mathfrak{g}}=D_{\overline{z}}\sigma_{\mathfrak{f}}=0,

(5.5b)

\displaystyle D_{\overline{z}}\phi=D_{\overline{z}}\overline{\phi}=0,

(5.5c)

and the ones containing derivatives along
I

\displaystyle D_{y}A_{\overline{z}}=0,

(5.6a)

\displaystyle D_{y}\sigma_{\mathfrak{g}}+\zeta+\mu_{\mathfrak{g}}=0,

(5.6b)

\displaystyle D_{y}\sigma_{\mathfrak{f}}+\text{i}\mathsf{D}_{F}=0,

(5.6c)

\displaystyle D_{y}\phi+(\sigma_{\mathfrak{g}}+\sigma_{\mathfrak{f}})\cdot\phi=0,

(5.6d)

along with the conjugate equation for
\overline{\phi}
. We have used the equations of motion (
5.3
) to eliminate the auxiliary fields
\mathsf{D}_{\mathfrak{g}}
,
\mathsf{F}
and
\overline{\mathsf{F}}
. The only constraint on the background auxiliary field
\mathsf{D}_{F}
is (
5.6c
) and we use this equation as the definition of this particular background. The equations (
5.5
) determine the fields along the elliptic curve
\mathbb{E}_{\tau}
and the equations (
5.6
) determine their flow in the
y
-direction.

The connections in the above equations all contain both dynamical and background fields for the gauge and the flavor symmetry groups respectively. According to (
5.5a
) all these connections are flat and will be characterized by their holonomies around the cycles
S^{1}_{A}
and
S^{1}_{B}
of
\mathbb{E}_{\tau}
. Equation (
5.6a
) further implies that these holonomies are constant along the flow in the
y
-direction.
\sigma_{\mathfrak{g}}
and
\sigma_{\mathfrak{f}}
are holomorphic according to (
5.5b
) but since they are elements of real Lie algebras, they have to be constant on
\mathbb{E}_{\tau}
. Similarly,
\phi
and
\overline{\phi}
are holomorphic according to (
5.5c
). However, in the path integral we would like to impose the reality condition that the anti-chiral field
\overline{\phi}
is the complex conjugate of the chiral field
\phi
. Then both
\phi
and
\overline{\phi}
have to be constant on
\mathbb{E}_{\tau}
as well. Having nonzero holonomies for the flat connections is only compatible with constant VEVs for
\phi
if the holonomies act trivially on
\phi

sx^{-1}\hbar^{\frac{1}{2}}z\phi=\phi.

(5.7)

Here
s
,
x
,
\hbar
and
z
are the group valued holonomies for the Cartan valued connections for the gauge group
G
, flavor groups
F_{\mathsf{A}}
and
\U(1)_{\hbar}
, and the topological symmetry group
\U(1)_{\text{top}}
. None of the chirals are charged under
\U(1)_{\text{top}}
so
z
acts as identity on all chirals. The equation (
5.7
) is saying that the combined holonomies are invisible to the chirals, which is also referred to as a screening condition.

There is no equation for
A_{y}
, which is gauge equivalent to the zero connection since the
y
-direction is contractible. We are now left with the flow equations for
\sigma_{\mathfrak{g}}
(
5.6b
) and
\phi
(
5.6d
), which we shall address in Section
5.2.3
.

5.2
Classical Higgs branch

We consider the classical Higgs branch, where the chiral multiplet scalars have VEVs in the absence of twisted masses. The vacuum equation (
5.4b
) is then automatically satisfied because
{\sigma_{\mathfrak{g}}=\sigma_{\mathfrak{f}}=0}
and we find the classical Higgs branch to be (cf. (
2.3
))

\mathcal{M}_{H}=\mu_{\mathfrak{g}}^{-1}(\zeta)/G,

(5.8)

where
G
is the gauge group,
\mu_{\mathfrak{g}}
is the real moment map of the
G
-action on the representation space
\mathbf{M}
of the chirals and
\zeta
is a positive central element in the gauge Lie algebra. The construction (
5.8
) is the real symplectic reduction of
\mathbf{M}
by
G

\mathcal{M}_{H}=\mathbf{M}/\!\!/G
.
In 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
, the space of chirals is always Kähler and so is its symplectic quotient by a real Lie group. If we are only interested in the complex structure of the Higgs branch, we can equivalently define it as a GIT quotient by the complexified gauge group instead
19
19

19

This is how the Higgs branch was defined in (
2.1
).

\mathcal{M}_{H}=\mathbf{M}^{s}/G_{\mathbb{C}}.

(5.9)

Here
\mathbf{M}^{s}
is the stable locus of
\mathbf{M}
, which consists of all points of
\mathbf{M}
whose
G_{\mathbb{C}}
orbits intersect
\mu_{\mathfrak{g}}^{-1}(\zeta)
.

Twisted masses are turned on by giving
\mathfrak{t}
-valued nonzero VEV to the adjoint scalar
\sigma_{\mathfrak{f}}
in the flavor vector multiplet. We then have the additional vacuum equation
(\sigma_{\mathfrak{g}}+\sigma_{\mathfrak{f}})\cdot\phi=0
(
5.4b
). This tells us that most of the Higgs branch is now lifted and only the fixed points of the
\mathsf{T}
-action in the Higgs branch remain as vacua of the theory.
20
20

20

Later in computations, we only turn on
\mathsf{A}
-twisted masses and look for
\mathsf{A}
-fixed points where
\mathsf{T}=\mathsf{A}\times\U(1)_{\hbar}
.

We work out some details of the construction of the classical Higgs branches and their flavor fixed points for the specific theories defined by the quiver in Figure
3(b)
.

5.2.1
Classical Higgs branches of 3d
\bm{{\mathcal{N}}=2}
SQCDs

The real moment map of the
G
-action on the space of chirals is dictated by the D-term in the chiral multiplet Lagrangian (
5.2
). For the theory defined by Figure
3(b)
with the representations of the chirals as in (
4.7
), the moment map is
21
21

21

{\mathcal{Q}}
and
\overline{{\mathcal{Q}}}
are a priori independent fields in Euclidean signature, but we have to choose some reality condition on them to do our path integrals and we choose
\overline{{\mathcal{Q}}}={\mathcal{Q}}^{\dagger}
.

\mu={\mathcal{Q}}{\mathcal{Q}}^{\dagger}-\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{\dagger}\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}
.
And so the real moment map equation becomes:

{\mathcal{Q}}{\mathcal{Q}}^{\dagger}-\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{\dagger}\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}-\zeta=0,

(5.10)

where
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
is the positive FI parameter. The Higgs branch is the quotient

\mathcal{M}_{H}(N,L):=\mu^{-1}(\zeta)/U(N)
,
From this, we get the dimension of the Higgs branch

\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{M}_{H}(N,L)=2NL-N^{2}.

(5.11)

Furthermore, since
{\mathcal{Q}},\overline{\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}}\in\text{Hom}\bigl(\mathbb{C}^{L},\mathbb{C}^{N}\bigr)
, we have

\operatorname{rk}\mu\leq L
.
On the other hand, the moment map equation sets
\mu_{\mathfrak{g}}
to
\zeta
which has rank
N
. We thus get the constraint

N\leq L.

(5.12)

Notice from (
5.11
) that for odd values of
N
the complex dimension is odd and the resulting space can not have a holomorphic symplectic form. This is in sharp contrast with the case of 3d
{{\mathcal{N}}=4}
supersymmetry where the hyper-Kähler structure of Higgs branches equips them with a holomorphic symplectic form.

For our purposes, we mainly need the complex structure of the Higgs branch instead of the full Kähler structure. Let us look at an example.

Example 5.1

(
N=1
,
L=2
)
.

For gauge group
G=\U(1)
and flavor group
F_{\mathsf{A}}=\SU(2)
, the Higgs branch is a
3
-dimensional complex manifold. The space of the chirals is,
\mathbf{M}=\text{Hom}\bigl(\mathbb{C}^{2},\mathbb{C}\bigr)\oplus\text{Hom}\bigl(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{C}^{2}\bigr)
with holmorphic coordinates
{\mathcal{Q}}^{i}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}
for
i=1,2
. The moment-map equation is given by

\big|{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}\big|^{2}+\big|{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}\big|^{2}-\big|\widetilde{{\mathcal{Q}}}_{1}\big|^{2}-\big|\widetilde{{\mathcal{Q}}}_{2}\big|^{2}-\zeta=0.

(5.13)

Under a complexified gauge transformation by
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
the coordinates transform as

\bigl({\mathcal{Q}},\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}\bigr)\mapstochar\rightarrow\bigl(\lambda{\mathcal{Q}},\lambda^{-1}\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}\bigr).

So a set of gauge invariant holomorphic functions on the Higgs branch is given by the matrix elements of
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}{\mathcal{Q}}

\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}{\mathcal{Q}}=\begin{pmatrix}\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}&\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}\\
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}&\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}\end{pmatrix}.

Any gauge invariant polynomial function on the Higgs branch can be written in terms of these. Since
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}{\mathcal{Q}}\colon\mathbb{C}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{2}
factors through
\mathbb{C}
, it has rank at most
1
, consequently

\det\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}{\mathcal{Q}}=0.

(5.14)

The equation (
5.14
) defines the singular conifold as a subspace of
\text{Hom}\bigl(\mathbb{C}^{2},\mathbb{C}^{2}\bigr)
with singularity at
{\mathcal{Q}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}=0
where the rank of
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}{\mathcal{Q}}
is strictly less than 1. The Higgs branch
\mathcal{M}_{H}(1,2)
is a resolution of this singularity by the moment map equation (
5.13
). The moment map equation requires a nonzero
\big|{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}\big|^{2}+\big|{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}\big|^{2}
, which means that with
\zeta>0
, at
\det\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}{\mathcal{Q}}=0
we have
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}=0
. Then the moment map equation becomes
\big|{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}\big|^{2}+\big|{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}\big|^{2}=\zeta
. This defines an
S^{3}
with radius
\sqrt{\zeta}
, which together with the
\U(1)
-gauge identification implies that the singularity of the conifold is replaced with
S^{3}/\U(1)\simeq\mathbb{P}^{1}
of size determined by
\zeta
.

The resolved complex geometry can be described as follows. For
\zeta>0
, the requirement
\bigl({\mathcal{Q}}^{1},{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}\bigr)\neq(0,0)
coming from the moment map equation says that the map
{\mathcal{Q}}\colon\mathbb{C}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
is surjective. Due to the quotient by complex gauge transformations, only the kernel of this map is physical. Different choices of
\ker{\mathcal{Q}}\simeq\mathbb{C}\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{2}
are parameterized by a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
.
{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}
and
{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}
are the homogeneous coordinates on this
\mathbb{P}^{1}
. We can define the two local coordinates

\mathsf{z}:=\frac{{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}}{{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}}
,
\mathsf{z}^{\prime}:=\frac{{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}}{{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}}
,
to cover this
\mathbb{P}^{1}
. There is no constraint on
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}\colon\mathbb{C}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{2}
and since we have already used up the gauge transformations to classify
{\mathcal{Q}}
, all choices of
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}\colon\mathbb{C}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{2}
are physically significant. A map
\mathbb{C}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{2}
is characterized by the image of
1
which is unconstrained. Over the chart of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
with local coordinate
\mathsf{z}
we can parameterize these images of
1
by
\bigl(\frac{\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}}{{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}},\frac{\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}}{{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}}\bigr)
and over the chart with coordinate
\mathsf{z}^{\prime}
, we can parameterize the same images by

\left(\frac{\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}}{{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}},\frac{\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}}{{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}}\right)=\mathsf{z}^{\prime}\left(\frac{\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}}{{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}},\frac{\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}}{{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}}\right).

Overall, the maps
\bigl({\mathcal{Q}},\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}\bigr)
modulo complex gauge transformations form a rank 2 vector bundle over
\mathbb{P}^{1}
with transition function
\mathsf{z}^{\prime}
as we change coordinate
\mathsf{z}\mapstochar\rightarrow\mathsf{z}^{\prime}
. Dualizing this transition function we get the underlying space as the Higgs branch

\mathcal{M}_{H}(1,2)=\mathscr{O}(-1)\oplus\mathscr{O}(-1)\rightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}.

(5.15)

It is easy to generalize this example to arbitrary
N
and
L\geq N
. First, as a consequence of the moment map equation we have

Proposition 5.1
.

For
\zeta>0
, on the Higgs branch
\operatorname{rk}{\mathcal{Q}}=N
.

Proof.

Suppose
{\mathcal{Q}}
has rank less than
N
. Then
{\mathcal{Q}}{\mathcal{Q}}^{\dagger}
has rank less than
N
and we can choose a nonzero
v\in\ker{\mathcal{Q}}{\mathcal{Q}}^{\dagger}
. The moment map equation (
5.10
) then implies

-\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{\dagger}\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}v=\zeta v
.
However, the operator on the left-hand side is negative semi-definite and can not have a positive eigenvalue – we have a contradiction.
∎

Now let us consider the Higgs branch. Once again, a generating set of holomorphic functions on the Higgs branch are given by the matrix elements of
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}{\mathcal{Q}}
. Since
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}{\mathcal{Q}}
factors through
\mathbb{C}^{N}
we have the constraint

\operatorname{rk}\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}{\mathcal{Q}}\leq N
.
This defines the determinantal variety, denoted by
\mathsf{Det}(N,L)
, which has singularities where the rank of
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}{\mathcal{Q}}
is strictly less than
N
. These singularities are resolved by the real moment map equation.

Since
{\mathcal{Q}}\colon\mathbb{C}^{L}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{N}
is surjective (see Proposition
5.1
), modulo complexified gauge transformation by
\text{GL}_{N}
,
{\mathcal{Q}}
is completely fixed by its kernel. Once
{\mathcal{Q}}
is fixed,
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}\colon\mathbb{C}^{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{L}
is unconstrained, though the choices of
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}
are fibered nontrivially over the choices of
{\mathcal{Q}}
due to the
\text{GL}_{N}
quotient that acts on both the choice of
{\mathcal{Q}}
and that of
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}
. The quotient of
\mathbb{C}^{L}
by
\ker{\mathcal{Q}}
is parameterized by the Grassmannian
\text{Gr}(N,L)
. We can remember the
\text{GL}_{N}
action on
{\mathcal{Q}}
by the tautological principal
\text{GL}_{N}
bundle
{\mathcal{K}}_{P}
over
\text{Gr}(N,L)
. Then the choices of
\bigl({\mathcal{Q}},\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}\bigr)
such that
{\mathcal{Q}}
is surjective are parameterized by
{\mathcal{K}}_{P}\times\text{Hom}\bigl(\mathbb{C}^{N},\mathbb{C}^{L}\bigr)
. Taking the gauge quotient, we find the Higgs branch

\mathcal{M}_{H}(N,L)={\mathcal{K}}_{P}\times^{\text{GL}_{N}}\text{Hom}\bigl(\mathbb{C}^{N},\mathbb{C}^{L}\bigr)=\text{Hom}\bigl({\mathcal{K}},\mathbb{C}^{L}\bigr),

where
{\mathcal{K}}
and
\mathbb{C}^{L}
refer to the tautological vector bundle and a trivial bundle over
\text{Gr}(N,L)
. By construction, this is a vector bundle over
\text{Gr}(N,L)
.
22
22

22

Had we used the opposite sign
\zeta<0
of the real FI parameter in the moment map equation (
5.10
), the Higgs branch would be the dual bundle
\text{Hom}\bigl(\mathbb{C}^{L},{\mathcal{K}}\bigr)
.
The above space is also called the desingularization of the determinantal variety
\mathsf{Det}(N,L)

[
83
]
, and can be denoted by
\widehat{\mathsf{Det}}(N,L)
.

The singular conifold (
5.14
) is the special case of a determinantal variety for
N=1
and
{L=2}
, and the resolved conifold (
5.15
) is the aforementioned resolution thereof. In all abelian cases, we get

\mathcal{M}_{H}(1,L)=\widehat{\mathsf{Det}}(1,L)=\mathscr{O}(-1)^{\oplus L}\rightarrow\mathbb{P}^{L-1}.

5.2.2
Fixed points

Flavor fixed points in the Higgs branch are points of
\mathbf{M}
satisfying the real moment map equation where the flavor torus action can be undone by a gauge transformation. We are considering the gauge and flavor symmetry group
G=\U(N)
and
\mathsf{A}=\U(1)^{L-1}\subseteq\SU(L)
. Under an infinitesimal
{G\times\mathsf{A}}
action, the chiral multiplets transform as

{\mathcal{Q}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{Q}}+\sigma{\mathcal{Q}}-{\mathcal{Q}}m
,

\widetilde{{\mathcal{Q}}}\rightarrow\widetilde{{\mathcal{Q}}}-\widetilde{{\mathcal{Q}}}\sigma+m\widetilde{{\mathcal{Q}}}

where
\sigma\in\mathfrak{u}(N)
, and

m=\begin{pmatrix}m_{1}&&&\\
&m_{2}&&\\
&&\ddots&\\
&&&m_{L}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathfrak{a}=\mathbb{R}^{L},

(5.16)

with the constraint
m_{1}+\cdots+m_{L}=0
. Therefore,
\bigl({\mathcal{Q}},\widetilde{{\mathcal{Q}}}\bigr)
is a fixed point if the following equations admit solutions for
\sigma

\displaystyle\sigma{\mathcal{Q}}-{\mathcal{Q}}m

\displaystyle=0,\qquad\widetilde{{\mathcal{Q}}}\sigma-m\widetilde{{\mathcal{Q}}}

\displaystyle=0.

(5.17)

And
\bigl({\mathcal{Q}},\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}\bigr)
must satisfy the moment map equations (
5.10
) to be in the Higgs branch.

Let us denote by
{\mathcal{Q}}^{i}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}
for
i=1,\dots,L
the columns and rows of
{\mathcal{Q}}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}
, respectively. Then we can write (
5.17
) as

\sigma{\mathcal{Q}}^{i}=m_{i}{\mathcal{Q}}^{i},\qquad\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}\sigma=m_{i}\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}.

(5.18)

These are eigenvalue equations for the
2L
vectors
{\mathcal{Q}}^{i}
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}
. We assume that the masses
m_{i}
are all distinct. There are then
L
different eigenvalue equations since
{\mathcal{Q}}^{i}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}
share an eigenvalue. Since
\sigma
is an
N\times N
matrix with
N\leq L
, at most
N
out of these
L
eigenvalue equations can produce nonzero eigenvectors. On the other hand, for
\zeta>0
, the moment map equation (
5.10
) implies that
\operatorname{rk}{\mathcal{Q}}=N
(see Proposition
5.1
). Consequently, a solution to the eigenvalue equations (
5.18
) contains exactly
N
nonzero eigenvectors. The nonzero eigenvectors are labeled by an inclusion

p\colon\ \{1,\dots,N\}\hookrightarrow\{1,\dots,L\},

(5.19)

such that the nonzero eigenvectors are
{\mathcal{Q}}^{p(a)}
. Eigenvectors of hermitian operators with distinct eigenvalues are mutually orthogonal. So, as an orthonormal basis for the
N
-dimensional vector space spanned by
{\mathcal{Q}}^{p(a)}
we can choose
\bigl\{\frac{{\mathcal{Q}}^{p(1)}}{\lVert{\mathcal{Q}}^{p(1)}\rVert},\dots,\frac{{\mathcal{Q}}^{p(N)}}{\lVert{\mathcal{Q}}^{p(N)}\rVert}\bigr\}
. In this basis, the matrix
{\mathcal{Q}}
satisfies

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Q}}^{i}_{a}=\delta^{i}_{p(a)}\big\lVert{\mathcal{Q}}^{p(a)}\big\rVert\qquad\text{(no sum over $a$)}.

(5.20)

\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}
must be a multiple of
{\mathcal{Q}}^{i}
since they have the same eigenvalue

\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{a}_{i}=\delta^{p(a)}_{i}{\rm e}^{\text{i}\varphi}\big\lVert\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{p(a)}\big\rVert.

(5.21)

The phase is not fixed. Substituting (
5.20
) and (
5.21
) in the moment map equation (
5.10
), we get

{\mathcal{Q}}{\mathcal{Q}}^{\dagger}-\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{\dagger}\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}=\text{diag}\bigl(\big\lVert{\mathcal{Q}}^{p(1)}\big\rVert^{2}-\big\lVert\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{p(1)}\big\rVert^{2},\dots,\big\lVert{\mathcal{Q}}^{p(N)}\big\rVert^{2}-\big\lVert\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{p(N)}\big\rVert^{2}\bigr)=\zeta\mathds{1}_{N}.

Using gauge symmetry, we can fix all components of
{\mathcal{Q}}
to be real and positive, then there is a one-parameter family of solutions to the above moment map equation:

{\mathcal{Q}}^{i}_{a}=\delta^{i}_{p(a)}\sqrt{\zeta+|\lambda|^{2}},\qquad\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{a}_{i}=\delta^{p(a)}_{i}\lambda,\qquad\lambda\in\mathbb{C}.

(5.22)

To summarize, we have the following characterization of
\mathsf{A}
-fixed points of the Higgs branch
\mathcal{M}_{H}(N,L)
. There are
\binom{L}{N}
mutually disjoint connected components of the fixed point set, labeled by a choice
p
of
N
numbers out of
L
(
5.19
).
23
23

23

Only maps (
5.19
) modulo permutations of
\{1,\dots,N\}
lead to physically inequivalent vacua since the permutations coincide with the action of the Weyl subgroup of the gauge group
\U(N)
.
For such a choice
p
, the corresponding component of the fixed point set in the Higgs branch is given by the gauge orbit of a reference
N
-dimensional subspace
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{p}\subseteq\mathbf{M}
which we take to be (no sum over
a
,
b
)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{p}:=\bigl\{\bigl({\mathcal{Q}},\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}\bigr)\in\mathbf{M}\mid\bigl({\mathcal{Q}}^{i}_{a},\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{b}_{j}\bigr)=\bigl(\delta^{i}_{p(a)}\sqrt{\zeta+|\lambda_{a}|^{2}},\delta^{p(b)}_{j}\lambda_{b}\bigr),(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{N})\in\mathbb{C}^{N}\bigr\}.

(5.23)

We denote the image of
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{p}
in the complex Higgs branch (
5.9
) under the bundle projection

\mathbf{M}^{s}\xrightarrow{\pi}\mathbf{M}^{s}/\text{GL}_{N}=\mathcal{M}_{H}

(5.24)

by

{\mathcal{F}}_{p}:=\pi\bigl(\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{p}\bigr)
.
The projection
\pi
is one-to-one when restricted to
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{p}
.

Observe that the fixed points also satisfy the vacuum equations (
5.4
) in the case
\sigma_{\mathfrak{f}}=\sigma_{\mathsf{A}}
,
in other words, when we turn on twisted masses only with respect to the flavor symmetry
\SU(L)
(the framing node of the quiver Figure
3(b)
).
At these vacua, the value of the vector multiplet scalar
\sigma
is determined by (
5.4b
) (or equivalently (
5.18
)), which is

\displaystyle\sigma_{p}(m):=\begin{pmatrix}m_{p(1)}&&&\\
&m_{p(2)}&&\\
&&\ddots&\\
&&&m_{p(N)}\end{pmatrix}.

(5.25)

Thus, the
\mathsf{A}
-fixed point set corresponds to the residual classical Higgs branch in the presence of the
\mathsf{A}
-twisted masses

\mathcal{M}_{H}^{\mathsf{A}}=\bigsqcup_{p}{\mathcal{F}}_{p}=\text{classical Higgs branch with $\mathsf{A}$-twisted masses.}

(5.26)

At the vacuum
p
, we also get constraints on the holonomies for the flat connections around the elliptic curve from the screening conditions (
5.7
). The nontrivial screening conditions come from the chirals (
5.22
) that take nonzero VEVs, the conditions being

s_{a}x_{p(a)}^{-1}\hbar^{1/2}=1.

(5.27)

We shall refer to the gauge holonomies satisfying this condition by
s^{(p)}_{a}
.

5.2.3
Attracting sets and Morse flow

Consider the definition of the attracting set of a fixed point
x_{0}\in{\mathcal{F}}_{p}
(
3.4
)

\mathsf{Attr}_{\mathfrak{C}}(x_{0})=\bigl\{x\in\mathcal{M}_{H}\mid\lim_{t\rightarrow 0}f(t)\cdot x=x_{0}\text{ for all }f\in\mathfrak{C}\bigr\}.

Choose some generic
m=\text{diag}(m_{1},\dots,m_{L})\in\mathfrak{a}
such that
t\mapstochar\rightarrow t^{m}
is a cocharacter in the chamber
\mathfrak{C}
. Now, introduce the variable
y=-\ln t
such that the limit
t\rightarrow 0
corresponds to the limit
y\rightarrow\infty
. We take the limit
t\rightarrow 0
along the positive real axis so that
y
becomes a non-negative real number. Then we can rewrite the definition of the above attracting set as

\mathsf{Attr}_{\mathfrak{C}}(x_{0})=\bigl\{x\in\mathcal{M}_{H}\mid\lim_{y\rightarrow\infty}{\rm e}^{-ym}\cdot x=x_{0}\bigr\}.

Define the trajectory of
x
in the Higgs branch, as we take the limit
y\rightarrow\infty
, as a path

x\colon\ [0,\infty)\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{H},\qquad x(y):={\rm e}^{-ym}\cdot x_{0}.

This path satisfies the flow equation

\partial_{y}x+m\cdot x=0,

(5.28)

with the asymptotic boundary condition

\lim_{y\rightarrow\infty}x=x_{0}
.
The definition of the above attracting set can now be rewritten as

\displaystyle\mathsf{Attr}_{\mathfrak{C}}(x_{0})=\bigl\{x(0)\in\mathcal{M}_{H}\mid\partial_{y}x+m\cdot x=0,\,\lim_{y\rightarrow\infty}x=x_{0}\bigr\}.

(5.29)

Any smooth path
x(y)
in the Higgs branch can be lifted to a smooth path in the stable locus
\mathbf{M}^{s}
by undoing the projection (
5.24
) and non-uniquely choosing some point from each fiber over
x(y)
. Let
\widehat{x}(y)
be such a lift of
x(y)
. The flow equation (
5.28
) for
x
lifts to the following flow equation for
\widehat{x}

D_{y}\widehat{x}+(\sigma+m)\cdot\widehat{x}=0
.
Here
D_{y}
involves some
\mathfrak{gl}_{N}
-connection and
\sigma(y)
is some
\mathfrak{gl}_{N}
-valued function of
y
. These are the extra ingredients that will be lost once we take the projection by the
\text{GL}_{N}
-quotient. Let
\widehat{x}_{0}\in\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{p}
be a lift of
x_{0}
. We can constraint the lift
\widehat{x}
by requiring that its asymptotic value, instead of being something arbitrary from the
\text{GL}_{N}
-orbit of
\widehat{x}_{0}
, be exactly equal to
\widehat{x}_{0}

\lim_{y\rightarrow\infty}\widehat{x}(y)=\widehat{x}_{0}
.
This completely breaks gauge symmetry at
y=\infty
and therefore the value of
\sigma
must also be constrained asymptotically to take the value (
5.25
)
{\lim_{y\rightarrow\infty}\sigma(y)=\sigma_{p}(m)}
.
We can rewrite the definition of the attracting set (
5.29
) once more by projecting
\widehat{x}
as follows

\displaystyle\mathsf{Attr}_{\mathfrak{C}}(x_{0})=

\displaystyle\pi\bigl(\text{Flow}^{+}_{\mathfrak{C}}\bigl(\widehat{x}_{0}\bigr)\bigr),

(5.30)

where we have defined

\text{Flow}^{\pm}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\widehat{x}_{0}):=\bigl\{\widehat{x}(0)\in\mathbf{M}^{s}\mid D_{y}\widehat{x}+(\sigma+m)\cdot\widehat{x}=0,\,\lim_{y\rightarrow\pm\infty}(\widehat{x},\sigma)=\bigl(\widehat{x}_{0},\sigma_{p}(m)\bigr)\bigr\}.

(5.31)

This relates Morse flow in
\mathbf{M}
to the attracting sets in the Higgs branch. The flows in
\mathbf{M}
are not completely fixed because both the connection
D_{y}
and
\sigma
can be changed by
\text{GL}_{N}
gauge transformations as long as we satisfy the limit condition. We can choose some convenient representatives of these flows as follows.

Let us zoom in on the relation (
5.30
) at
x_{0}
, i.e., we look at the tangent space to the attracting set at
x_{0}

\displaystyle T_{x_{0}}\mathsf{Attr}_{\mathfrak{C}}(x_{0})=

\displaystyle\pi_{*}\bigl(\bigl\{\widehat{x}(0)\!\in\!\mathbf{M}^{s}\mid D_{y}\widehat{x}+(\sigma_{p}(m)+yD_{y}\sigma|_{x_{0}}\!\!+m)\!\cdot\!\widehat{x}=0,\,\lim_{y\rightarrow\infty}\widehat{x}=\widehat{x}_{0}\bigr\}\bigr).\!\!\!

(5.32)

We further use our gauge freedom to set
D_{y}=\partial_{y}
and
D_{y}\sigma\big|_{x_{0}}=0
. Small displacements around
{x_{0}\in\mathbf{M}^{s}}
can be parameterized by the chiral scalars
{\mathcal{Q}}^{i}_{a}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{a}_{i}
. These displacements are scaled by the action of
(\sigma_{p}(m)+m)
as

\displaystyle(\sigma_{p}(m)+m)\cdot{\mathcal{Q}}^{i}_{a}=(m_{p(a)}-m_{i}){\mathcal{Q}}^{i}_{a},\qquad(\sigma_{p}(m)+m)\cdot\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}^{a}=-(m_{p(a)}-m_{i})\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}^{a}.

(5.33)

Solutions to the flow equation
\partial_{y}\widehat{x}+(\sigma_{p}(m)+m)\cdot\widehat{x}=0
are therefore given by

{\mathcal{Q}}^{i}_{a}(y)={\rm e}^{-y(m_{p(a)}-m_{i})}{\mathcal{Q}}^{i}_{a}(0),\qquad\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}^{a}(y)={\rm e}^{y(m_{p(a)}-m_{i})}\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}^{a}(0).

The linear combination
\widehat{x}(y)=\widehat{x}_{0}+\kappa^{a}_{i}{\mathcal{Q}}^{i}_{a}(y)+\widetilde{\kappa}^{i}_{a}\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{a}_{i}(y)
then satisfies the same flow equation as well as the limit condition
\lim_{y\rightarrow\infty}\widehat{x}=\widehat{x}_{0}
if and only if the coefficients
\kappa
and
\widetilde{\kappa}
vanish for all chirals with non-positive weights. Let us decompose the space of chirals into subspaces according to the sign of their weights under the action (
5.33
)

\mathbf{M}=\mathbf{M}_{\mathfrak{C};+}(p)\oplus\mathbf{M}_{0}(p)\oplus\mathbf{M}_{\mathfrak{C};-}(p),

(5.34a)

where

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}_{\mathfrak{C};+}(p):=\text{Span}_{\mathbb{C}}\bigl\{{\mathcal{Q}}^{i}_{a},\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{b}_{j}\mid m_{p(a)}-m_{i}>0,\,m_{p(b)}-m_{j}<0\bigr\},

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}_{0}(p):=\text{Span}_{\mathbb{C}}\bigl\{{\mathcal{Q}}^{i}_{a},\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{b}_{j}\mid m_{p(a)}-m_{i}=m_{p(b)}-m_{j}=0\bigr\},

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}_{\mathfrak{C};-}(p):=\text{Span}_{\mathbb{C}}\bigl\{{\mathcal{Q}}^{i}_{a},\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{b}_{j}\mid m_{p(a)}-m_{i}<0,\,m_{p(b)}-m_{j}>0\bigr\}.

We can now rewrite (
5.32
) as

\displaystyle T_{x_{0}}\mathsf{Attr}_{\mathfrak{C}}(x_{0})\simeq

\displaystyle\pi_{*}\left(\mathbf{M}_{\mathfrak{C};+}(p)\right).

(5.35)

Here the vector space inside the projection on the right-hand side has its origin at
\widehat{x}_{0}
. The attracting set
\mathsf{Attr}_{\mathfrak{C}}(x_{0})
is generated by the action of
f(t)=t^{m}
for some generic cocharacter
f
in the chamber
\mathfrak{C}
. This action can be lifted to an action
\widehat{f}
on
\mathbf{M}
via an embedding
\mathsf{A}_{\mathbb{C}}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{H}_{\mathbb{C}}\times\mathsf{A}_{\mathbb{C}}
, such that for
\widehat{x}\in\mathbf{M}
we have
\widehat{f}(t)\cdot\widehat{x}=t^{\sigma_{p}(m)+m}\cdot\widehat{x}
. We can then define the attracting set of
\widehat{x}_{0}
in
\mathbf{M}^{s}
with respect to this action:

\widehat{\mathsf{Attr}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\widehat{x}_{0}):=\bigl\{\widehat{x}\in\mathbf{M}\mid\lim_{t\rightarrow 0}t^{\sigma_{p}(m)+m}\cdot\widehat{x}=\widehat{x}_{0}\bigr\}.

The tangent space to this affine space at
\widehat{x}_{0}
is
\mathbf{M}_{\mathfrak{C};+}(p)
. The projection
\pi
is
\mathsf{A}_{\mathbb{C}}
equivariant and it is a surjection (
5.35
) on tangent spaces. Therefore, it is a surjection globally, i.e.,
\widehat{\mathsf{Attr}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\widehat{x}_{0})
projects onto the full attracting set
\mathsf{Attr}_{\mathfrak{C}}(x_{0})
. We can also immediately see that the attracting set
\widehat{\mathsf{Attr}}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\widehat{x}_{0})
is nothing but
\{\widehat{x}_{0}\}\times\mathbf{M}_{\mathfrak{C};+}(p)
. Therefore,

\mathsf{Attr}_{\mathfrak{C}}(x_{0})=\pi\bigl(\bigl\{\widehat{x}_{0}\bigr\}\times\mathbf{M}_{\mathfrak{C};+}(p)\bigr).

Comparing with (
5.30
) we find a gauge equivalence between positive weight spaces in
\mathbf{M}
and Morse flow in the stable locus
\mathbf{M}^{s}
that asymptotes some fixed point in
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{p}
. More specifically, by varying
x_{0}
over
{\mathcal{F}}_{p}
, we find

\pi\bigl(\text{Flow}^{+}_{\mathfrak{C}}\bigl(\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{p}\bigr)\bigr)=\mathsf{Attr}_{\mathfrak{C}}({\mathcal{F}}_{p})=\pi\bigl(\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{p}\times\mathbf{M}_{\mathfrak{C};+}(p)\bigr).

(5.36)

Replacing the limit
y\rightarrow\infty
with
y\rightarrow-\infty
, and following the same arguments we find

\pi\bigl(\text{Flow}^{-}_{\mathfrak{C}}\bigl(\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{p}\bigr)\bigr)=\mathsf{Rep}_{\mathfrak{C}}({\mathcal{F}}_{p})=\pi\bigl(\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{p}\times\mathbf{M}_{\mathfrak{C};-}(p)\bigr).

(5.37)

Here
\mathsf{Rep}
refers to the repelling set defined similarly to the attracting set (
3.4
) but with the opposite limit
t\rightarrow\infty
.

5.3
Boundaries and interfaces

We discuss half-BPS boundary conditions that are equivalent, in localization computations, to forcing the fields to reach any particular connected component of the solutions to the vacuum equation (
5.4
). The choice of localizing supercharge is discussed in Appendix
B.3
, (
B.8
) in particular. We discuss half-BPS boundaries preserving
\mathscr{Q}_{-}
,
\overline{\mathscr{Q}}_{-}
. So these are
{\mathcal{N}}=(0,2)
boundary conditions in the 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
theory. We also discuss the mass Janus interface preserving the same supersymmetry. Our discussion mostly follows similar boundary conditions for the 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=4
case considered in
[
40
]
. Our description of the thimble boundaries (see Section
5.3.1
) differs slightly from this reference due to the non-isolated nature of 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
Higgs branch vacua.

5.3.1
Thimble (exceptional Dirichlet),
\bm{\mathscr{D}_{\mathfrak{C}}(p)}

To find a boundary condition at
y=y_{-}
that is cohomologous to a choice of vacuum, we extend our space-time to negative infinity and consider the theory on
(-\infty\times y_{-}]\times\mathbb{E}_{\tau}
. We impose the constraint that the theory must reach the vacuum
p
at infinity. Then we observe the supersymmetric evolution of the fields along the interval
(-\infty,y_{-}]
and see what values the fields take at
y=y_{-}
. More specifically, we solve the BPS equations (
5.6
) with the boundary condition that at
y=-\infty
the fields satisfy the vacuum equation (
5.4
). Then the values of these solutions at
y=y_{-}
provide a past boundary condition for the theory at
y>=y_{-}
that mimics the chosen vacuum in BPS computations. Similar boundary conditions were constructed and called thimble or exceptional Dirichlet boundary conditions in
[
22
]
.

In this process, we observe that the BPS equation (
5.6d
) with the condition that at past infinity the chiral and vector multiplet scalars satisfy the vacuum equations (
5.4
) is precisely what defines the set
\text{Flow}^{-}_{\mathfrak{C}}\bigl(\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{p}\bigr)
(
5.31
). The choice of chamber and vacuum determine the Morse function and the connected component of the vacuum configurations defining the boundary condition. The BPS equation (
5.6b
) puts constraints on the evolution of
\sigma_{\mathfrak{g}}
but
\text{Flow}^{-}_{\mathfrak{C}}\bigl(\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{p}\bigr)
is agnostic to such constraints. According to (
5.37
), the set
\text{Flow}^{-}_{\mathfrak{C}}\bigl(\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{p}\bigr)
is gauge equivalent to the subspace
{\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{p}\times\mathbf{M}_{\mathfrak{C};-}(p)}
of
\mathbf{M}
. So, we formulate a
(0,2)
boundary condition of brane type with support for the chiral multiplet scalars on
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{p}\times\mathbf{M}_{\mathfrak{C};-}(p)
.

Remark 5.1

(thimbles in
{\mathcal{N}}=4
vs
{\mathcal{N}}=2
)
.

Support of this brane (at the past boundary) on the Higgs branch is the projection of
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{p}\times\mathbf{M}_{\mathfrak{C};-}(p)
, which is the repelling set (
5.37
). In
[
22
]
, the thimble boundaries are supported on holomorphic Lagrangians in the hyperkähler Higgs branches of 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=4
theories. For 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
theories, the Higgs branches are generally only Kähler and the attracting/repelling sets (
5.36
), (
5.37
) are not supported on holomorphic Lagrangians, they are just holomorphic.

Let us decompose the space of chirals further from (
5.34a
) based on where the
{\mathcal{Q}}
s and the
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}
s belong

\mathbf{M}_{\mathfrak{C};\pm}(p)={\mathcal{R}}_{\mathfrak{C};\pm}(p)\oplus\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{\mathfrak{C};\pm}(p),\qquad\mathbf{M}_{0}(p)={\mathcal{R}}_{0}(p)\oplus\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{0}(p).

Since
{\mathcal{Q}}^{i}_{a}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{a}_{i}
have weights with opposite signs (see (
5.33
)), we have the following relations among these subspaces

{\mathcal{R}}_{\mathfrak{C};\pm}(p)=\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{\mathfrak{C};\mp}^{\vee}(p),\qquad{\mathcal{R}}_{0}(p)=\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{0}^{\vee}(p).

We thus have a Lagrangian splitting of the chirals

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}=\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{C}}(p)\oplus\mathbb{L}^{\perp}_{\mathfrak{C}}(p),\qquad\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{C}}(p):=\mathbf{M}_{\mathfrak{C};+}(p)\oplus{\mathcal{R}}_{0}(p),

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}^{\perp}_{\mathfrak{C}}(p):=\mathbf{M}_{\mathfrak{C};-}(p)\oplus\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{0}(p).

(5.38)

\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{p}
is parameterized by arbitrary VEVs of fields from
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{0}(p)
(see (
5.23
)), therefore, chirals from
\mathbb{L}^{\perp}_{\mathfrak{C}}(p)
parameterize the brane
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{p}\times\mathbf{M}_{\mathfrak{C};-}(p)
. So we shall put Dirichlet and Neumann boundary conditions on the scalars from the chirals in
\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{C}}(p)
and
\mathbb{L}^{\perp}_{\mathfrak{C}}(p)
respectively. The boundary conditions will be extended to the rest of the fields of these multiplets by the requirement of preserving
(0,2)
supersymmetry.

Gauge symmetry is broken at the vacuum by the VEVs of the chirals and as such the gauge field receives Dirichlet boundary condition, the rest of the vector multiplet receiving boundary conditions accordingly. The holonomy of the dynamical gauge field at the vacuum is given in terms of the holonomy of the background gauge fields via the screening condition (
5.27
).

Altogether, the thimble boundary condition
\mathscr{D}_{\mathfrak{C}}(p)
involves:

\displaystyle\text{Chirals in }\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{C}}(p)\colon\ \text{Dirichlet with VEVs according to vacuum }p,

(5.39)

\displaystyle\text{Chirals in }\mathbb{L}^{\perp}_{\mathfrak{C}}(p)\colon\ \text{Neumann},

\displaystyle\text{Vector multiplet}\colon\ \text{Dirichlet with screening (\ref{screening}) of gauge holonomy.}

Boundary ’t Hooft anomaly.
Let
{\mathbf{f}}\in\mathfrak{h}
,
{\mathbf{f}}_{x}\in\mathfrak{a}
and
{\mathbf{f}}_{\hbar}\in\mathbb{R}_{\hbar}
be the curvatures of the gauge,
\SU(L)
flavor, and
\U(1)_{\hbar}
flavor symmetry at the boundary. The gauge symmetry is completely broken at the boundary and
{\mathbf{f}}
is a function of the flavor curvatures. Their dependence is fixed by demanding that the nonzero VEVs at the boundary do not observe any flux at the boundary
{\mathbf{f}}=f^{(p)}({\mathbf{f}}_{x},{\mathbf{f}}_{\hbar})
such that,

w({\mathbf{f}}+{\mathbf{f}}_{x}+{\mathbf{f}}_{\hbar})=0
for all
w\in{\mathcal{R}}_{0}(p)
.
Contributions to the anomaly polynomial come from two sources: 1) Chern–Simons terms created by integrating out massive fermions and the Chern–Simons coupling to background multiplet associated with
\U(1)_{\text{top}}
symmetry, 2) purely from boundary conditions. In the present case, these contributions are
[
40
,
43
]
24
24

24

The trace without explicit representation is taken over the fundamental representation.

P^{\text{CS}}_{-}(\mathscr{D}_{\mathfrak{C}}(p))=2\operatorname{tr}_{\mathbf{M}_{\mathfrak{C};+}(p)}({\mathbf{f}}_{\hbar}({\mathbf{f}}+{\mathbf{f}}_{x}))-2\operatorname{tr}({\mathbf{f}}{\mathbf{f}}_{\text{top}}),\qquad P^{\partial}(\mathscr{D}_{\mathfrak{C}}(p))=2\operatorname{tr}_{{\mathcal{R}}_{0}(p)}({\mathbf{f}}_{\hbar}({\mathbf{f}}+{\mathbf{f}}_{x})).

The subscript in
P^{\text{CS}}_{-}
refers to the past boundary. The Chern–Simons anomaly depends on the orientation of the boundary and therefore
P^{\text{CS}}_{-}=-P^{\text{CS}}_{+}
.
P^{\partial}
is independent of the choice of past or future. If we define

P(\mathscr{D}_{\mathfrak{C}}(p)):=P^{\text{CS}}_{-}(\mathscr{D}_{\mathfrak{C}}(p))+P^{\partial}(\mathscr{D}_{\mathfrak{C}}(p))=2\operatorname{tr}_{\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{C}}(p)}({\mathbf{f}}_{\hbar}({\mathbf{f}}+{\mathbf{f}}_{x}))-2\operatorname{tr}({\mathbf{f}}{\mathbf{f}}_{\text{top}}),

then the total anomaly polynomial associated to the thimble boundary is
P(\mathscr{D}_{\mathfrak{C}}(p))
evaluated at
{\mathbf{f}}={\mathbf{f}}^{(p)}({\mathbf{f}}_{x},{\mathbf{f}}_{\hbar})
.

5.3.2
Enriched Neumann,
\bm{\mathscr{N}_{\mathfrak{C}}(p)}

Swap the boundary conditions on the chiral multiplets from (
5.39
). Meaning, the chirals in
\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{C}}(p)
now receive Neumann, and those in
\mathbb{L}^{\perp}_{\mathfrak{C}}(p)
receive Dirichlet boundary conditions with zero VEV. The gauge field in the vector multiplet receives the Neumann boundary condition. This implies that the adjoint scalar
\sigma_{\mathfrak{g}}
from the vector multiplet gets Dirichlet
[
43
, Section 2.5.2]
. We set the value of
\sigma_{\mathfrak{g}}
equal to the VEV
\sigma_{p}(m)
(
5.25
) corresponding to the vacuum
p
. This breaks the gauge symmetry down to the maximal torus
\mathsf{H}=\U(1)^{N}
. Additionally, we couple a 2d
{\mathcal{N}}=(0,2)
theory
\Upsilon_{\mathfrak{C}}(p)
at the boundary by gauging some of its global symmetries with the boundary gauge symmetry
\mathsf{H}
. We shall characterize the theory
\Upsilon_{\mathfrak{C}}(p)
momentarily. Let us first summarize this enriched Neumann boundary condition
\mathscr{N}_{\mathfrak{C}}(p)

\displaystyle\text{Chirals in }\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{C}}(p)\colon\ \text{Neumann},

\displaystyle\text{Chirals in }\mathbb{L}^{\perp}_{\mathfrak{C}}(p)\colon\ \text{Dirichlet with zero VEVs},

\displaystyle\text{Vector multiplet}\colon\ \text{Neumann},

\displaystyle\text{Coupled to the boundary}\colon\ \text{2d }{\mathcal{N}}=(0,2)\text{ theory }\Upsilon_{\mathfrak{C}}(p).

The relation between the thimble boundary conditions (
5.39
) and vacua are relatively straightforward. However, in computation, we shall use this new boundary condition
\mathscr{N}_{\mathfrak{C}}(p)
to represent the vacuum
p
. In
[
40
, Section 6.3]
, Dedushenko–Nekrasov argue that both
\left|\mathscr{D}_{\mathfrak{C}}(p)\right\rangle\left\langle\mathscr{N}_{\mathfrak{C}}(p)\right|
and
{\left|\mathscr{N}_{\mathfrak{C}}(p)\right\rangle\left\langle\mathscr{D}_{\mathfrak{C}}(p)\right|}
are projections onto the vacuum
p
in cohomology, where
\left\langle\cdot\right|
and
\left|\cdot\right\rangle
refer to future and past boundary condition, respectively. A necessary condition for this to be true is that these projectors are free of ’t Hooft anomaly

P_{\pm}(\mathscr{D}_{\mathfrak{C}}(p))\big|_{{\mathbf{f}}={\mathbf{f}}^{(p)}({\mathbf{f}}_{x},{\mathbf{f}}_{\hbar})}+P_{\mp}(\mathscr{N}_{\mathfrak{C}}(p))=0.

(5.40)

Here
P_{+}
and
P_{-}
refer to anomaly polynomials of the future and past boundary conditions, respectively. In this paper, we only describe the
\mathscr{N}_{\mathfrak{C}}(p)
boundary condition and show that this anomaly condition is satisfied, beyond that, we take their equivalence as an ansatz. For more details, see
[
40
]
.

Boundary theory
\bm{\Upsilon_{\mathfrak{C}}(p)}
.
At the boundary preserving
{\mathcal{N}}=(0,2)
supersymmetry, we have
\U(1)^{N}
gauge symmetry. So we can couple a 2d
{\mathcal{N}}=(0,2)
theory with
\U(1)^{N}
global symmetry by gauging it as long as the anomaly matching condition (
5.40
) is satisfied. It is straightforward to write down a boundary theory for any chamber, but for the sake of explicit formulas we shall focus here on the following two chambers

\mathfrak{C}_{1}:=m_{1}<m_{2}<\cdots<m_{L},\qquad\mathfrak{C}_{2}:=m_{1}>m_{2}>\cdots>m_{L}.

(5.41)

For
\mathfrak{C}_{1}
, we propose the following field content and charges for the theory under various symmetry groups applying the procedure of field content of
\Upsilon_{\mathfrak{C}_{1}}(p)
:

\begin{array}[]{|c|ccc|}\hline\cr&\U(1)_{a}&\U(1)_{\hbar}&\U(1)_{\text{top}}\\
\hline\cr\text{Chiral}_{1,a}&-1&1&0\\
\text{Chiral}_{2,a}&0&\frac{L}{2}-p(a)&1\\[2.84526pt]
\text{Fermi}_{1,a}&1&\frac{L}{2}-p(a)&1\\
\text{Fermi}_{2,a}&0&1&0\\
\hline\cr\end{array}.

We have one set of these fields for each
a=1,\dots,N
, totaling in
2N
Fermi and
2N
chiral multiplets. The
\U(1)_{a}
global symmetry is identified with a
\U(1)
subgroup of the bulk
{\U(1)^{N}\times\U(1)^{L-1}\times\U(1)_{\hbar}}
symmetry such that the 2d
\U(1)_{a}
-fugacity is identified with the bulk fugacity
s_{a}x^{-1}_{p(a)}\hbar^{1/2}
. The remaining symmetries are identified straightforwardly. The elliptic genus of this theory is
[
17
,
18
]

\mathbb{W}(\Upsilon_{\mathfrak{C}_{1}}(p))=\prod_{a=1}^{N}\frac{\vartheta\bigl(s_{a}x_{p(a)}^{-1}\hbar^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{L}{2}-p(a)}z\bigr)\vartheta(\hbar)}{\vartheta\bigl(s^{-1}_{a}x_{p(a)}\hbar^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigr)\vartheta\bigl(z\hbar^{\frac{L}{2}-p(a)}\bigr)}.

(5.42)

Here we are using the theta functions defined in the notation of
[
2
]

\vartheta(t;q):=\bigl(t^{\frac{1}{2}}-t^{-\frac{1}{2}}\bigr)\bm{\prod}_{n=1}^{\infty}\bigl(1-tq^{n}\bigr)\bigl(1-t^{-1}q^{n}\bigr).

(5.43)

Some of its identities are
