\displaystyle\vartheta(1;q)=0,\qquad\vartheta\bigl(t^{-1};q\bigr)=-\vartheta(t;q),\qquad\vartheta\bigl({\rm e}^{2\pi\mathfrak{i}}t;q\bigr)=-\vartheta(t;q),

\displaystyle\vartheta\bigl(q^{k}t;q\bigr)=q^{-\frac{k^{2}}{2}}(-t)^{-k}\vartheta(t;q),\qquad k\in\mathbb{Z}.

This theta function can be related to Jacobi’s theta function and the Dedekind eta function as follows:

q^{\frac{1}{12}}\vartheta(t;q)=\frac{\text{i}\theta_{1}\bigl(\frac{\ln t}{2\pi\text{i}};\frac{\ln q}{2\pi\text{i}}\bigr)}{\eta\bigl(\frac{\ln q}{2\pi\text{i}}\bigr)}.

We omit the argument
q
in the theta function when it is clear from the context.

For the chamber
\mathfrak{C}_{2}
, we simply write down the elliptic genus

\mathbb{W}(\Upsilon_{\mathfrak{C}_{2}}(p))=\prod_{a=1}^{N}\frac{\vartheta\bigl(s_{a}x_{p(a)}^{-1}\hbar^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{L}{2}+p(a)}z\bigr)\vartheta(\hbar)}{\vartheta\bigl(s^{-1}_{a}x_{p(a)}\hbar^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigr)\vartheta\bigl(z\hbar^{-\frac{L}{2}+p(a)-1}\bigr)}.

(5.44)

The field content of the theory and their charges can be read off from this if needed. Notice that when the screening conditions (
5.27
) are imposed the elliptic genera reduce to 1.

The choice of the boundary theory is defined only up to the addition of anomaly-free 2d
{\mathcal{N}}=(0,2)
theories. This leads to some ambiguities regarding the overall partition function, and consequently regarding the stable envelope. We can fix some of this ambiguity by imposing certain normalization conditions on the stable envelopes (as was done in their definition in Theorem
3.1
). The normalization condition restricts our choice of boundary theory to those without anomaly and whose elliptic genus reduces to 1 when the screening conditions (
5.27
) are imposed. Even then the choice is not unique. Our choice differs minimally from the closely related example of the
T^{*}\text{Gr}(N,L)
Grassmannian theory with 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=4
symmetry from
[
40
, Section 6.5.3]
.

Boundary ’t Hooft anomaly.

The enriched Neumann boundary condition (
5.3.2
) receives contributions to its anomaly polynomial from Chern–Simons terms, boundary conditions on chirals and vectors, and the boundary theory

P(\mathscr{N}_{\mathfrak{C}}(p)):=P^{\text{CS}}_{-}(\mathscr{N}_{\mathfrak{C}}(p))+P^{\partial}(\mathscr{N}_{\mathfrak{C}}(p))+P(\Upsilon_{\mathfrak{C}}(p)).

The Chern–Simons anomalies only depend on the orientation of the boundary,
P^{\text{CS}}_{-}(\mathscr{N}_{\mathfrak{C}}(p))=P^{\text{CS}}_{-}(\mathscr{D}_{\mathfrak{C}}(p))
. Whereas, the boundary conditions on the chirals and the vector multiplet fields are swapped between the thimble (
5.39
) and the enriched Neumann (
5.3.2
). So they contribute with opposite signs:
P^{\partial}_{-}(\mathscr{N}_{\mathfrak{C}}(p))=-P^{\partial}_{-}(\mathscr{D}_{\mathfrak{C}}(p))
. Since we have a gauge theory at the boundary, the gauge anomalies from all the different sources must neatly cancel. In fact, the boundary theory is defined to satisfy the constraint that only ’t Hooft anomalies survive and they match the negative of the future thimble boundary anomaly (
5.40
) define
\Upsilon_{\mathfrak{C}}(p)
such that

P_{-}(\mathscr{N}_{\mathfrak{C}}(p))=-P_{+}(\mathscr{D}_{\mathfrak{C}}(p))\big|_{{\mathbf{f}}={\mathbf{f}}^{(p)}({\mathbf{f}}_{x},{\mathbf{f}}_{\hbar})}.

The anomaly of the elliptic genera (
5.42
) and (
5.44
) satisfy this condition for the chambers
\mathfrak{C}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{C}_{2}
(
5.41
) respectively. For reference, we note that in any chamber
\mathfrak{C}
, the anomaly of the boundary theory must be

P(\Upsilon_{\mathfrak{C}}(p))=-2(\operatorname{tr}_{\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{C}}(p)}-2\operatorname{tr}_{{\mathcal{R}}_{0}(p)})\bigl(\bigl({\mathbf{f}}-{\mathbf{f}}^{(p)}\bigr){\mathbf{f}}_{\hbar}\bigr)+2\operatorname{tr}\bigl(\bigl({\mathbf{f}}-{\mathbf{f}}^{(p)}\bigr){\mathbf{f}}_{\text{top}}\bigr).

It can be checked that the elliptic genera (
5.42
) and (
5.44
) contribute the appropriate anomalies for their respective chambers.

5.3.3
Massless boundary condition,
\bm{\mathscr{B}_{\mathbb{L}(p)}(p)}

Broadly speaking, stable envelope is a map from the cohomology of the flavor fixed points of the Higgs branch to the cohomology of the Higgs branch itself (
1.3
). To compute this map, we compute the partition function of the 3d theory on
[y_{-},y_{+}]\times\mathbb{E}_{\tau}
such that at the past boundary we have twisted masses which eventually vanish at the future boundary (
4.11
). At the past boundary, we can, in principle, use either the thimble (see Section
5.3.1
) or the enriched Neumann (see Section
5.3.2
) boundary conditions both of which are labeled by connected components of the fixed point set and provide a basis for the cohomology of the fixed point set. As vector spaces, the cohomology of the Higgs branch is isomorphic to that of its fixed points. We, therefore, look for a set of boundary conditions to impose at the future massless boundary parameterized by connected components of the fixed point set as well, such that we can write down the matrix elements of the stable envelope in terms of pairs of connected components. Such a set of boundary conditions was proposed by Dedushenko–Nekrasov in
[
40
]
. These boundary conditions are very similar to the thimble boundary conditions. However, since there are no masses at this boundary, there is no choice of chamber and we can not choose a polarization, as we did for the thimbles in (
5.38
), based on positive and negative masses. More specifically, there are no obvious candidates for
\mathbf{M}_{\mathfrak{C};\pm}(p)
. The massless parts of the polarization, namely
{\mathcal{R}}_{0}(p)
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{0}(p)
still make sense as they are chamber independent. The proposal of Dedushenko–Nekrasov in this case is to arbitrarily choose a polarization of the chirals

\mathbf{M}=\mathbb{L}(p)\oplus\mathbb{L}^{\perp}(p)
,
as long as they satisfy (cf. (
5.38
))

{\mathcal{R}}_{0}(p)\subseteq\mathbb{L}(p),\qquad\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{0}(p)\subseteq\mathbb{L}^{\perp}(p).

(5.45)

In all our computations, we make the following choice

\mathbb{L}(p):={\mathcal{R}}
,
\mathbb{L}^{\perp}(p):=\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}
,
which obviously satisfies (
5.45
).

Once the choice of polarization is made, this boundary condition, which we call
\mathscr{B}_{\mathbb{L}(p)}(p)
, can be described just as in (
5.39
):

\displaystyle\text{Chirals in}\mathbb{L}(p)\colon\ \text{Dirichlet with VEVs according to vacuum}\ p,

\displaystyle\text{Chirals in}\mathbb{L}^{\perp}(p)\colon\ \text{Neumann},

\displaystyle\text{Vector multiplet}\colon\ \text{Dirichlet with screening (\ref{screening}) of gauge holonomy.}

5.3.4
Janus interface,
\bm{{\mathcal{J}}(m_{\mathfrak{C}},0)}

The transition from a massive boundary in the past labeled by a fixed point
p
to a massless boundary in the future is achieved via a mass Janus interface. We assume that the masses in the past belong to the chamber
\mathfrak{C}
. Take
y_{-}<0
and
y_{+}>0
and put the Janus interface at
y=0
wrapping the elliptic curve
\mathbb{E}_{\tau}
.

We only vary the
F_{\mathsf{A}}=\SU(L)
twisted masses in the theory across the Janus interface. We do not turn on any twisted masses for the
\U(1)_{\hbar}
flavor symmetry. In principle, the mass Jauns is implemented by giving a
y
-dependent background value to the adjoint scalar
\sigma_{\mathfrak{f}_{\mathsf{A}}}
in the flavor vector multiplet. The background value interpolates between some generic mass
m
(
5.16
) in the chamber
\mathfrak{C}
and zero. This is supersymmetric as long as we also turn on the auxiliary field in the multiplet satisfying the BPS equation (
5.6c
) (with
\sigma_{\hbar}=\mathsf{D}_{\hbar}=0
such that
{\sigma_{\mathfrak{f}}=\sigma_{\mathfrak{f}_{\mathsf{A}}}}
and
{\mathsf{D}_{F}=\mathsf{D}_{F_{\mathsf{A}}}}
). In particular, we can choose a background profile for the mass that is constant on the intervals
[y_{-},0)
and
(0,y+]
and changes rapidly near
y=0
. Similar Janus interfaces in 3d
{{\mathcal{N}}=4}
theories were previously used to compute stable envelopes
[
40
]
and
R
-matrices
[
23
,
24
]
for bosonic spin chains.

The supersymmetric quantities we compute are supposed to be renormalization group invariants and capture the infrared dynamics. We, therefore, do not lose anything by considering the nonzero masses to be arbitrarily large. In this limit, we can integrate out the massive fields from the interval
[y_{-},0)
. But these fields are all present on the other side
(0,y_{+}]
. Thus, the fields that acquire masses in the massive vacuum
p
, including chirals and W-bosons, terminate at the interface when approaching from the massless side. This is implemented by a set of half-BPS boundary conditions at
y=0
that were worked out in
[
40
]
. We are simply going to use their prescription.

The chirals with positive and negative masses in the vacuum
p
belong to
\mathbf{M}_{\mathfrak{C};+}
and
\mathbf{M}_{\mathfrak{C};-}
respectively, according to our earlier definition of these spaces (
5.34a
). To characterize the massive W-bosons, let us decompose the set
\Delta
of roots of the gauge Lie algebra into subsets corresponding to the sign of their masses at the vacuum
p
. At
p
, the adjoint scalar in the vector multiplet takes the VEV
\sigma_{p}(m)
given by (
5.25
). Therefore, the vector field associated to the root
\alpha\in\Delta
picks up a mass
\alpha(\sigma_{p}(m))
. The decomposition of the roots can then be written as
\Delta=\Delta_{+}(p)\sqcup\Delta_{0}\sqcup\Delta_{-}(p)
,
where

\displaystyle\Delta_{+}(p):=\{\alpha\in\Delta|\alpha(\sigma_{p}(m))>0\},\qquad\Delta_{0}:=\{\alpha\in\Delta|\alpha(\sigma_{p}(m))=0\},

\displaystyle\Delta_{-}(p):=\{\alpha\in\Delta|\alpha(\sigma_{p}(m))<0\}.

For generic masses, always only the Cartan valued fields remain massless, so
\Delta_{0}=\{0\}
in any vacuum.

The interface is then implemented by the following boundary conditions on fields
[
40
]
:

\displaystyle\text{Chirals in }\mathbf{M}_{\mathfrak{C};+}(p)\colon\ \text{Dirichlet},

(5.46)

\displaystyle\text{Chirals in }\mathbf{M}_{\mathfrak{C};-}(p)\colon\ \text{Neumann},

\displaystyle\text{Vectors in }\Delta_{+}(p)\colon\ \text{Dirichlet},

\displaystyle\text{Vectors in }\Delta_{-}(p)\colon\ \text{Neumann}.

From the definition of the Janus interface as a supersymmetric position-dependent background profile for the masses, it is clear that it does not introduce any anomaly. However, in practice, we implement the Janus interface effectively using the above-mentioned boundary conditions and it is not obvious that they are anomaly free. After all, both the boundary conditions and the Chern–Simons terms created by integrating out massive fermions contribute to anomaly at the boundary. It is however a simple exercise to check that these two anomalies precisely cancel each other, as was checked explicitly in
[
40
]
.

6
Stable envelopes and
\bm{R}
-matrix from gauge theory:

the computation

6.1
Elliptic stable envelope

We are now in a position to compute the matrix elements of the stable envelope of
\mathfrak{sl}(1|1)
elliptic spin chains. For the purpose of BPS computations, we can shrink the interval
[y_{-},y_{+}]
to a point, resulting in an effective 2d
{\mathcal{N}}=(0,2)
theory on
\mathbb{E}_{\tau}
. Then the 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
partition function on
{[y_{-},y_{+}]\times\mathbb{E}_{\tau}}
is given by the elliptic genus of this effective 2d theory. To derive the field content of this effective 2d theory, we need to look at the intervals
[y_{-},0]
and
[0,y_{+}]
, in addition to their union, and determine which 3d multiplets have compatible boundary conditions at both ends of all intervals where it is defined. If any field living on
[y_{\text{start}},y_{\text{end}}]
has opposite boundary conditions at
y_{\text{start}}
and
y_{\text{end}}
then it vanishes in the effective theory. A 3d multiplet that has the same boundary condition at both ends of an interval survives the collision of boundaries and decomposes into 2d
{\mathcal{N}}=(0,2)
multiplets. Of these
(0,2)
multiplets, the ones with Dirichlet boundary conditions are simply frozen to their boundary values. The remaining
(0,2)
multiplets with Neumann boundary conditions, along with the theory
\Upsilon_{\mathfrak{C}_{1}}(p_{1})
at the past massive boundary, comprise the field content of the effective 2d theory. Note that throughout the computation of the stable envelope, we use the specific chamber (
5.41
) to write down formulas.

The vector fields that remain massless in the vacuum
p_{1}
are part of the theory along the entire interval
[y_{-},y_{+}]
and they have opposite boundary conditions at
\left|\mathscr{B}_{\mathbb{L}(p_{2})}(p_{2})\right\rangle
(Dirichlet) and
\left\langle\mathscr{N}_{\mathfrak{C}_{1}}(p_{1})\right|
(Neumann). These vector fields, therefore, do not contribute to the elliptic genus. All the vector fields receive Dirichlet boundary condition at
\left|\mathscr{B}_{\mathbb{L}(p_{2})}(p_{2})\right\rangle
and all the vector fields with positive mass in the vacuum
p_{1}
receive Dirichlet at the Janus interface. In the IR, these vector multiplets with positive masses in
p_{1}
are only part of the massless theory along
y>0
and therefore they survive. They decompose into 2d
{\mathcal{N}}=(0,2)
vector and adjoint chiral multiplets. Dirichlet for 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
vector multiplet imposes Dirichlet and Neumann on its constituent 2d vector and 2d adjoint chiral multiplets respectively
[
43
]
. Contributions from these adjoint chirals to the elliptic genus are

\mathbb{V}(p_{1}):=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}a,b=1\\
p_{1}(a)>p_{1}(b)\end{subarray}}^{N}\frac{q^{-1/12}}{\vartheta\bigl(s_{a}s_{b}^{-1}\bigr)}.

(6.1)

These 2d chirals are not charged under any global symmetry group.

Next, let us look at all the 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
chiral multiplets that survive with Dirichlet boundary conditions. Multiplets receiving Dirichlet at
\left|\mathscr{N}_{\mathfrak{C}_{1}}(p_{1})\right\rangle
are in
\mathbb{L}^{\perp}_{\mathfrak{C}_{1}}(p_{1})=\mathbf{M}_{\mathfrak{C}_{1};-}(p_{1})\oplus\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{0}(p_{1})
((
5.3.2
) and (
5.38
)). But everything in
\mathbf{M}_{\mathfrak{C}_{1};-}(p_{1})
receives Neumann at the Janus (
5.46
) and therefore vanishes in the effective 2d theory. Multiplets in
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{0}(p_{1})
extends unconstrained across the Janus and intersects with the multiplets
\mathbb{L}(p_{2})
receving Dirichlet at
\left\langle\mathscr{B}_{\mathbb{L}(p_{2})}(p_{2})\right|
. Similarly, everything in
\mathbf{M}_{\mathfrak{C}_{1};+}(p_{1})
receives Dirichlet at the Janus and intersects with multiplets receiving Dirichlet at
\left\langle\mathscr{B}_{\mathbb{L}(p_{2})}(p_{2})\right|
. Therefore, 3d chiral multiplets surviving the collision of boundaries with Dirichlet are from

\mathbb{L}(p_{2})\cap\bigl(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{0}(p_{1})\oplus\mathbf{M}_{\mathfrak{C}_{1};+}(p_{1})\bigr)={\mathcal{R}}\cap\bigl(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{0}(p_{1})\oplus{\mathcal{R}}_{\mathfrak{C}_{1};+}(p_{1})\oplus\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{\mathfrak{C}_{1};+}(p_{1})\bigr)={\mathcal{R}}_{\mathfrak{C}_{1};+}(p_{1}).

Dirichlet on a 3d chiral multiplet means Dirichlet and Neumann on its constituent 2d
{\mathcal{N}}=(0,2)
chiral and fermi multiplets respectively. Fields with Dirichlet are frozen at their boundary values in the effective 2d theory and the remaining fermi multiplets contribute to the elliptic genus

\mathbb{M}_{\text{fe}}(p_{1}):=\prod_{a=1}^{N}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
i<p_{1}(a)\end{subarray}}^{L}q^{1/12}\vartheta\bigl(s_{a}x_{i}^{-1}\hbar^{1/2}\bigr).

Now we look for the 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
chiral multiplets with Neumann boundary condition contributing to the effective 2d theory. At
\left|\mathscr{N}_{\mathfrak{C}_{1}}(p_{1})\right\rangle
all fields in
\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{C}_{1}}(p_{1})=\mathbf{M}_{\mathfrak{C}_{1};+}\oplus{\mathcal{R}}_{0}(p_{1})
get Neumann. Only
{\mathcal{R}}_{0}(p_{1})
passes through the Janus unconstrained. On the interval
[0,y_{+}]
all fields with negative masses in the vacuum
p_{1}
receive Neumann boundary condition at the Janus interface. Taking the intersection of all these fields with the fields receiving Neumann at
\left\langle\mathscr{B}_{\mathbb{L}}(p_{2})\right|
, we find

\mathbb{L}^{\perp}(p_{2})\cap({\mathcal{R}}_{0}(p_{1})\oplus\mathbf{M}_{\mathfrak{C}_{1};-}(p_{1}))=\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}\cap\bigl({\mathcal{R}}_{0}(p_{1})\oplus{\mathcal{R}}_{\mathfrak{C}_{1};-}(p_{1})\oplus\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{\mathfrak{C}_{1};-}(p_{1})\bigr)=\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{\mathfrak{C}_{1};-}(p_{1}).

Neumann on a 3d chiral multiplet means Neumann and Dirichlet on its constituent 2d
{\mathcal{N}}=(0,2)
chiral and fermi multiplets respectively
[
43
]
. These 2d chirals contribute to the elliptic genus

\mathbb{M}_{\text{ch}}(p_{1}):=\prod_{a=1}^{N}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
i<p_{1}(a)\end{subarray}}^{L}\frac{q^{-1/12}}{\vartheta\bigl(s_{a}^{-1}x_{i}\hbar^{1/2}\bigr)}.

Lastly, we have the contribution (
5.42
) of the boundary theory
\Upsilon_{\mathfrak{C}_{1}}(p_{1})
to the elliptic genus. Putting all these together, we find the (unnormalized) elliptic stable envelope

\widetilde{\mathbf{Stab}}_{\mathfrak{C}_{1}}(p)=\text{Sym}_{S_{N}}\left(\mathbb{V}(p)\mathbb{M}_{\text{fe}}(p)\mathbb{M}_{\text{ch}}(p)\mathbb{W}(\Upsilon_{\mathfrak{C}_{1}}(p))\right).

(6.2)

The symmetrization is over the gauge fugacities. In addition to the maximal torus
\U(1)^{N}
, the Weyl group
S_{N}
of
\U(N)
also remains as gauge symmetry at the massive vacuum
p_{1}
and it acts on the gauge fugacities by permuting them.

Let us define

\bm{f}_{\mathfrak{C}_{1},m}(s,\bm{x},\hbar,z):=\Biggl(\prod_{i<m}\vartheta(sx_{i})\Biggr)\frac{\vartheta\bigl(sx_{m}z\hbar^{m-L}\bigr)}{\vartheta\bigl(z\hbar^{m-L}\bigr)}\Biggl(\prod_{i>m}\vartheta(sx_{i}\hbar)\Biggr).

(6.3)

Then the stable envelope (
6.2
) becomes

\widetilde{\mathbf{Stab}}_{\mathfrak{C}_{1}}(p)=\frac{q^{-\binom{N}{2}/12}\vartheta(\tilde{\hbar})^{N}}{\prod_{a,i}\vartheta\bigl(s_{a}\tilde{x}_{i}\tilde{\hbar}\bigr)}\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C}_{1}}(p),

where

\displaystyle\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C}_{1}}(p):={}

\displaystyle(-1)^{\sum_{a}\#(i<p(a))}

\displaystyle\times\text{Sym}_{S_{N}}\Biggl[\Biggl(\prod_{a}\bm{f}_{\mathfrak{C}_{1},p(a)}(s_{a},\tilde{\bm{x}},\tilde{\hbar},\tilde{z})\Biggr)\Biggl(\prod_{p(a)>p(b)}\frac{1}{\vartheta\bigl(s_{a}s_{b}^{-1}\bigr)}\Biggr)\Biggr],

(6.4)

and we have also redefined the fugacities as

\tilde{x}_{i}:=x^{-1}_{i}\hbar^{\frac{1}{2}},\qquad\tilde{\hbar}:=\hbar^{-1},\qquad\tilde{z}:=z\hbar^{-\frac{L}{2}}.

(6.5)

These redefinitions and the specific form of the stable envelope in terms of the function (
6.3
) are merely to facilitate comparison with analogous known formulas coming from BPS computations in 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=4
SQCDs
[
40
]
or from the theory of elliptic stable envelopes on complex symplectic varieties
[
2
]
that correspond to bosonic XYZ spin chains.

Remark 6.1

(normalization of the stable envelopes)
.

The overall factor in
\widetilde{\mathbf{Stab}}_{\mathfrak{C}_{1}}(p)
is independent of the choice of the chamber
\mathfrak{C}
and the vacuum
p
. This makes it ambiguous in the context of defining stable envelopes since we can change it by normalizing all the stable envelopes simultaneously. Only the relative normalization of the stable envelopes associated to different vacua and different chambers is meaningful. We have therefore defined the normalized stable envelope
\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C}_{1}}(p)
without any leading factor. This normalized version is used in Section
3.4
to check that it satisfies all formal properties of elliptic stable envelopes for
\mathfrak{sl}(1|1)
spin chains. Note however that, the overall sign from (
6.4
) is missing in (
3.6
) due to a slightly different choice of normalization for the stable envelopes, see footnote
8
.

In the expression (
6.4
), we have not yet fully imposed the boundary condition
\mathscr{B}_{\mathbb{L}(p_{2})}(p_{2})
. We still need to impose the screening condition (
5.27
) associated to the fixed point
p_{2}
. Doing so completes the computation of the 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
interval partition function, providing us with the matrix elements of the stable envelope

\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C}_{1}}(p_{2},p_{1})=\left\langle\mathscr{B}_{\mathbb{L}(p_{2})}(p_{2})\right|{\mathcal{J}}(m,0)\left|\mathscr{N}_{\mathfrak{C}_{1}}(p_{1})\right\rangle=\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C}_{1}}(p_{1})\big|_{\bm{s}=\bm{s}^{(p_{2})}}.

The specific values of the mass
m
is not important as long as it belongs to the chamber
\mathfrak{C}_{1}
.

Remark 6.2

(SQED and comparison with 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=4
/bosonic stable envelopes)
.

The difference between the quiver (
3(b)
) we used to compute elliptic
\mathfrak{sl}(1|1)
stable envelope and the quiver used in
[
40
]
to compute the elliptic
\mathfrak{sl}(2)
stable envelope is the lack of a self-loop at the gauge node in the
\mathfrak{sl}(1|1)
-quiver. In field theory, this simply means the absence of the 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
adjoint chiral multiplet, which is necessarily a part of the 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=4
vector multiplet. In the computation of the last section, the 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=4
vector multiplet contribution to the elliptic genus would involve
\mathbb{V}(p)
from (
6.1
) along with the contributions from this adjoint chiral. This is the only difference between the
\mathfrak{sl}(1|1)
and
\mathfrak{sl}(2)
cases. Notice then, in the 1-magnon sector, i.e., for SQEDs, there is no distinction between the two cases. Because the vector multiplet, as well as any adjoint chiral multiplet, do not contribute anything to the elliptic genus in abelian theories. Thus, the
\mathfrak{sl}(1|1)
stable envelopes and the
R
-matrix in the 1-magnon sector must coincide with those of the
\mathfrak{sl}(2)
spin chains. More generally, the following two quivers will always lead to the same stable envelopes, assuming that the hidden parts of the quivers coincide:

Another way of saying this is that in a magnon sector with magnon number 1 assigned to a simple root, the stable envelopes (and consequently the
R
-matrix) will remain invariant if we change the parity of the simple root. The equivalence between even and odd gauge nodes in the case of abelian gauge groups was also pointed out in Example
3.6
from a more mathematical perspective.

6.2
\bm{R}
-matrix for elliptic
\bm{\mathfrak{sl}(1|1)}
spin chains

From the perspective of integrable systems, we are interested in computing the
R
-matrix for supersymmetric XYZ spin chains, not just the stable envelope. As stated in (
3.12
),
R
-matrix is a composition of a stable envelope and an inverse stable envelope.

The action of an
R
-matrix on a spin chain preserves magnon numbers. As a result, an
R
-matrix takes a block diagonal form, one block for each sector with fixed magnon numbers. We focus on the
\mathfrak{sl}(1|1)
spin chain with two fundamental spins.
25
25

25

The spin chain can be arbitrarily large, but the
R
-matrix always acts on the tensor product of two representations. So for the purpose of computing the
R
-matrix it suffices to consider just two sites. While testing Yang–Baxter we will have to extend the
R
-matrix to act trivially on a third site as well.
The fundamental representation of
\mathfrak{sl}(1|1)
is
\mathbb{C}^{1|1}
, where the lowest weight state is bosonic, and the only excited state is fermionic. Thus, the tensor product of two such representations is four complex dimensional, consisting of a bosonic one-dimensional subspace of magnon number 0, a fermionic two-dimensional subspace of magnon number 1, and a bosonic one-dimensional subspace of magnon number 2. The
R
-matrix also decomposes as

\mathbf{R}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{R}^{(0)}&&\\
&\mathbf{R}^{(1)}&\\
&&\mathbf{R}^{(2)}\end{pmatrix},

(6.6)

where the superscript refers to the magnon number.

The gauge theory dual of a spin chain with two fundamental spins for
\mathfrak{sl}(1|1)
corresponds to a 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
SQCD with
\SU(2)
flavor symmetry. Each magnon sector corresponds to a different unitary gauge group, the rank of the gauge group is equal to the magnon number. The zero magnon sector thus corresponds to a trivial gauge theory and accordingly, the
R
-matrix in the 0-magnon sector is always trivial

\mathbf{R}^{(0)}_{\mathfrak{C}_{2}\leftarrow\mathfrak{C}_{1}}=1.

(6.7)

In the above formula,
\mathfrak{C}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{C}_{2}
are the two chambers for the Cartan of
\mathfrak{su}(2)
. To compute the
R
-matrix, we always need stable envelopes in two different chambers. We use two real masses
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
to parameterize the Cartan subalgebra of
\mathfrak{su}(2)
with the tracelessness condition
{m_{1}+m_{2}=0}
. There are then two chambers for the masses

\mathfrak{C}_{1}=m_{1}<m_{2}
,
\mathfrak{C}_{2}=m_{1}>m_{2}
.
We computed the stable envelope for
\mathfrak{C}_{1}
in (
6.4
). The stable envelope in the other chamber can be computed following identical steps as in the previous section. Here we simply write down the answer

\displaystyle\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C}_{2}}(p):={}

\displaystyle(-1)^{\sum_{a}\#(i>p(a))}

\displaystyle\times\text{Sym}_{S_{N}}\Biggl[\Biggl(\prod_{a}\bm{f}_{\mathfrak{C}_{2},p(a)}(s_{a},\bm{x},\hbar,z)\Biggr)\Biggl(\prod_{p(a)<p(b)}\frac{1}{\vartheta\bigl(s_{a}s_{b}^{-1}\bigr)}\Biggr)\Biggr],

(6.8)

where we have defined

\bm{f}_{\mathfrak{C}_{2},m}(s,\bm{x},\hbar,z):=\Biggl(\prod_{i>m}\vartheta(sx_{i})\Biggr)\frac{\vartheta\bigl(sx_{m}z\hbar^{1-m}\bigr)}{\vartheta\bigl(z\hbar^{1-m}\bigr)}\Biggl(\prod_{i<m}\vartheta(sx_{i}\hbar)\Biggr).

Note that, in this section, all fugacities are the newly defined ones from (
6.5
), but to avoid clutter we omit the tildes.

Let us now specialize these formulas to the cases of gauge group
\U(1)
and
\U(2)
to compute the stable envelopes and the
R
-matrix in the 1 and 2-magnon sectors.

1-magnon.

The Higgs branch (see Example
5.1
) has two fixed points
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
, corresponding to the two possible maps
\{1\}\hookrightarrow\{1,2\}
mapping 1 to either 1 or 2 (cf. (
5.19
)). There is a single gauge fugacity and two screening conditions (
5.27
) for the two fixed points. We can now compute the four matrix elements of each of the two stable envelopes (
6.4
) and (
6.8
) in the two chambers, we find

\displaystyle\mathbf{Stab}^{(1)}_{\mathfrak{C}_{1}}=

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\vartheta\left(x_{1}^{-1}x_{2}\hbar\right)&0\\
\frac{\vartheta(x_{1}x^{-1}_{2}z\hbar^{-1})\vartheta(\hbar)}{\vartheta(zh^{-1})}&-\vartheta\left(x_{1}x^{-1}_{2}\right)\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle\mathbf{Stab}^{(1)}_{\mathfrak{C}_{2}}=

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}-\vartheta\left(x_{1}^{-1}x_{2}\right)&\frac{\vartheta\left(x^{-1}_{1}x_{2}z\hbar^{-1}\right)\vartheta(\hbar)}{\vartheta(zh^{-1})}\\
0&\vartheta\left(x_{1}x^{-1}_{2}\hbar\right)\end{pmatrix}.

The
R
-matrix is a simple matrix product

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}^{(1)}_{\mathfrak{C}_{2}\leftarrow\mathfrak{C}_{1}}={}

\displaystyle\mathbf{Stab}^{-1}_{\mathfrak{C}_{2}}\cdot\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C}_{1}}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\frac{-1}{\vartheta\left(x^{-1}_{1}x_{2}\hbar^{-1}\right)}\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\vartheta(x^{-1}_{1}x_{2})\vartheta(z)\vartheta(z\hbar^{-2})}{\vartheta(z\hbar^{-1})^{2}}&\frac{\vartheta(\hbar)\vartheta(x^{-1}_{1}x_{2}z\hbar^{-1})}{\vartheta(z\hbar^{-1})}\\
\frac{\vartheta(\hbar)\vartheta(x_{1}x^{-1}_{2}z\hbar^{-1})}{\vartheta(z\hbar^{-1})}&-\vartheta(x_{1}x^{-1}_{2})\end{pmatrix}.

(6.9)

To simplify the upper left component, we have used the identity

\sum_{\text{cyclic}}\vartheta(AB)\vartheta\bigl(AB^{-1}\bigr)\vartheta(C)^{2}=0.

2-magnon.

The gauge theory dual of the 2-magnon sector has gauge group
\U(2)
. There are two gauge fugacities and a unique vacuum corresponding to the unique (modulo permutation) map
\{1,2\}\hookrightarrow\{1,2\}
. The stable envelopes are just functions

\mathbf{Stab}^{(2)}_{\mathfrak{C}_{1}}=-\vartheta\bigl(x^{-1}_{1}x_{2}\hbar\bigr),\qquad\mathbf{Stab}^{(2)}_{\mathfrak{C}_{2}}=-\vartheta\bigl(x_{1}x^{-1}_{2}\hbar\bigr).

As is the
R
-matrix,

\mathbf{R}^{(2)}_{\mathfrak{C}_{2}\leftarrow\mathfrak{C}_{1}}=\mathbf{Stab}^{-1}_{\mathfrak{C}_{2}}\mathbf{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C}_{1}}=\frac{\vartheta\bigl(x^{-1}_{1}x_{2}\hbar\bigr)}{\vartheta\bigl(x_{1}x^{-1}_{2}\hbar\bigr)}.

(6.10)

Putting together (
6.7
), (
6.9
), and (
6.10
) as in (
6.6
), we find the complete
R
-matrix for the elliptic
\mathfrak{sl}(1|1)
spin chain

\mathbf{R}_{\mathfrak{C}_{2}\leftarrow\mathfrak{C}_{1}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c|cc|c}1&0&0&0\\
\hline\cr 0&-\frac{\vartheta(x^{-1}_{1}x_{2})\vartheta(z)\vartheta(z\hbar^{-2})}{\vartheta(x^{-1}_{1}x_{2}\hbar^{-1})\vartheta(z\hbar^{-1})^{2}}&\frac{\vartheta(\hbar)\vartheta(x^{-1}_{1}x_{2}z\hbar^{-1})}{\vartheta(x_{1}x^{-1}_{2}\hbar)\vartheta(z\hbar^{-1})}&0\\[6.0pt]
0&\frac{\vartheta(\hbar)\vartheta(x_{1}x^{-1}_{2}z\hbar^{-1})}{\vartheta(x_{1}x^{-1}_{2}\hbar)\vartheta(z\hbar^{-1})}&-\frac{\vartheta(x^{-1}_{1}x_{2})}{\vartheta(x^{-1}_{1}x_{2}\hbar^{-1})}&0\\[4.0pt]
\hline\cr 0&0&0&\frac{\vartheta(x^{-1}_{1}x_{2}\hbar)}{\vartheta(x_{1}x^{-1}_{2}\hbar)}\end{array}\right).

(6.11)

Here
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
are referred to as the spectral parameters associated to the two sites,
z
as the dynamical parameter, and
\hbar
as the quantization parameter. This
R
-matrix solves the dynamical Yang–Baxter equation for
\mathfrak{sl}(1|1)
spin chains (
3.19
). Note that, relative to the
R
-matrix presented in (
3.18
), the signs in front of the two diagonal entries in the 1-magnon sector are different in the above formula. This is due to the slightly different choice of normalization of the stable envelopes, as pointed out in Remark
6.1
.

7
2d and 1d avatars of elliptic stable envelopes and the
\bm{R}
-matrix

This section is devoted to taking the 3d
\rightarrow
2d and 2d
\rightarrow
1d reduction of elliptic stable envelopes. In Section
7.1
, we take the 3d
\rightarrow
2d reduction, which produces the K-theoretic stable envelopes and the trigonometric
R
-matrix for
\mathfrak{sl}(1|1)
. The 2d
\rightarrow
1d reduction, which produces cohomological stable envelopes and the rational
R
-matrix, is the subject of Section
7.2
. In the case of cohomological stable envelopes and the rational
R
-matrix for
\mathfrak{sl}(1|1)
, we compare our result with that of Rimányi and Rozansky in
[
117
]
and find perfect agreement. The discussion in this section is based on Section
3.9
and also the physical description in
[
40
, Section 2.1]
.

7.1
The K-theory limit

In this section, we reduce the formulas for elliptic stable envelopes to two dimensions and give a prediction for K-theoretic stable envelopes associated with Lie superalgebra
\mathfrak{sl}(1|1)
.

Procedure for 3d
\bm{\rightarrow}
2d reduction.
In Section
4.2
, we explained the parameters in three-dimensional theory on
I\times\mathbb{E}_{\tau}
. One could shrink one of the cycles of
\mathbb{E}_{\tau}
, corresponding to
\tau\rightarrow 0
, to get a two-dimensional theory
S^{1}_{\text{A}}\times I
. The procedure of going from three to two dimensions is that we shrink the B-cycles of
\mathbb{E}_{\tau}
. This limit could be better handled if we take our parameters to take value in the
S
-transformed elliptic curve
\mathbb{E}_{-\frac{1}{\tau}}
. Then, the two-dimensional limit is given by

\tau\rightarrow 0
,
q^{-\frac{2\pi\mathfrak{i}}{\tau}}\rightarrow 0
.
In this limit, the elliptic curve becomes nodal and the smooth locus is isomorphic to
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
. It turns out that the K-theoretic stable envelope depends on the choice of a slope. This makes the behavior of elliptic Kähler parameters in the
q\rightarrow 0
limit more subtle. Defining the slope parameter as (
3.22
) such that
\mathsf{s}
is generic (i.e., it belongs to an alcove). The precise procedure to take the K-theory limit is explained in Section
3.9
.

In the case of
\mathcal{N}=2
SQCD, we need two results for deriving the K-theoretic stable envelopes:

(1)

the behavior of this function in the limit
q\rightarrow 0
, that can be deduced from its very definition (
5.43
)

\lim_{q\rightarrow 0}\vartheta(t;q)=t^{\frac{1}{2}}-t^{-\frac{1}{2}}
.

(2)

and that
[
2
, equation (2)]

\lim_{q\rightarrow 0}\left.\left(\frac{\vartheta(t_{1}z;q)}{\vartheta(t_{2}z;q)}\right)\right|_{z=q^{\mathsf{s}}}=\left(\frac{t_{1}}{t_{2}}\right)^{\lfloor\mathsf{s}\rfloor+\frac{1}{2}}.

(7.1)

Finally, we note that all the holonomies introduced in Section
4.2
are still multiplicative but are now valued in
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
.

K-theoretic stable envelopes for
\bm{\mathfrak{sl}(1|1)}
.

We now perform a 3d
\rightarrow
2d reduction of the elliptic stable envelope, which gives us the K-theoretic stable envelopes.

From the definition of the function
\bm{f}_{\mathfrak{C}_{1},m}
(
6.3
) and (
7.1
), we find that

\lim_{q\rightarrow 0}\Biggl[\frac{\vartheta\bigl(s_{a}x_{p(a)}\hbar^{-(L-p(a))}z\bigr)}{\vartheta\bigl(\hbar^{-(L-p(a))}z\bigr)}\Bigg|_{z=q^{\mathsf{s}}}\Biggr]=(s_{a}x_{p(a)})^{\lfloor{\mathsf{s}}\rfloor+\frac{1}{2}}.

Hence,

\displaystyle\lim_{q\rightarrow 0}\bm{f}_{\mathfrak{C}_{1},m}(s,\bm{x},\hbar,z)

\displaystyle=\biggl(\hbar^{\frac{\#(i>p(a))}{2}}\prod_{i}(sx_{i})^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigg)(sx_{m})^{\lfloor\mathsf{s}\rfloor}\prod_{i<m}\bigl(1-(sx_{i})^{-1}\bigr)\prod_{i>m}\bigl(1-(sx_{i}\hbar)^{-1}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\biggl(\hbar^{\frac{\#(i>p(a))}{2}}\prod_{i}(sx_{i})^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigg)\mathsf{f}_{\mathfrak{C}_{1},\mathsf{s},m}(s,\bm{x},\hbar),

where we have defined

\mathsf{f}_{\mathfrak{C}_{1},\mathsf{s},m}(s,\bm{x},\hbar):=(sx_{m})^{\lfloor{\mathsf{s}}\rfloor}\prod_{i<m}\bigl(1-(sx_{i})^{-1}\bigr)\prod_{i>m}\bigl(1-(sx_{i}\hbar)^{-1}\bigr).

Furthermore,

\lim_{q\rightarrow 0}\left(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}a>b\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\vartheta\bigl(s_{a}s_{b}^{-1}\bigr)}\right)=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}a>b\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\bigl(s_{a}s_{b}^{-1}\bigr)^{\frac{1}{2}}-\bigl(s_{a}s_{b}^{-1}\bigr)^{-\frac{1}{2}}}.

Finally, we know from (
3.23
) that we need to multiply the result by two factors: (1) the inverse of the square root of the determinant of the partial polarization
(\det\text{Pol}_{\mathsf{X}})^{-\frac{1}{2}}
for
\mathsf{X}=\mathcal{M}_{H}(N,L)
, which is given by the product of fugacities of the quarks
Q

(\det\text{Pol}_{\mathsf{X}})^{-\frac{1}{2}}=\prod_{a,i}\frac{1}{\bigl(s_{a}x_{i}^{-1}\hbar^{1/2}\bigr)^{\frac{1}{2}}}.

However, we need to implement the redefinition of fugacities according to (
6.5
). Therefore,

(\det\text{Pol}_{\mathsf{X}})^{-\frac{1}{2}}=\prod_{a,i}\frac{1}{(s_{a}x_{i})^{\frac{1}{2}}},

(2) the square root of the determinant of the polarization of the fixed loci
(\det\text{Pol}_{\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}})^{\frac{1}{2}}
, which in our case is trivial

(\det\text{Pol}_{\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{A}}})^{+\frac{1}{2}}=1
.
Putting these results together, and using (
3.23
), we see that
26
26

26

The K-theory limit of elliptic stable envelope for the resolved determinantal variety
\mathcal{M}_{H}(N,L)
has also been computed in (
3.26
) using a different polarization as explained there. Compared to (
3.26
), the expression (
7.2
) contains the extra factor
(-1)^{\sum_{a}\#(i<p(a))|}
, which is the result of a choice of different polarization in the BPS computation of elliptic stable envelope.

\displaystyle\mathsf{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C}_{1},\mathsf{s}}(p)={}

\displaystyle(-1)^{\sum_{a}\#(i<p(a))}\hbar^{\frac{1}{2}\sum_{a}\#(i>p(a))}

\displaystyle\times\text{Sym}_{S_{N}}\left[\left(\prod_{a=1}^{N}\mathsf{f}_{\mathfrak{C}_{1},\mathsf{s},p(a)}(s_{a},\bm{x},\hbar)\right)\cdot\Biggl(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}a>b\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\bigl(s_{a}s_{b}^{-1}\bigr)^{\frac{1}{2}}-\bigl(s_{a}s_{b}^{-1}\bigr)^{-\frac{1}{2}}}\Biggr)\right].

(7.2)

By Lemma
3.2
, this is the K-theoretic stable envelope for the Lie superalgebra
\mathfrak{sl}(1|1)
. Notice that it is manifestly a locally-constant function of
\mathsf{s}
, which is a characteristic feature of K-theoretic stable envelopes as a function of
\mathsf{s}
.

Trigonometric
\bm{R}
-matrix for
\bm{\mathfrak{sl}(1|1)}
.

We can now perform the reduction of the
\mathfrak{sl}(1|1)
elliptic
R
-matrix (
6.11
) to construct the
\mathfrak{sl}(1|1)
trigonometric
R
-matrix. Let us define the function
\mathsf{G}(x):=x^{\frac{1}{2}}-x^{-\frac{1}{2}}
.
The result of the reduction is

\mathsf{R}_{\mathfrak{C}_{2}\leftarrow\mathfrak{C}_{1}}(u)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c|cc|c}1&0&0&0\\
\hline\cr 0&\frac{\mathsf{G}(u)}{\mathsf{G}(u^{-1}\hbar)}&u^{\lfloor\mathsf{s}\rfloor+\frac{1}{2}}\frac{\mathsf{G}(\hbar)}{\mathsf{G}(u^{-1}\hbar)}&0\\[6.0pt]
0&u^{-\lfloor\mathsf{s}\rfloor-\frac{1}{2}}\frac{\mathsf{G}(\hbar)}{\mathsf{G}(u^{-1}\hbar)}&\frac{\mathsf{G}(u)}{\mathsf{G}(u^{-1}\hbar)}&0\\[4.0pt]
\hline\cr 0&0&0&\frac{\mathsf{G}(u\hbar)}{\mathsf{G}(u^{-1}\hbar)}\end{array}\right).

(7.3)

One can check that it satisfies the trigonometric version of Yang–Baxter equations given by

\mathsf{R}_{12}(u/v)\mathsf{R}_{13}(u/w)\mathsf{R}_{23}(v/w)=\mathsf{R}_{23}(v/w)\mathsf{R}_{13}(u/w)\mathsf{R}_{12}(u/v).

7.2
The cohomology limit

In the previous section, we have constructed the K-theoretic stable envelopes and the trigonometric
R
-matrix for Lie superalgebra
\mathfrak{sl}(1|1)
by performing a 3d
\rightarrow
2d reduction. It is then natural to consider a further 2d
\rightarrow
1d reduction to construct the cohomological stable envelopes and the rational
R
-matrix for
\mathfrak{sl}(1|1)
. Fortunately, the mathematical construction of cohomological stable envelope for Lie superalgebra
\mathfrak{sl}(1|1)
is available in the literature
[
117
]
, and we can compare our result. In this section, we perform this 2d
\rightarrow
1d reduction, and we recover the cohomological stable envelopes and the geometrical
R
-matrix of Rimányi and Rozansky.

Procedure for 2d
\bm{\rightarrow}
1d reduction.

We need to know what happens by going from 2d to 1d. In the 3d
\rightarrow
2d, reduction, one considers the reduction of the theory on one of the circles of the elliptic curve. Similarly, the 2d
\rightarrow
1d result is obtained by the reduction of the theory on the remaining circle. This amounts to the following substitution of gauge and flavor holonomies
27
27

27

The precise statement in going from K-theoretic stable envelope to the cohomological one is given in (
3.25
).
(see Section
3.9
and
[
40
, Section 2.1]
)

(s_{a},x_{i},\hbar)\mapstochar\rightarrow\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\bigl({\rm e}^{\epsilon s_{a}},{\rm e}^{\epsilon x_{i}},{\rm e}^{\epsilon\hbar}\bigr),

(7.4)

On the other hand, the holonomy associated with the topological symmetry defines Käher parameter in three dimensions. Upon 3d
\rightarrow
2d reduction, this becomes a theta angle, i.e., the slope parameter, as explained in Section
7.1
. By 2d
\rightarrow
1d reduction, it turns out that this parameter completely disappears
[
40
, p. 16]
. This is consistent with the fact that cohomological stable envelopes are not dependent on extra choices like a slope. Therefore, one should (1) use (
7.4
) to replace multiplicative holonomies with additive parameters in the
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
limit by throwing away terms of order
\mathcal{O}\bigl(\epsilon^{2}\bigr)
, and (2) discard all
\mathsf{s}
-dependent factors. In this process, all gauge and flavor holonomies become additive and
\mathbb{C}
-valued.

Cohomological stable envelope for
\bm{\mathfrak{sl}(1|1)}
.

We can now perform the reduction explicitly. Different factors of (
7.2
) are reduced as follows

\mathsf{f}_{\mathfrak{C}_{1},\mathsf{s},m}\quad\rightarrow\quad\mathrm{f}_{\mathfrak{C}_{1},m}(s,\bm{x},\hbar):=\prod_{i<m}(s+x_{i})\prod_{i>m}(s+x_{i}+\hbar),

and

\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}a>b\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\bigl(s_{a}s_{b}^{-1}\bigr)^{\frac{1}{2}}-\bigl(s_{a}s_{b}^{-1}\bigr)^{-\frac{1}{2}}}\quad\rightarrow\quad\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}a>b\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{s_{a}-s_{b}}.

Therefore, according to Lemma
3.2
, we end up with the cohomological stable envelope
28
28

28

The cohomology limit of K-theoretic stable envelope for the resolved determinantal variety
\mathcal{M}_{H}(N,L)
has also been computed in (
3.27
) using a different polarization as explained there. Compared to (
3.27
), the expression (
7.5
) contains the extra factor
(-1)^{\sum_{a}\#(i<p(a))|}
, which is the result of a choice of different polarization in the BPS computation of elliptic stable envelope.

\mathrm{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C}_{1}}(p)=(-1)^{\sum_{a}\#(i<p(a))}\text{Sym}_{S_{N}}\left[\left(\prod_{a=1}^{N}\mathrm{f}_{\mathfrak{C}_{1},m}(s,\bm{x},\hbar)\right)\cdot\Biggl(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}a>b\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{s_{a}-s_{b}}\Biggr)\right].

(7.5)

Rational
\bm{R}
-matrix for
{\bm{\mathfrak{sl}(1|1)$}.}Wecannowperformthereductionofthe
sl
(1—1)
trigonometric
R
-matrix\eqref{eq:gl(1|1) trigonometric r-matrix}toconstructthe
sl
(1—1)
rational
R
-matrix.Theresultis\begin{equation}\mathrm{R}_{\mathfrak{C}_{2}\leftarrow\mathfrak{C}_{1}}(u)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c|cc|c}1&0&0&0\\
\hline\cr 0&\frac{u}{\hbar-u}&\frac{\hbar}{\hbar-u}&0\\[4.0pt]
0&\frac{\hbar}{\hbar-u}&\frac{u}{\hbar-u}&0\\[4.0pt]

.

Appendix A

Equivariant elliptic cohomology

In this appendix, we give a brief overview of equivariant elliptic cohomology, we only list the essential ingredients that are used in this paper. We basically follow the presentation in
[
109
, Section 1.4, Appendices A and B]
. The general theory on equivariant elliptic cohomology can be found in
[
58
,
59
,
60
,
62
,
87
,
126
]
,

Let
q
be a nonzero complex number such that
|q|<1
, then take the elliptic curve
\mathbb{E}=\mathbb{C}^{\times}/q^{\mathbb{Z}}
. For simplicity, we choose the elliptic curve
\mathbb{E}
to be general enough such that it has no complex multiplication, i.e.,
\mathrm{End}(\mathbb{E})=\mathbb{Z}
.

Fix a compact Lie group
G
and denote its complexification by
G_{\mathbb{C}}
, then the
G
-equivariant elliptic cohomology is a functor

\{\text{pairs of $G$-spaces}\}\rightarrow\{\text{graded super schemes over }\mathscr{E}_{G}\}\qquad(X,\partial X)\mapstochar\rightarrow\mathrm{Ell}_{G}(X,\partial X),

where
\mathscr{E}_{G}
is the moduli scheme of semistable principal
G_{\mathbb{C}}
-bundles of trivial topological type on the dual elliptic curve
\mathbb{E}^{\vee}
. For a torus
T
,

\mathscr{E}_{T}=\mathbb{E}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{Cochar}(T)
,
and for a compact Lie group
G
with maximal torus
T
, it is known that
[
56
]

\mathscr{E}_{G}\cong\mathscr{E}_{T}/W
,
where
W
is the Weyl group of
G
which acts on
\mathrm{Cochar}(T)
naturally. When
G
is simple and simply-connected, it is known that
[
86
]

\mathscr{E}_{G}
is isomorphic to the weighted projective space
\mathbb{P}(1,g_{1},\dots,g_{r})
, where
g_{i}
are coefficients in the decomposition

\theta^{\vee}=\sum_{i}g_{i}\alpha_{i}^{\vee}
,
of the dual of highest root into simple coroots. The map
\mathrm{Ell}_{G}
is functorial with respect to the change of groups, i.e., for a group homomorphism
G\rightarrow G^{\prime}
, there is a natural transformation
\mathrm{Ell}_{G}\rightarrow\mathrm{Ell}_{G^{\prime}}
. In the following, we focus on the case when
\partial X=\varnothing
and
X
is a complex algebraic variety.

The graded super scheme means the structure sheaf
\mathscr{O}^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{Ell}_{G}(X)}=\bigoplus_{d\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathscr{O}^{d}_{\mathrm{Ell}_{G}(X)}
is graded and graded-commutative. It is known that each homogeneous piece
\mathscr{O}^{d}_{\mathrm{Ell}_{G}(X)}
is a coherent sheaf on
\mathscr{E}_{G}
. Moreover,
\mathrm{Ell}_{G}
is two-periodic

\mathscr{O}^{d}_{\mathrm{Ell}_{G}(X)}=\mathscr{O}^{d+2}_{\mathrm{Ell}_{G}(X)}\otimes\omega
,
\omega=T^{*}_{0}E
.
Since the stable envelope is in degree zero, we will focus on
\mathscr{O}^{0}_{\mathrm{Ell}_{G}(X)}
, which is the structure sheaf of a scheme, and it is finite over
\mathscr{E}_{G}

[
60
]
. In the later discussions, we use the same notation
\mathrm{Ell}_{G}(X)
to denote the degree zero piece.

A.1

Chern class

If
H
is another compact Lie group, and
P\rightarrow X
is a
G
-equivariant principal
H
-bundle, then
P
induces a map

c\colon\mathrm{Ell}_{G}(X)\rightarrow\mathrm{Ell}_{H}(\mathrm{pt})=\mathscr{E}_{H}
,
called the
Chern class
map. For a vector bundle
V
of rank
r
, which is equivalent to a principal
\mathrm{GL}_{r}
-bundle, the
Thom line bundle
associated to
V
is defined as

\Theta(V):=c^{*}\mathscr{O}(D_{\Theta}),\qquad D_{\Theta}=\{0\}+S^{r-1}\mathbb{E}\subset S^{r}\mathbb{E}=\mathscr{E}_{\mathrm{GL}_{r}}.

\Theta(V)
inherits a canonical section
\vartheta(V)
from the effective divisor
D_{\Theta}
.
\Theta\colon V\rightarrow\Theta(V)
descends to a group homomorphism
K_{G}(X)\rightarrow\mathrm{Pic}(\mathrm{Ell}_{G}(X))
, this follows from

\Theta(V)=\Theta(V_{1})\otimes\Theta(V_{1})\qquad\text{for short exact sequence }0\rightarrow V_{1}\rightarrow V\rightarrow V_{2}\rightarrow 0.

Note that the canonical section simply multiplies:
\vartheta(V)=\vartheta(V_{1})\vartheta(V_{2})
. We also have

\Theta\bigl(V^{\vee}\bigr)\cong\Theta(V)
,
such that the canonical section picks up a sign
\vartheta\bigl(V^{\vee}\bigr)=(-1)^{\operatorname{rk}V}\vartheta(V)
.

For vector bundles
V_{1}
,
V_{2}
of ranks
r_{1}
,
r_{2}
,
\Theta(V_{1}\otimes V_{2})
is the pullback of
\mathscr{O}(D_{\Theta})
on
S^{r_{1}r_{2}}E
via the composition of maps

\mathrm{Ell}_{G}(X)\overset{c_{1}\times c_{2}}{\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow}S^{r_{1}}\mathbb{E}\times S^{r_{2}}\mathbb{E}\overset{m}{\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow}S^{r_{1}r_{2}}\mathbb{E},

where
m
is induced from the multiplication of elliptic curve

m\colon\ (\{x_{1},\dots,x_{r_{1}}\},\{y_{1},\dots,y_{r_{2}}\})\mapstochar\rightarrow\{x_{i}+y_{j}\}.

In particular, for
G=1
and considering the tensor product of line bundles, the above gives rise to the formal group law of non-equivariant elliptic cohomology.

A.2

Gysin map

For a proper
G
-equivariant map
f\colon X\rightarrow Y
, assume that
f
factors as a regular embedding
{i\colon X\hookrightarrow Z}
and a smooth projection
p\colon Z\rightarrow Y
, and that both
i
and
p
are
G
-equivariant, then there exists a distinguished element

f_{\circledast}\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{O}_{\mathrm{Ell}_{G}(Y)}}(f_{*}\Theta(T_{f}),\mathscr{O}_{\mathrm{Ell}_{G}(Y)}),

where
f_{*}\colon\mathrm{Ell}_{G}(X)\rightarrow\mathrm{Ell}_{G}(Y)
is the induced map between elliptic cohomologies, and
T_{f}
is the relative tangent bundle. We call
f_{\circledast}
the Gysin map.

If
T_{f}
equals to
f^{*}V
in
K_{G}(X)
for some
V\in K_{G}(Y)
, then we denote by
[X]
the section
{\Gamma(\mathrm{Ell}_{G}(Y),\Theta(-V))}
induced by the Gysin map
f_{\circledast}\colon f_{*}\mathscr{O}_{\mathrm{Ell}_{G}(X)}\rightarrow\Theta(-V)
precomposed with the canonical map
\mathscr{O}_{\mathrm{Ell}_{G}(Y)}\rightarrow f_{*}\mathscr{O}_{\mathrm{Ell}_{G}(X)}
.

For example, let
N\rightarrow X
be a
G
-equivariant vector bundle and let
i\colon X\hookrightarrow N
be the zero section, then
f_{*}\colon\mathrm{Ell}_{G}(X)\rightarrow\mathrm{Ell}_{G}(N)
is an isomorphism of schemes due to the homotopy invariance of elliptic cohomology, and
i_{\circledast}\in\Gamma(\mathrm{Ell}_{G}(X),\Theta(N))
is the section
\vartheta(N)
.

A.3

Supports

For a section
\alpha
of a coherent sheaf
\mathscr{F}
on
\mathrm{Ell}_{G}(X)
, and a
G
-invariant open subset
j\colon U\hookrightarrow X
, we say that
\alpha
is supported on
X\setminus U
if
j^{*}(\alpha)=0
in
\mathrm{Ell}_{G}(U)
. Define the support
\mathrm{supp}(\alpha)
to be the intersection of
G
-invariant closed subset that
\alpha
is supported on.

The Gysin map can be defined for compactly supported sections. Namely for a
G
-equivariant map
f\colon X\rightarrow Y
such that it factors as a regular embedding followed by a smooth projection, then there exists a distinguished element

f_{\circledast}\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{O}_{\mathrm{Ell}_{G}(Y)}}(f_{*}\Theta(T_{f})_{c},\mathscr{O}_{\mathrm{Ell}_{G}(Y)}),

where
{\Theta(T_{f})_{c}\subset\Theta(T_{f})}
is the subsheaf of sections
\alpha
such that
f|_{\mathrm{supp}(\alpha)}
is proper.

A.4

Correspondences

Consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{2}\times X_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1}.}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pt}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{1}}

Assume that
X_{1}
is smooth, then for a pair of line bundles
\mathscr{L}_{i}\in\mathrm{Pic}(\mathrm{Ell}_{G}(X_{i}))
, and for any section

\alpha\in\Gamma\bigl(\mathrm{Ell}_{G}(X_{2}\times X_{1}),\mathscr{L}_{2}\boxtimes\bigl(\mathscr{L}_{1}^{\vee}\otimes\Theta(T_{X_{1}})\bigr)\bigr)
,
such that
\mathrm{supp}(\alpha)
is proper over
X_{2}
,
\alpha
induces a map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{1*}\mathscr{L}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{2*}\mathscr{L}_{2},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2\circledast}(\alpha p_{1}^{*}(\cdot))}

in
\mathrm{Coh}(\mathscr{E}_{G})
.

A.5

Degree of a line bundle

For a line bundle
\mathscr{L}
on an abelian variety
\mathcal{A}
, we define its degree
\deg\mathscr{L}
to be its image in the Néron–Severi group
\mathrm{NS}(\mathcal{A})=\mathrm{Pic}(\mathcal{A})/\mathrm{Pic}^{0}(\mathcal{A})
. It is known that
\mathrm{NS}(\mathcal{A})
is isomorphic to the subgroup of homomorphisms
f\in\mathrm{Hom}\bigl(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A}^{\vee}\bigr)
such that
f=f^{\vee}
, and the isomorphism is given by
34
34

34

This fact can be derived from Theorem 2 together with the remark after that in
[
92
, Section 20]
.

\mathscr{L}\mapstochar\rightarrow\bigl(\phi_{\mathscr{L}}\colon x\mapstochar\rightarrow x^{*}\mathscr{L}\otimes\mathscr{L}^{-1}\bigr)
.
Let
T
be a torus, then
\mathrm{Hom}\bigl(\mathscr{E}_{T},\mathscr{E}^{\vee}_{T}\bigr)
is isomorphic to
{\mathrm{Char}(T)^{\otimes 2}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{End}(\mathbb{E})}
. As we have assumed in the beginning,
\mathbb{E}
is chosen such that
{\mathrm{End}(\mathbb{E})=\mathbb{Z}}
, so the Néron–Severi group
\mathrm{NS}(\mathscr{E}_{T})
is isomorphic to
S^{2}\mathrm{Char}(T)
. Explicitly, any
\mu\in\mathrm{Char}(T)
gives rise to a map
\phi_{\mu}\colon\mathscr{E}_{T}\rightarrow\mathbb{E}
, then
\deg\phi_{\mu}^{*}\mathscr{O}(D_{\Theta})=\mu\otimes\mu\in S^{2}\mathrm{Char}(T)
. So for
V=\sum_{\mu}V_{\mu}\cdot\mu\in K_{T}(\mathrm{pt})
, we have

\deg\Theta(V)=\sum_{\mu}(\dim V_{\mu})\mu\otimes\mu
.

Appendix B

Notations and conventions for supersymmetry

In this appendix, we collect the notations and conventions regarding supersymmetry used in the computation of elliptic stable envelopes in Section
6
.

B.1

Conventions for spinors

For a two component
\SU(2)
spinor
\epsilon
, we write its components as
\epsilon^{\alpha}
where
\alpha=1,2
. We use
{\alpha,\beta,\ldots}
as spinor indices. Our spinors will be anti-commuting. Let
\varepsilon^{\alpha\beta}
and
\varepsilon_{\alpha\beta}
be the totally antisymmetric tensors satisfying

\varepsilon^{12}=\varepsilon_{21}=1
.
These tensors are used to raise and lower spinor indices

\epsilon_{\alpha}=\varepsilon_{\alpha\beta}\epsilon^{\beta}
,
\epsilon^{\alpha}=\varepsilon^{\alpha\beta}\epsilon_{\beta}
.
Let
\gamma^{\mu}
for
\mu=1,2,3
be the Pauli matrices

\bigl[\mathchoice{\bigl(\gamma^{1}\bigr)^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}}}_{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\bigl(\gamma^{1}\bigr)^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}}}_{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\bigl(\gamma^{1}\bigr)^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}}}_{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\bigl(\gamma^{1}\bigr)^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}}}_{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\bigr]=\begin{pmatrix}&1\\
1&\end{pmatrix},\qquad\bigl[\mathchoice{\bigl(\gamma^{2}\bigr)^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}}}_{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\bigl(\gamma^{2}\bigr)^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}}}_{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\bigl(\gamma^{2}\bigr)^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}}}_{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\bigl(\gamma^{2}\bigr)^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}}}_{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\bigr]=\begin{pmatrix}&-\text{i}\\

&-1\end{pmatrix}.

We also define the charge conjugation matrix

C=\bigl[C^{\alpha\beta}\bigr]:=-\text{i}\gamma^{2}=\begin{pmatrix}&-1\\
1&\end{pmatrix}.

In other words,
C^{\alpha\beta}=-\varepsilon^{\alpha\beta}
. Some properties of the charge conjugation matrix are

C^{2}=-1,\qquad C^{T}=C^{-1}=-C,\qquad C\gamma^{\mu}=-(\gamma^{\mu})^{T}C.

(B.1)

Contraction of spinors
\epsilon
and
\lambda
are defined as

\epsilon\lambda:=\epsilon^{\alpha}\lambda_{\alpha},

(B.2)

i.e., we use the NE-SW convention for contracting indices on anti-commuting spinors. We can think of two-component spinors as column vectors, with lowered indices, e.g.,

\epsilon=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\epsilon_{1}\\
\epsilon_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.
Then we can write the spinor bilinear (
B.2
) in matrix notation as
35
35

35

There should not be any confusion between when the matrix notation is being used in spinor bilinears, since in the matrix notation there will always be the transpose of a spinor to the left (as in the right-hand side of (
B.3
)), otherwise there will not be any transpose (as in the left-hand side of (
B.3
)).

\epsilon\lambda=\epsilon^{T}C\lambda.

(B.3)

Using the properties (
B.1
) of the charge conjugation matrix, we can derive some symmetry properties of various spinor bilinears:

\epsilon\gamma^{\mu_{1}}\cdots\gamma^{\mu_{n}}\lambda=(-1)^{\frac{n(n+1)}{2}}\lambda\gamma^{\mu_{1}}\cdots\gamma^{\mu_{n}}\epsilon.

B.2

3d
\bm{\mathcal{N}=2}
supersymmetry

The supersymmetry algebra has four supercharges which we can denote as
\mathscr{Q}_{\alpha}
,
\overline{\mathscr{Q}}_{\alpha}
with
\alpha
being a spinor index. A generic supercharge is a linear combination of these

\mathscr{Q}_{\epsilon,\overline{\epsilon}}:=\epsilon^{\alpha}\mathscr{Q}_{\alpha}+\overline{\epsilon}^{\alpha}\overline{\mathscr{Q}}_{\alpha},

(B.4)

where the coefficients
\epsilon
,
\overline{\epsilon}
are two components spinors. The variation of a field in the theory caused by the supercharge
\mathscr{Q}_{\epsilon,\overline{\epsilon}}
will be denoted
\delta_{\epsilon,\overline{\epsilon}}
.

For a Lie group
G
, the corresponding 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
vector multiplet consists of a vector field
A
, two scalars
\sigma
and
\mathsf{D}
and two spinors
\lambda
and
\overline{\lambda}
. All these fields are valued in the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
of
G
. We adopt the convention that elements of real Lie algebras are hermitian. Their supersymmetry variations are

\displaystyle\delta_{\epsilon,\overline{\epsilon}}A_{\mu}=\frac{\text{i}}{2}\bigl(\overline{\epsilon}\gamma_{\mu}\lambda-\overline{\lambda}\gamma_{\mu}\epsilon\bigr),\qquad\delta_{\epsilon,\overline{\epsilon}}\sigma=\frac{1}{2}\bigl(\overline{\epsilon}\lambda-\overline{\lambda}\epsilon\bigr),

\displaystyle\delta_{\epsilon,\overline{\epsilon}}\lambda=-\frac{1}{2}\gamma^{\mu\nu}F_{\mu\nu}\epsilon-\mathsf{D}\epsilon+\text{i}\gamma^{\mu}D_{\mu}\sigma\epsilon,\qquad\delta_{\epsilon,\overline{\epsilon}}\overline{\lambda}=-\frac{1}{2}\gamma^{\mu\nu}F_{\mu\nu}\overline{\epsilon}+\mathsf{D}\overline{\epsilon}-\text{i}\gamma^{\mu}D_{\mu}\sigma\overline{\epsilon},

\displaystyle\delta_{\epsilon,\overline{\epsilon}}\mathsf{D}=-\frac{\text{i}}{2}\overline{\epsilon}\gamma^{\mu}D_{\mu}\lambda-\frac{\text{i}}{2}D_{\mu}\overline{\lambda}\gamma^{\mu}\epsilon+\frac{\text{i}}{2}[\overline{\epsilon}\lambda,\sigma]+\frac{\text{i}}{2}\bigl[\overline{\lambda}\epsilon,\sigma\bigr].

(B.5)

Given a representation
R
of
G
we can add an
R
-valued chiral multiplet containing two scalars
\phi
and
\mathsf{F}
, and a spinor
\psi
. We also have the anti-chiral multiplet containing scalars
\overline{\phi}
,
\overline{\mathsf{F}}
, and spinor
\overline{\psi}
valued in the dual representation
R^{\vee}
. These fields transform under supersymmetry as

\displaystyle\delta_{\epsilon,\overline{\epsilon}}\phi=\overline{\epsilon}\psi,\qquad\delta_{\epsilon,\overline{\epsilon}}\overline{\phi}=\epsilon\overline{\psi},\qquad\delta_{\epsilon,\overline{\epsilon}}\psi=\text{i}\gamma^{\mu}\epsilon D_{\mu}\phi+\text{i}\epsilon\sigma\cdot\phi+\overline{\epsilon}\mathsf{F},

\displaystyle\delta_{\epsilon,\overline{\epsilon}}\overline{\psi}=\text{i}\gamma^{\mu}\overline{\epsilon}D_{\mu}\overline{\phi}-\text{i}\overline{\epsilon}\sigma\cdot\overline{\phi}+\epsilon\overline{\mathsf{F}},\qquad\delta_{\epsilon,\overline{\epsilon}}\mathsf{F}=\epsilon\bigl(\text{i}\gamma^{\mu}D_{\mu}\psi-\text{i}\sigma\cdot\psi-\text{i}\lambda\cdot\phi\bigr),

\displaystyle\delta_{\epsilon,\overline{\epsilon}}\overline{\mathsf{F}}=\overline{\epsilon}\bigl(\text{i}\gamma^{\mu}D_{\mu}\overline{\psi}+\text{i}\sigma\cdot\overline{\psi}-\text{i}\overline{\lambda}\cdot\overline{\phi}\bigr).

(B.6)

Here
\sigma\cdot
is the action of
\sigma
on whatever follows in the corresponding representation.

By computing the commutator
[\delta_{\epsilon,\overline{\epsilon}},\delta_{\eta,\overline{\eta}}]
on fields, we find the (anti)commutation relations between the supercharges

\bigl\{\mathscr{Q}_{\alpha},\overline{\mathscr{Q}}_{\beta}\bigr\}=-\text{i}\gamma^{\mu}_{\alpha\beta}D_{\mu}-\text{i}\varepsilon_{\alpha\beta}\sigma\cdot.

(B.7)

In a gauge theory, we always have a dynamical vector multiplet for the gauge group. So the last term above is an infinitesimal gauge transformation by the adjoint scalar
\sigma
of this vector multiplet. But we can also introduce background vector multiplets for flavor symmetry groups. If
G
is the gauge group and
F
the flavor group with Lie algebras
\mathfrak{g}
and
\mathfrak{f}
, respectively, then we can treat the vector multiplet from (
B.5
) as
(\mathfrak{g}\oplus\mathfrak{f})
-valued. Thus all the fields of the multiplet become sums of
\mathfrak{g}
and
\mathfrak{f}
-valued fields. In particular, we write
\sigma=\sigma_{G}+\sigma_{F}
according to this decomposition and the supersymmetry algebra (
B.7
) contains a gauge and a flavor symmetry transformation by the dynamical
\sigma_{G}
and the background
\sigma_{F}
respectively. We only turn on background values for scalar fields, namely
\sigma_{F}
and
\mathsf{D}_{F}
. Furthermore, these values are constrained by the requirement to preserve some amount of supersymmetry, i.e., to solve the BPS equations. More on BPS equations in Section
5.1.2
.

B.3

Localizing supercharge

Suppose the elliptic curve
\mathbb{E}_{\tau}
in our 3d space-time
I\times\mathbb{E}_{\tau}
has a holomorphic volume form
\text{d}z
and two 1-cycles,
S^{1}_{A}
and
S^{1}_{B}
defined by:

\oint_{S^{1}_{A}}\text{d}z=1
,
\oint_{S^{1}_{B}}\text{d}z=\tau
.
Let
y
,
\theta_{A}
and
\theta_{B}
be the real coordinates on
I
,
S^{1}_{A}
and
S^{1}_{B}
, respectively. We take the orientation defining volume form on
M
to be
\text{d}y\wedge\text{d}\theta_{A}\wedge\text{d}\theta_{B}
. This induces the volume forms
\text{d}y\wedge\text{d}\theta_{A}
on
\Sigma:=\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}_{A}
and
\text{d}\theta_{A}\wedge\text{d}\theta_{B}
on
\mathbb{E}_{\tau}
. We use holomorphic coordinates
w
and
z
on
\Sigma
and
\mathbb{E}_{\tau}
respectively such that the volume forms in real and complex coordinates are related by

\text{d}y\wedge\text{d}\theta_{A}=\frac{\text{i}}{2}\text{d}w\wedge\text{d}\overline{w}
,
\text{d}\theta_{A}\wedge\text{d}\theta_{B}=\frac{\text{i}}{2}\text{d}z\wedge\text{d}\overline{z}
.
The real and complex coordinates are related by

w=y+\text{i}\theta_{A}
,
z=\theta_{A}+\text{i}\theta_{B}
.
We denote the derivatives with respect to
y
,
\theta_{A}
and
\theta_{B}
by
\partial_{y}
,
\partial_{A}
and
\partial_{B}
, respectively. They are related to the derivatives with respect to the complex coordinates as

\partial_{w}=\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{y}-\text{i}\partial_{A})
,
\partial_{z}=\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{A}-\text{i}\partial_{B})
.
Similarly for the covariant derivatives
D_{w}
,
D_{z}
,
D_{y}
,
D_{A}
and
D_{B}
.

We can relabel our 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
supercharges
\mathscr{Q}_{\alpha}
,
\overline{\mathscr{Q}}_{\alpha}
to adjust to the complex structures of either
\Sigma
or
\mathbb{E}_{\tau}
. If we define

\displaystyle\mathscr{Q}_{+}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\mathscr{Q}_{1}+\mathscr{Q}_{2}),\qquad\mathscr{Q}_{-}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\mathscr{Q}_{1}-\mathscr{Q}_{2}),\qquad\overline{\mathscr{Q}}_{+}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\bigl(\overline{\mathscr{Q}}_{1}+\overline{\mathscr{Q}}_{2}\bigr),

\displaystyle\overline{\mathscr{Q}}_{-}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\bigl(\overline{\mathscr{Q}}_{1}-\overline{\mathscr{Q}}_{2}\bigr),

then the subscripts
\pm
refer to the chirality on
\mathbb{E}_{\tau}
and the algebra (
B.7
) reflects the complex structure of
\mathbb{E}_{\tau}

\displaystyle\bigl\{\mathscr{Q}_{+},\overline{\mathscr{Q}}_{+}\bigr\}=2D_{z},\qquad\bigl\{\mathscr{Q}_{-},\overline{\mathscr{Q}}_{-}\bigr\}=2D_{\overline{z}},\qquad\bigl\{\mathscr{Q}_{+},\overline{\mathscr{Q}}_{-}\bigr\}=\text{i}(D_{y}-\sigma),

\displaystyle\bigl\{\mathscr{Q}_{-},\overline{\mathscr{Q}}_{+}\bigr\}=\text{i}(D_{y}+\sigma).

Alternatively, if we define

\displaystyle q_{+}:=\frac{\mathscr{Q}_{1}}{\sqrt{2}},\qquad q_{-}:=-\frac{\mathscr{Q}_{2}}{\sqrt{2}},\qquad\overline{q}_{+}:=\frac{\overline{\mathscr{Q}}_{1}}{\sqrt{2}},\qquad\overline{q}_{-}:=-\frac{\overline{\mathscr{Q}}_{2}}{\sqrt{2}},

then the subscripts
\pm
refer to the chirality on
\Sigma
and the algebra (
B.7
) reflects the complex structure of
\Sigma

\displaystyle\{q_{+},\overline{q}_{+}\}=\text{i}D_{w},\qquad\{q_{-},\overline{q}_{-}\}=-\text{i}D_{\overline{w}},\qquad\{q_{+},\overline{q}_{-}\}=\frac{\text{i}}{2}(D_{B}-\sigma),

\displaystyle\{q_{-},\overline{q}_{+}\}=\frac{\text{i}}{2}(D_{B}+\sigma).

The localization results used in this note are with respect to the supercharge

\mathscr{Q}:=\mathscr{Q}_{-}+\overline{\mathscr{Q}}_{-}=q_{+}+q_{-}+\overline{q}_{+}+\overline{q}_{-},

(B.8)

which satisfies

\mathscr{Q}^{2}=2D_{\overline{z}}.

(B.9)

Furthermore, translation in the
y
-direction is
\mathscr{Q}
-exact

\bigl\{\mathscr{Q},\mathscr{Q}_{+}+\overline{\mathscr{Q}}_{+}\bigr\}=2\text{i}D_{y}.

(B.10)

From the point of view of both
\Sigma
and
\mathbb{E}_{\tau}
, the 3d
{\mathcal{N}}=2
algebra appears as the 2d
{\mathcal{N}}=(2,2)
algebra. On the elliptic curve, the supercharge
\mathscr{Q}
belongs to the
{\mathcal{N}}=(0,2)
subalgebra. On
\Sigma
, the supercharge
\mathscr{Q}
does not belong to any proper subalgebra of
(2,2)
but rather it can be related to certain deformations of both the A-model and the B-model BRST operators, as we find below.

Dimensional reduction.
By compactifying the
S^{1}_{B}
direction of the 3d space-time, we land on a 2d
{\mathcal{N}}=(2,2)
theory on
\Sigma
with four supercharges
\mathsf{Q}_{\pm}
,
\overline{\mathsf{Q}}_{\pm}
that descend from the 3d supercharges
q_{\pm}
,
\overline{q}_{\pm}
, respectively. The 2d
{\mathcal{N}}=(2,2)
supersymmetry has the usual A-model and B-model supercharges

\mathsf{Q}_{A}:=\overline{\mathsf{Q}}_{+}+\mathsf{Q}_{-}
,
\mathsf{Q}_{B}:=\overline{\mathsf{Q}}_{+}+\overline{\mathsf{Q}}_{-}
,
that are nilpotent up to gauge transformations.
36
36

36

After compactifying
S^{1}_{B}
the component of a gauge field in this direction becomes an adjoint scalar field in the 2d theory and the derivative
D_{B}
becomes a gauge transformation generated by this field.
The remaining supersymmetry in the A and B-model can be labeled as

\mathsf{G}_{+}^{A}:=-\text{i}\mathsf{Q}_{+},\qquad\mathsf{G}_{-}^{A}:=\text{i}\overline{\mathsf{Q}}_{-},\qquad\mathsf{G}_{+}^{B}:=-\text{i}\mathsf{Q}_{+},\qquad\mathsf{G}_{-}^{B}:=\text{i}\mathsf{Q}_{-}.

For any vector field
V=V_{+}\partial_{w}+V_{-}\partial_{\overline{w}}
, we define the linear combination
\iota_{V}\mathsf{G}:=V_{+}\mathsf{G}_{+}+V_{-}\mathsf{G}_{-}
and we have the anti-commutation relations

\bigl\{\mathsf{Q}_{A},\iota_{V}\mathsf{G}^{A}\bigr\}=\bigl\{\mathsf{Q}_{B},\iota_{V}\mathsf{G}^{B}\bigr\}=V_{+}D_{w}+V_{-}D_{\overline{w}}={\mathcal{L}}_{V}+\text{gauge transformation}.

\Omega
-deforming the A and B-model supercharges with respect to a vector field
V
means changing the BRST operator from
\mathsf{Q}_{A}
and
\mathsf{Q}_{B}
to
\mathsf{Q}_{A}+\iota_{V}\mathsf{G}^{A}
and
\mathsf{Q}_{B}+\iota_{V}\mathsf{G}^{B}
such that the new BRST operator squares to the space-time transformation generated by
V

\bigl(\mathsf{Q}_{A}+\iota_{V}\mathsf{G}^{A}\bigr)^{2}=\bigl(\mathsf{Q}_{B}+\iota_{V}\mathsf{G}^{B}\bigr)^{2}={\mathcal{L}}_{V}+\text{gauge transformation}.

We observe that the localizing supercharge (
B.8
), upon reduction to 2d, descends to an
\Omega
-deformed supercharge both in the A and in the B-model

\mathscr{Q}\xrightarrow{\text{3d $\rightarrow$ 2d}}\mathsf{Q}_{+}+\mathsf{Q}_{-}+\overline{\mathsf{Q}}_{+}+\overline{\mathsf{Q}}_{-}=\mathsf{Q}_{A}+\text{i}\mathsf{G}^{A}_{+}-\text{i}\mathsf{G}^{A}_{-}=\mathsf{Q}_{B}+\text{i}\mathsf{G}^{B}_{+}-\text{i}\mathsf{G}^{B}_{-}.

These
\Omega
-deformations are defined with respect to the vector field

\text{i}\partial_{w}-\text{i}\partial_{\overline{w}}=\partial_{A}
,
which rotates the
S^{1}_{A}
circle. So, the 3d localizing supercharge can be thought of as a lift of the
S^{1}_{A}
-rotating
\Omega
-deformed supercharge from either the A or the B-model on
\Sigma
.

If we further compactify the
S^{1}_{A}
direction and reduce to a 1d theory on
I
, we get an
{{\mathcal{N}}=4}
supersymmetric gauged quantum mechanics. The localizing supercharge
\mathscr{Q}
descends to a nilpotent (up to gauge transformation) supercharge. Nilpotency in 1d follows from the fact that there is no
D_{y}
on the right-hand side of (
B.9
), which is the only remaining translation in 1d.

Appendix C

\bm{\mathfrak{sl}(1|1)}
rational
\bm{R}
-matrix from the A-model localization

The aim of this appendix is to compute the geometric rational
R
-matrix for
\mathfrak{sl}(1|1)
superspin chain from its natural theory from the perspective of the Bethe/Gauge correspondence, i.e., a 2d gauge theory corresponding to the
\mathfrak{sl}(1|1)
spin chains with fundamental representations, and using the A-model localization formula of Closset, Cremonesi and Park
[
28
]
. The computation here is inspired by insights and observations in
[
23
,
97
,
98
]
, and its
\mathfrak{sl}(2)
counterpart has been performed in
[
23
]
. Unlike the construction in the main body of this work, the computation in this appendix should be considered an interesting observation rather than being based on a systematic framework. Furthermore, in this section, we assume that the explicit form of cohomological stable envelopes is known. Of course, by knowing the cohomological stable envelopes, the computation of
R
-matrix is a simple matrix manipulation. However, viewing the
R
-matrix elements as the two-point functions of certain (normalized) A-model observables would provide an alternative route and may lead to a better understanding of the Bethe/Gauge correspondence.

C.1

Construction of geometric rational
\bm{R}
-matrix

In Section
7
, we have successfully constructed cohomological stable envelopes and the geometric
R
-matrix associated with
\mathfrak{sl}(1|1)
by starting from their 3d counterparts and performing a 3d
\rightarrow
2d
\rightarrow
1d reduction, corresponding to reducing the three-dimensional gauge theory, living on
I\times\mathbb{E}_{\tau}
, on the two cycles of the elliptic curve
\mathbb{E}_{\tau}
. This is expected since the interval
partition
function of the 3d theory reduces to the interval partition function of the corresponding 1d quantum mechanical system after discarding all instanton corrections coming from particles wrapping
S^{1}_{\text{A}}
and
S^{1}_{\text{B}}
. Before presenting the setup and the computation of the
R
-matrix, let us make a series of remarks.

Remark C.1

(2d Gauge theory)
.

The first remark is the theory we use for localization computation. This is the gauge theory that Bethe/Gauge correspondence naturally associates to an
\mathfrak{sl}(1|1)
spin chain in a fundamental representation. This gauge theory is constructed in Example
4.1
. For the
N
-magnon sector of a spin chain with
L
sites, the 2d gauge theory is a
\U(N)
gauge theory with
\mathcal{N}=(2,2)
supersymmetry coupled to a pair of
\SU(L)\times\U(1)_{\hbar}
fundamental/anti-fundamental chiral multiplets.

Remark C.2

(relation to the dimensional reduction of 3d Gauge theory of Section
4.2
)
.

A natural question is the relation between the gauge theory we use for the localization computation of this section and the 3d
\rightarrow
2d dimensional reduction of the 3d theory of Section
4.2
used for the computation of elliptic stable envelopes. The dimensional reduction of the 3d theory of Section
4.2
is again a 2d gauge theory with an
\mathcal{N}=(2,2)
supersymmetry. However, this is a
different
2d theory, although with the same gauge and matter content. To see the relation, it is expected
[
40
]
that we should start from a 4d theory
37
37

37

The reason we need to start in 4d is that such a theory is a natural arena for the quantum version of equivariant elliptic cohomology. The BRST cohomology of a 4d analog of the A-model supercharge is expected to be equivalent to quantum equivariant elliptic cohomology of the Higgs branch of the 4d theory.
with
\mathcal{N}=1
supersymmetry on
{I\times S^{1}_{\text{A}}\times S^{1}_{\text{B}}\times S^{1}_{\text{C}}}
,
38
38

38

Alternatively, we can think of the 4d theory on
S^{2}\times S^{1}_{\text{A}}\times S^{1}_{\text{B}}
. We can then think of
S^{2}
as
I\times S^{1}_{\text{C}}
to which two cigars are capped. Reduction of the theory on
S^{1}_{\text{A}}\times S^{1}_{\text{B}}
gives rise to a theory on
S^{2}
.
where
S^{1}_{\text{C}}
is an extra circle.
The dimensional reduction of the 4d theory on
S^{1}_{C}
would be the 3d theory of Section
4.2
.
39
39

39

The reason that this 3d theory is enough for the purpose of the computation of the
R
-matrix is that the latter is insensitive to the deformed non-perturbative corrections coming from the extra circle
S^{1}_{\text{C}}
or equivalently, the deformed ring structure of the quantum equivariant elliptic cohomology.
On the other hand, the dimensional reduction of the 4d theory on
S^{1}_{\text{A}}\times S^{1}_{\text{B}}
gives a 2d theory on
I\times S^{1}_{\text{C}}
, which is the gauge theory that the 2d gauge theory we use for the localization of this section. This is precisely the gauge theory that the Bethe/Gauge correspondence assigns to an
\mathfrak{sl}(1|1)
spin chain with fundamental representations. Furthermore, the 2d gauge theory on
I\times S^{1}_{\text{C}}
can be thought of as a theory living on the sphere from which two cigars have been capped off as shown in Figure
7
.

S^{1}_{\text{C}}
\Longrightarrow

Remark C.3

(identification of parameters)
.

As we have seen in Section
6.1
, the elliptic stable envelopes depend on the holonomies for the gauge
\bm{s}
, flavor
(\bm{x},\hbar)
and the topological symmetry
z
along the cycle of the elliptic curve. Furthermore, we constructed cohomological stable envelopes by reducing the elliptic ones to 1d upon which the holonomy
z
disappears and all other holonomies become complex twisted masses of the 2d theory on
I\times S^{1}_{\text{C}}
. We thus need to compare the quantities in the theory
\mathscr{T}^{\prime\prime\prime}_{\text{1d}}
(the theory used in deriving (
7.5
)) and
\mathscr{T}^{\prime}_{\text{2d}}
(the theory we use in this appendix) to be able to write the formulas in terms of parameters of the latter (see Figure
8
). First note that in the theory
\mathscr{T}^{\prime\prime\prime}_{1d}
,

\bm{s}:=\mathop{\text{\Large$\oint_{\text{\normalsize$\scriptstyle\text{A}$}}$}}\nolimits A^{\mathfrak{h}}_{\text{4d}}+\mathfrak{i}\mathop{\text{\Large$\oint_{\text{\normalsize$\scriptstyle\text{B}$}}$}}\nolimits A^{\mathfrak{h}}_{\text{4d}},

where
A^{\mathfrak{h}}_{\text{4d}}
is the four-dimensional gauge field for the Cartan of the gauge Lie algebra
\mathfrak{h}=\mathbb{R}^{\oplus N}
. Hence, they will be identified with
\bm{\sigma}^{\mathbb{C}}:=\text{diag}(\sigma^{\mathbb{C}}_{1},\ldots,\sigma^{\mathbb{C}}_{N})
, the complex scalar in the
\mathfrak{u}(N)
vector multiplet, in the theory
\mathscr{T}^{\prime}_{\text{2d}}
. Furthermore, in the theory
\mathscr{T}^{\prime\prime\prime}_{1d}
,

\displaystyle\bm{x}:=\mathop{\text{\Large$\oint_{\text{\normalsize$\scriptstyle\text{A}$}}$}}\nolimits A^{\mathfrak{a}}_{\text{4d}}+\mathfrak{i}\mathop{\text{\Large$\oint_{\text{\normalsize$\scriptstyle\text{B}$}}$}}\nolimits A^{\mathfrak{a}}_{\text{4d}},\qquad\hbar:=\mathop{\text{\Large$\oint_{\text{\normalsize$\scriptstyle\text{A}$}}$}}\nolimits A^{\hbar}_{\text{4d}}+\mathfrak{i}\mathop{\text{\Large$\oint_{\text{\normalsize$\scriptstyle\text{B}$}}$}}\nolimits A^{\hbar}_{\text{4d}},

where
A^{\mathfrak{a}}_{\text{4d}}
is the four-dimensional gauge field for the Cartan of the flavor symmetry
\mathfrak{a}=\mathfrak{u}(1)^{\oplus L-1}
,
40
40

40

Note that
x_{1}+\dots+x_{L}=0
.
and
A^{\hbar}_{\text{4d}}
is the four-dimensional gauge field for the
\mathbb{R}_{\hbar}
flavor symmetry. Therefore, they are identified with
\bm{m}^{\mathbb{C}}:=\bigl(m^{\mathbb{C}}_{1},\ldots,m^{\mathbb{C}}_{L}\bigr)
and
\hbar^{\mathbb{C}}
, the complex twisted masses, in the theory
\mathscr{T}^{\prime}_{\text{2d}}
. Hence, we identify the parameters as follows:

\displaystyle s_{a}\longleftrightarrow\sigma^{\mathbb{C}}_{a},\qquad a=1,\ldots,N,\qquad x_{i}\longleftrightarrow m_{i}^{\mathbb{C}},\qquad i=1,\ldots,L,\qquad\hbar\longleftrightarrow\hbar^{\mathbb{C}}.

(C.1)

Let us emphasize that say
m^{\mathbb{C}}_{i}
has nothing to do with the (expectation value of the) quantity defined in (
4.9
), which is the complex scalar in the vector multiplet of the theory
\mathscr{T}^{\prime\prime\prime}_{2d}
(see the right portion of Figure
8
), while
\bigl(\sigma^{\mathbb{C}}_{a},m^{\mathbb{C}}_{i},\hbar^{\mathbb{C}}\bigr)
in (
C.1
) are parameters of the theory
\mathscr{T}^{\prime}_{\text{2d}}
(or equivalently
\mathscr{T}^{\prime\prime}_{\text{2d}}
). On the other hand, there are real masses in the theory
\mathscr{T}^{\prime\prime\prime}_{1d}
whose order determines a chamber
\mathfrak{C}
in the theory
\mathscr{T}^{\prime\prime\prime}_{1d}
,

m_{i}=\mathop{\text{\Large$\oint_{\text{\normalsize$\scriptstyle\text{C}$}}$}}\nolimits A^{\mathfrak{a}}_{\text{4d}},\qquad i=1,\ldots,L.

(C.2)

From the perspective of
\mathscr{T}^{\prime}_{2d}
, these are holonomies of the 2d gauge field
41
41

\displaystyle s_{a}\rightarrow s_{a},\qquad a=1,\ldots,N,\qquad x_{i}\rightarrow x_{i}+\frac{\hbar}{2},\qquad i=1,\ldots,L.
